\mathbb{J}(\kappa^{\iota^{-1}\circ\gamma^{-1}\circ\nu_{w}},\kappa^{\iota^{-1}\circ\gamma^{-1}\circ\bar{\nu}_{w}})\ =\ \begin{cases}\mathbb{J}(\kappa^{\iota^{-1}\circ\nu_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot w}},\kappa^{\iota^{-1}\circ\bar{\nu}_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot w}})&\mbox{if $\gamma^{-1}\circ\nu_{w}=\nu_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot w}$,}\\
\\
\overline{\mathbb{J}(\kappa^{\iota^{-1}\circ\nu_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot w}},\kappa^{\iota^{-1}\circ\bar{\nu}_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot w}})}&\mbox{if $\gamma^{-1}\circ\nu_{w}=\overline{\nu_{\gamma^{-1}\cdot w}}.$}\end{cases}

Hence the archimedean components of
{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\pi
is a permutation of the archimedean components of
{}^{\iota}\pi
up to taking conjugates.

2.5.3.3
When
F
is totally imaginary in the TR-case.

The Galois action on
\Sigma_{F}
and
\Sigma_{F_{1}}
preserves the fibers of the restriction map
\Sigma_{F}\to\Sigma_{F_{1}}
, and since
F_{1}
is totally real,
identify the Galois-sets
\Sigma_{F_{1}}=\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F_{1}).
Using the notations of (
2.18
), if

{}^{\iota}\pi_{\infty}\ =\ \bigotimes_{w\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F_{1})}\bigotimes_{v|w}\ \mathbb{J}(\kappa^{\iota^{-1}\circ\nu_{w}},\kappa^{\iota^{-1}\circ\nu_{w}}),

then for
\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
one has:

({}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\pi)_{\infty}\ =\ \bigotimes_{w\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F_{1})}\bigotimes_{v|w}\ \mathbb{J}(\kappa^{\iota^{-1}\circ\nu_{\gamma^{-1}\circ w}},\kappa^{\iota^{-1}\circ\nu_{\gamma^{-1}\circ w}}).

Hence the archimedean components of
{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\pi
is a permutation of the archimedean components of
{}^{\iota}\pi
.

2.6.
Boundary Cohomology

The cohomology
H^{\bullet}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})

of the boundary of the Borel–Serre compactification of the locally symmetric space
\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}}
is briefly discussed here, and the reader is referred to

[
26
, Chap. 4]
for more details and proofs.
There is a spectral sequence built from the cohomology of the boundary strata
\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}}
that converges to the cohomology of the boundary.
To understand the cohomology of a single stratum
\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},
note that

H^{\bullet}(\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\ =\ H^{\bullet}(P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})/K_{\infty}K_{f},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E}).

The space
P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})/K_{\infty}K_{f}
fibers over locally symmetric spaces of
M_{P}.

Let
\Xi_{K_{f}}
be a complete set of representatives for
P(\mathbb{A}_{f})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K_{f}.

Let
K^{P}_{\infty}=K_{\infty}\cap P(\mathbb{R}),
and for
\xi_{f}\in\Xi_{K_{f}}
, let
K_{f}^{P}(\xi_{f})=P(\mathbb{A}_{f})\cap\xi_{f}K_{f}\xi_{f}^{-1}.

Then

P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})/K_{\infty}^{0}K_{f}\ =\ \coprod_{\xi_{f}\,\in\,\Xi_{K_{f}}}P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash P(\mathbb{A})/K^{P}_{\infty}K_{f}^{P}(\xi_{f}).

Let
\kappa_{P}:P\to P/U_{P}=M_{P}
be the canonical map, and define

K_{\infty}^{M_{P}}=\kappa_{P}(K^{P}_{\infty})
, and for
\xi_{f}\in\Xi_{K_{f}}
, let

K_{f}^{M_{P}}(\xi_{f})=\kappa_{P}(K_{f}^{P}(\xi_{f})).

Define

\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{M_{P}}_{K_{f}^{M_{P}}(\xi_{f})}\ :=\ M_{P}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash M_{P}(\mathbb{A})/K_{\infty}^{M_{P}}K_{f}^{M_{P}}(\xi_{f}).

The underline is to emphasize that we have divided by
K_{\infty}^{M_{P}}
that may be explicated as follows: for the maximal
parabolic
P=P_{(n,n^{\prime})},
whose Levi quotient
M_{P}
may be identified with
the block diagonal subgroup
G_{n}\times G_{n^{\prime}}
where
G_{n}=R_{F/\mathbb{Q}}({\rm GL}_{n})
and
G_{n^{\prime}}=R_{F/\mathbb{Q}}({\rm GL}_{n^{\prime}}),
one has

K_{\infty}^{M_{P}}=\kappa_{P}(P(\mathbb{R})\cap K_{\infty})=M_{P}(\mathbb{R})\cap K_{\infty}=\\
\prod_{v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})&\\
&{\rm GL}_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C})\end{bmatrix}\cap{\rm U}(N)\right)S(\mathbb{R})\ =\ \prod_{v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}{\rm U}(n)&\\
&{\rm U}(n^{\prime})\end{bmatrix}\right)S(\mathbb{R}).

Note that
K_{\infty}^{M_{P}}
is connected.
Let
K_{f}^{U_{P}}(\xi_{f})=U_{P}(\mathbb{A}_{f})\cap\xi_{f}K_{f}\xi_{f}^{-1}.
We have the fibration:

U_{P}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash U_{P}(\mathbb{A})/K_{f}^{U_{P}}(\xi_{f})\ \hookrightarrow\ P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash P(\mathbb{A})/K^{P}_{\infty}K_{f}^{P}(\xi_{f})\ \twoheadrightarrow\ \underline{\mathcal{S}}^{M_{P}}_{K_{f}^{M_{P}}(\xi_{f})}.

The corresponding Leray–Serre spectral sequence is known to degenerate at the
E_{2}
-level. The cohomology of the total space is given in terms of the cohomology of the base with coefficients in the cohomology of the fiber. For the cohomology of the fiber, if
\mathfrak{u}_{P}
is the Lie algebra of
U_{P}
then
the cohomology of the fiber is the same as the Lie algebra cohomology group

H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,E})
–by a classical theorem due to van Est, which is naturally an algebraic representation of
M_{P}
; the associated sheaf on
\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{M_{P}}_{K_{f}^{M_{P}}(\xi_{f})}
is denoted by putting a tilde on top. One has:

(2.22)

H^{\bullet}(\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\ =\ \bigoplus_{\xi_{f}\,\in\,\Xi_{K_{f}}}H^{\bullet}\left(\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{M_{P}}_{K_{f}^{M_{P}}(\xi_{f})},\widetilde{H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,E})}\right).

Pass to the limit over all open compact subgroups
K_{f}
and define:

H^{\bullet}(\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E}):=\varinjlim_{K_{f}}\,H^{\bullet}(\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E}).

Let
\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{M_{P}}\ :=\ M_{P}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash M_{P}(\mathbb{A})/K_{\infty}^{M_{P}};
(
2.22
) can be rewritten as:

H^{\bullet}(\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})^{K_{f}}\ =\ \bigoplus_{\xi_{f}\,\in\,\Xi_{K_{f}}}H^{\bullet}\left(\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{M_{P}},\widetilde{H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,E})}\right)^{K_{f}^{M_{P}}(\xi_{f})}.

It is clear using Mackey theory that the right hand side is the
K_{f}
-invariants of an algebraically induced representation; hence one has the following

Proposition 2.23
.

The cohomology of
\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G}
is given by:

H^{\bullet}(\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\ =\ {}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}\left(H^{\bullet}(\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{M_{P}},\widetilde{H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,E})})\right).

The notation
{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}
stands for algebraic, or un-normalized, induction.

The following is a brief review of well-known results of Kostant
[
34
]
on the structure of
H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,E}).

The calculation of the unipotent cohomology group is over the field
E
. Recall that
G\times E=\prod_{\tau:F\to E}G_{0}^{\tau}
where
G_{0}^{\tau}=G_{0}\times_{F,\tau}E={\rm GL}_{N}/E.

Let
\mathbf{\Delta}_{G_{0}}
stand for the set of roots of
G_{0}
with respect to
T_{N,0}
,

\mathbf{\Delta}_{G_{0}}^{+}
the subset of positive roots (for choice of Borel subgroup being the upper triangular subgroup), and
\mathbf{\Pi}_{G_{0}}
the set of simple roots.
The notations
\mathbf{\Delta}_{G_{0}^{\tau}}
,
\mathbf{\Delta}_{G_{0}^{\tau}}^{+}
and
\mathbf{\Pi}_{G_{0}^{\tau}}
are clear.
Let
P=\mathrm{Res}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(P_{0})
be the parabolic subgroup of
G
as above, and let
P_{0}^{\tau}:=P_{0}\times_{\tau}E.

The Weyl group factors as
W=\prod_{\tau:F\to E}W_{0}^{\tau}
with each
W_{0}^{\tau}
isomorphic to the permutation group
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
on
N
-letters.
Let
W^{P}
be the set of Kostant representatives in the Weyl group
W
of
G
corresponding to the parabolic subgroup
P
defined as:
W^{P}=\{w=(w^{\tau}):w^{\tau}\in W^{\tau}_{0}{}^{P^{\tau}_{0}}\},
where,

W^{\tau}_{0}{}^{P^{\tau}_{0}}\ :=\{w^{\tau}\in W^{\tau}_{0}:\ (w^{\tau})^{-1}\alpha>0,\ \forall\alpha\in\mathbf{\Pi}_{M_{P_{0}^{\tau}}}\}.

Here
\mathbf{\Pi}_{M_{P_{0}^{\tau}}}\subset\mathbf{\Pi}_{G_{0}^{\tau}}
denotes the set of simple roots in the Levi quotient
M_{P_{0}^{\tau}}
of
P_{0}^{\tau}.

The twisted action of
w\in W
on
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
is

w\cdot\lambda=(w^{\tau}\cdot\lambda^{\tau})_{\tau:F\to E}
and

w^{\tau}\cdot\lambda^{\tau}=w^{\tau}(\lambda^{\tau}+\bm{\uprho}_{N})-\bm{\uprho}_{N},
where

\bm{\uprho}_{N}=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbf{\Delta}_{G_{0}}^{+}}\alpha.

For
w\in W^{P}
, the irreducible finite-dimensional representation of
M_{P}\times E
with extremal weight
w\cdot\lambda
is denoted
\mathcal{M}_{w\cdot\lambda,E}
.
Kostant’s theorem asserts that one has a multiplicity-free decomposition of
M_{P}\times E
-modules:

(2.24)

H^{q}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,E})\ \simeq\ \bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in W^{P}\\
l(w)=q\end{subarray}}\mathcal{M}_{w\cdot\lambda,E}.

As explained in
[
26
]
, the above result of Kostant can be parsed over the set of embeddings
\tau:F\to E.

Denote by
H^{l(w)}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,E})(w)
the summand of
H^{q}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,E})
corresponding to the Kostant representative
w
which is nonzero
for
q=l(w)
and isomorphic to
\mathcal{M}_{w\cdot\lambda,E}
.
Applying (
2.24
) to the boundary cohomology as in Prop.
2.23
gives the following

Proposition 2.25
.

The cohomology of
\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G}
is given by:

H^{q}(\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\ =\ \bigoplus_{w\in W^{P}}{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}\left(H^{q-l(w)}(\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{M_{P}},\widetilde{H^{q}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,E})(w)})\right).

There is a canonical surjection
\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{M_{P}}\to\mathcal{S}^{M_{P}}
, using which we may inflate up the cohomology of
\mathcal{S}^{M_{P}}
to the cohomology of
\underline{\mathcal{S}}^{M_{P}}
;
this will be especially relevant to strongly inner cohomology classes of
\mathcal{S}^{M_{P}}
, which after inducing up to
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
will contribute to boundary cohomology; see Sect.
5.1.2
.

2.7.
Galois action and local systems in boundary cohomology

For an embedding
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
, the map
\gamma_{*}
induced by a Galois element
\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
in unipotent cohomology

H^{q}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{{}^{\iota}\!\lambda,\mathbb{C}})({}^{\iota}w)\ \to\ H^{q}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\!\lambda,\mathbb{C}})({}^{\gamma\circ\iota}w),

where,
q=l(w)=l({}^{\iota}w)=l({}^{\gamma\circ\iota}w)
, will play a role in the proof of the reciprocity law of the main theorem. By Schur’s lemma, this can be understood by its effect
on the highest weight vector for the irreducible representation

H^{q}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{{}^{\iota}\!\lambda,\mathbb{C}})({}^{\iota}w)\simeq\mathcal{M}_{{}^{\iota}w\cdot{}^{\iota}\!\lambda,\mathbb{C}}.

Such a highest weight vector
{\sf h}(\lambda,w,\iota)

will be fixed by fixing a harmonic representative the corresponding cohomology class as in Kostant
[
34
, Thm. 5.14]
. To explicate this vector note that:

\mathfrak{u}_{P}\otimes E\ =\ \bigoplus_{\tau:F\to E}\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}}^{\tau};\quad\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}}^{\tau}:=\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}}\otimes_{F,\tau}E.

Fix an ordering:

{\rm Hom}(F,E)=\{\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\dots,\tau_{d}\}.

Let
\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}})
denote the subset of
\mathbf{\Delta}^{+}
of those positive roots
\varphi
whose root space
X_{\varphi}
is in
\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}}
. Fix an ordering

\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}})=\{\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\dots,\varphi_{nn^{\prime}}\}.

For example, thinking in terms of upper triangular matrices,
this ordering could be taken as the lexicographic ordering on the set of pairs of indices
\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}\}
. Fix a generator
e_{\varphi}
for
X_{\varphi}
for each

\varphi\in\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}});
note that
e_{\varphi}
is well-defined up to
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}.
Let
\{e_{\varphi}^{*}\}
denote the basis of
\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}}^{*}
that is dual
to
\{e_{\varphi}\}.

For a Kostant representative
w_{0}\in W^{P_{0}}\subset W_{G_{0}}
,
define
\Phi_{w_{0}}=\{\varphi>0:w_{0}^{-1}\varphi<0\};
then
\Phi_{w_{0}}\subset\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}})
; with respect to the ordering that it inherits from
\mathbf{\Delta}(\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}})
, denote

\Phi_{w_{0}}=\{\varphi^{w_{0}}_{1},\dots,\varphi^{w_{0}}_{l}\}
as an ordered set, where
l=l(w_{0}^{-1})=l(w_{0}).
Define

e_{\Phi_{w_{0}}}^{*}\ :=\ e_{\varphi^{w_{0}}_{1}}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{\varphi^{w_{0}}_{l}}^{*}\ \in\ \wedge^{q_{0}}(\mathfrak{u}_{P_{0}}^{*});\quad q_{0}:=l(w_{0}).

Let
e_{\varphi}^{\tau}
denote the image
e_{\varphi}\otimes 1
of
e_{\varphi}
under the canonical map
X_{\varphi}\to X_{\varphi}^{\tau}=X_{\varphi}\otimes_{F,\tau}E
. For

w=(w^{\tau})_{\tau:F\to E}\in W_{G}=\prod_{\tau:F\to E}W_{G_{0}}\times_{F,\tau}E
, written using the ordering on
{\rm Hom}(F,E)
as
w=\{w^{\tau_{1}},\dots,w^{\tau_{d}}\}
, define:

e_{\Phi_{w}}^{*}\ :=\ e_{\Phi_{w^{\tau_{1}}}}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{\Phi_{w^{\tau_{d}}}}^{*}\ \in\ \wedge^{q}(\mathfrak{u}_{P}^{*}\otimes E);\quad q:=l(w).

Changing the base to
\mathbb{C}
via
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
gives

(2.26)

e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w}}^{*}\ :=\ e_{\Phi_{w^{\iota\circ\tau_{1}}}}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{\Phi_{w^{\iota\circ\tau_{d}}}}^{*}\ \in\ \wedge^{q}(\mathfrak{u}_{P}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}).

Fix a weight vector
{\sf s}(\lambda^{\tau})\in\mathcal{M}_{\lambda^{\tau},E}
for the highest weight
\lambda^{\tau};
then

{\sf s}(\lambda)={\sf s}(\lambda^{\tau}_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes{\sf s}(\lambda^{\tau}_{d})
is the highest weight vector for
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda,E}
.
For each
w\in W,
fix its representative in
G(E)
, which amounts to fixing a permutation matrix representing
w^{\tau}
in
{\rm GL}_{n}(E)
for
each embedding
\tau:F\to E
. Let

(2.27)

{\sf s}(w\lambda)\ :=\ \rho_{\lambda^{\tau_{1}}}(w^{\tau_{1}}){\sf s}(\lambda^{\tau}_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\rho_{\lambda^{\tau_{d}}}(w^{\tau_{d}}){\sf s}(\lambda^{\tau}_{d})

be the weight vector of extremal weight
w\lambda.
These vectors can be composed via
\iota
:

{\sf s}({}^{\iota}\!w\,{}^{\iota}\!\lambda)
is the weight vector in
\mathcal{M}_{{}^{\iota}\!\lambda,\mathbb{C}}
of extremal weight
{}^{\iota}\!w\,{}^{\iota}\!\lambda.
Theorem 5.14 of
[
34
]
asserts that

(2.28)

{\sf h}(\lambda,w,\iota)\ =\ e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w}}^{*}\otimes{\sf s}({}^{\iota}\!w\,{}^{\iota}\!\lambda)

is the highest weight vector for
H^{q}(\mathfrak{u}_{P},\mathcal{M}_{{}^{\iota}\!\lambda,\mathbb{C}})({}^{\iota}w).
The image of
{\sf h}(\lambda,w,\iota)
under the
map
\gamma_{*}
induced by
\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
is a multiple of
{\sf h}(\lambda,w,\gamma\circ\iota)
; the scaling factor is captured by what

\gamma
does to the wedge-products
e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w}}^{*}
, motivating the following

Definition 2.29
.

Let
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
and
\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
then we have

e_{\Phi_{{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\!w}}^{*}\ =\ \varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w}}^{*}

for a signature
\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\in\{\pm 1\}
.

From (
2.27
), (
2.28
), and the above definition one has:

(2.30)

\gamma_{*}({\sf h}(\lambda,w,\iota))\ =\ \varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\cdot{\sf h}(\lambda,w,\gamma\circ\iota).

3.
The critical set and a combinatorial lemma

3.1.
The critical set for
L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})

Let
n
and
n^{\prime}
be two positive integers, and consider weights
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times\mathbb{C})
and

\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{C})
given by:

(3.1)

\mu=(\mu^{\eta})_{\eta:F\to\mathbb{C}},\quad\mu^{\eta}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(a^{\eta}_{i}-1)\bm{\upgamma}_{i}+d^{\eta}\cdot\bm{\updelta}\ =\ (b^{\eta}_{1},\dots,b^{\eta}_{n}),

and similarly,

(3.2)

\mu^{\prime}=(\mu^{\prime\eta})_{\eta:F\to\mathbb{C}},\quad\mu^{\prime\eta}=\sum_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}-1}(a^{\prime\eta}_{i}-1)\bm{\upgamma}_{j}+d^{\prime\eta}\cdot\bm{\updelta}\ =\ (b^{\prime\eta}_{1},\dots,b^{\prime\eta}_{n^{\prime}}).

Let
\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu)
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime})
be strongly inner Hecke-summands; these Hecke-summands, take a unique representation at infinity to contribute to the respective cuspidal spectrum cohomology. Denote
\sigma_{\infty}=\mathbb{J}_{\mu}
and

\sigma^{\prime}_{\infty}=\mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}}
then
\sigma=\sigma_{\infty}\otimes\sigma_{f}
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}_{\infty}\otimes\sigma^{\prime}_{f}
are cuspidal automorphic representations. We let
L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})
stand for the completed standard Rankin–Selberg
L
-function of
degree
nn^{\prime}.
We refer the reader to
[
44
, Sect. 10.1]
for a summary of the basic analytic properties of these
L
-functions.
The purpose of this section is to identify the set of integers or possibly half-integers
m
which are critical for

L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}).
(Note that we have dualized
\sigma^{\prime}.
)

3.1.1.
Definition of the critical set

For any two half-integers
\alpha
and
\beta,
the local
L
-factor (see
[
33
]
) of the character
z\mapsto z^{\alpha}\bar{z}^{\beta}
of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is given by:

(3.3)

L(s,z^{\alpha}\bar{z}^{\beta})\ =\ 2(2\pi)^{-\left(s+\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}+\frac{|\alpha-\beta|}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(s+\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}+\frac{|\alpha-\beta|}{2}\right)\\
\sim\ \Gamma\left(s+\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}+\frac{|\alpha-\beta|}{2}\right),

where, by
\sim
, we mean up to nonzero constants and exponential functions, which are entire and nonvanishing everywhere and hence are irrelevant to the computation of critical points; see Def.
3.4
below. For any

v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}
, let
\{\eta_{v},\bar{\eta}_{v}\}
be the pair of conjugate embeddings of
F
to
\mathbb{C}
as before. Let

\alpha^{v}\ =\ -w_{0}\mu^{\eta_{v}}+\bm{\uprho}_{n}\ =\ (\alpha^{v}_{1},\dots,\alpha^{v}_{n})\ \ \ {\rm and}\ \ \ \beta^{v}\ =\ -\mu^{\bar{\eta}_{v}}-\bm{\uprho}_{n}\ =\ (\beta^{v}_{1},\dots,\beta^{v}_{n})

be the cuspidal parameters of
\mu
at
v
; see
(
2.8
) and (
2.9
). Similarly, let

\alpha^{\prime v}\ =\ -w_{0}\mu^{\prime\eta_{v}}+\bm{\uprho}_{n^{\prime}}\ =\ (\alpha^{\prime v}_{1},\dots,\alpha^{\prime v}_{n^{\prime}})\ \ \ {\rm and}\ \ \ \beta^{\prime v}\ =\ -\mu^{\prime\bar{\eta}_{v}}-\bm{\uprho}_{n^{\prime}}\ =\ (\beta^{\prime v}_{1},\dots,\beta^{\prime v}_{n^{\prime}})

be the cuspidal parameters of
\mu^{\prime}
at
v.

Note that

\alpha:=\alpha_{i}^{v}+\alpha^{\prime v}_{j}\ \in\ \frac{n-1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}+\frac{n^{\prime}-1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}\ =\ \frac{N}{2}+\mathbb{Z},\quad{\rm and}\quad\beta:=\beta_{i}^{v}+\beta^{\prime v}_{j}\ \in\ \frac{N}{2}+\mathbb{Z}.

Then, it is clear that the quantity
\tfrac{\alpha+\beta}{2}+\tfrac{|\alpha-\beta|}{2}

inside the argument of the
\Gamma
-function above is in

\tfrac{N}{2}+\mathbb{Z}.

This tells us that the critical set for
L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})
will be a subset of
\tfrac{N}{2}+\mathbb{Z}.

Let
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
be cuspidal automorphic representations of
G_{n}(\mathbb{A})
and
G_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A})
, respectively. The set of critical points for

L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})
is defined to be:

(3.4)

{\rm Crit}(L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}))\ :=\ \\
\left\{m\in\tfrac{N}{2}+\mathbb{Z}\ :\mbox{both $L_{\infty}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})$ and $L_{\infty}(1-s,\sigma^{\sf v}\times\sigma^{\prime})$ are finite at $s=m$}\right\}.

If
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are cohomological with respect to
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
, then we denote

(3.5)

{\rm Crit}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ :=\ {\rm Crit}(L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})).

3.1.2.
Computing the critical set

Recall, the purity conditions:

\alpha^{v}_{i}+\beta^{v}_{i}=-{\bf w},\ \ {\rm and}\ \ \alpha^{\prime v}_{i}+\beta^{\prime v}_{i}=-{\bf w}^{\prime}.

We define a quantity
a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
, and call it the
abelian width
between
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
, as:

(3.6)

a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ :=\ \frac{{\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}}{2}\ =\ \frac{(d^{\eta}+d^{\bar{\eta}})-(d^{\prime\eta}+d^{\prime\bar{\eta}})}{2}.

From the local Langlands correspondence and (
3.3
) on abelian local
L
-factors, we get

(3.7)

L_{\infty}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})\ \sim\ \prod_{v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\ \Gamma\left(s-a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})+\frac{|\alpha^{v}_{i}-\alpha^{\prime v}_{j}-\beta^{v}_{i}+\beta^{\prime v}_{j}|}{2}\right).

And, similarly,

(3.8)

L_{\infty}(1-s,\sigma^{\sf v}\times\sigma^{\prime})\ \sim\ \prod_{v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\ \Gamma\left(1-s+a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})+\frac{|\alpha^{v}_{i}-\alpha^{\prime v}_{j}-\beta^{v}_{i}+\beta^{\prime v}_{j}|}{2}\right).

Let
m\in\tfrac{N}{2}+\mathbb{Z}.
Then
m\in{\rm Crit}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
if and only if

(3.9)

m-a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})+\frac{|\alpha^{v}_{i}-\alpha^{\prime v}_{j}-\beta^{v}_{i}+\beta^{\prime v}_{j}|}{2}\ \geq\ 1,\quad\forall v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty},\forall i,\forall j,

which is the condition that
L_{\infty}(m,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})
is finite from (
3.7
), and

(3.10)

1-m+a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})+\frac{|\alpha^{v}_{i}-\alpha^{\prime v}_{j}-\beta^{v}_{i}+\beta^{\prime v}_{j}|}{2}\ \geq\ 1,\quad\forall v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty},\forall i,\forall j,

which is the condition that
L_{\infty}(1-m,\sigma^{\sf v}\times\sigma^{\prime})
is finite from (
3.8
).
Define the
cuspidal width

\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
between
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
as:

(3.11)

\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ :=\ {\rm min}\left\{|\alpha^{v}_{i}-\alpha^{\prime v}_{j}-\beta^{v}_{i}+\beta^{\prime v}_{j}|\ :\ v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty},1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}\right\}.

Then (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) together gives us the following

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times\mathbb{C})
and
\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{C}).
For

\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu)
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime})
, the critical set for the Rankin–Selberg
L
-function

L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})
is given by:

{\rm Crit}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ =\ \left\{m\in\tfrac{N}{2}+\mathbb{Z}\ :\ 1-\frac{\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}{2}+a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ \leq\ m\ \leq\ \frac{\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}{2}+a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\right\}.

This is contiguous string of integers or half-integers (depending on whether
N
is even or odd),
centered around
\tfrac{1}{2}+a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
, of length
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime}).

Corollary 3.13
.

With notations as in Prop.
3.12
, the points
s=-N/2
and
s=1-N/2
are both critical for
L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})
if and only if

-\frac{N}{2}+1-\frac{\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}{2}\ \leq\ a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ \leq\ -\frac{N}{2}-1+\frac{\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}{2}.

Of course, for this to be possible one needs
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\geq 2
, i.e., that there at least two critical points.
The corollary, which is one part of a
combinatorial lemma
below, is to be viewed like this: the two successive
L
-values at
s=-N/2
and
s=1-N/2
are critical if and only if the abelian width is bounded in absolute value in terms of the cuspidal width.

Corollary 3.14
.

Suppose
F
is in the
TR
-case and
F_{1}=F_{0}
is the maximal totally real subfield of
F
. Given
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times\mathbb{C})
and
\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{C}),

if
n
and
n^{\prime}
are both odd, then
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=0
; in particular, the Rankin–Selberg
L
-function

L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})
has no critical points.

Proof.

Recall from Prop.
2.6
that
\mu
is the base change of a strongly-pure weight over
F_{1}
. For
v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F)
, one has

\eta_{v}|_{F_{1}}=\bar{\eta}_{v}|_{F_{1}}
, hence

\mu^{\eta_{v}}=\mu^{\bar{\eta}_{v}}
. Hence, for the cuspidal parameters, one has
\alpha^{v}=w_{0}\beta^{v}
, i.e.,
\alpha^{v}_{i}=\beta^{v}_{n+1-i}.
If
n
is odd, then

\alpha^{v}_{(n+1)/2}=\beta^{v}_{(n+1)/2}.
Similarly, if
n^{\prime}
is odd, then
\alpha^{\prime v}_{(n^{\prime}+1)/2}=\beta^{\prime v}_{(n^{\prime}+1)/2}.
From (
3.11
) it follows
that
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=0
, as
0
is realized as the minimum by taking
i=(n+1)/2
and
j=(n^{\prime}+1)/2.

∎

3.1.3.
Critical set at an arithmetic level

Let
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times E)
and
\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times E),
and take

\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu)
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime}).
For any
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
,
Prop.
3.12
gives
the critical set
{\rm Crit}({}^{\iota}\mu,{}^{\iota}\mu^{\prime})
for the Rankin–Selberg
L
-function

L(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}).

Corollary 3.15
.

The critical set
{\rm Crit}({}^{\iota}\mu,{}^{\iota}\mu^{\prime})={\rm Crit}(L(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}))
is independent of
\iota:

{\rm Crit}(L(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}))\ =\ {\rm Crit}(L(s,{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})),\quad\forall\ \iota:E\to\mathbb{C},\ \ \forall\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}).

Proof.

From Rem.
2.12
, one can deduce

\ell({}^{\iota}\mu,{}^{\iota}\mu^{\prime})=\ell({}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\mu,{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\mu^{\prime})
and
a({}^{\iota}\mu,{}^{\iota}\mu^{\prime})=a({}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\mu,{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\mu^{\prime}).

One can also see this directly, since by the results of
2.5.3
, the archimedean components of
{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma
are a permutation
of those of
{}^{\iota}\sigma
up to conjugates; similarly, for
{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}
; since
L(s,z^{\alpha}\bar{z}^{\beta})=L(s,z^{\beta}\bar{z}^{\alpha})
one gets
L_{\infty}(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})=L_{\infty}(s,{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}).

∎

3.2.
Combinatorial Lemma

3.2.1.
Statement of the lemma

Lemma 3.16
.

For strongly-pure weights
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times\mathbb{C})
and
\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{C})
, and cuspidal Hecke summands

\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu),

\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime}),
the following are equivalent:

(1)

The points
s=-N/2
and
s=1-N/2
are both critical for
L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})
.

(2)

The abelian width is bounded in terms of the cuspidal width as:

-\frac{N}{2}+1-\frac{\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}{2}\ \leq\ a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ \leq\ -\frac{N}{2}-1+\frac{\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}{2}.

(3)

There exists
w\in W^{P}
such that
w^{-1}\cdot(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
is dominant and
l(w^{\eta})+l(w^{\bar{\eta}})=\dim(U_{P_{0}})
for all
\eta:F\to\mathbb{C}.

(Recall:
w=(w^{\eta})_{\eta:F\to\mathbb{C}}
with
w^{\eta}\in W^{P_{0}\times_{\eta}\mathbb{C}}\subset W_{G_{0}}\times_{\eta}\mathbb{C}
.)

We have already proved
(1)\iff(2)
. It remains to prove
(2)\iff(3).

It is clear that

l(w^{\eta})+l(w^{\bar{\eta}})=\dim(U_{P_{0}}),\ \forall\eta:F\to\mathbb{C}\ \implies\ l(w)=\tfrac{1}{2}\dim(U_{P}).

However, if the degree of
F
is greater than
2
(i.e., if
{\sf r}>1
) then the converse is not true in general.

Definition 3.17
.

A Kostant representative
w\in W^{P}
is said to be
balanced
if

l(w^{\eta})+l(w^{\bar{\eta}})=\dim(U_{P_{0}}),\ \forall\eta:F\to\mathbb{C}.

For the benefit of the reader, we will make two passes over the proof of (2)
\iff
(3) in simpler situations, because the proof in the general case is intricate in details and somewhat tedious; it is the
sort of proof that makes one believe the dictum “
der Teufel steckt im Detail.
”

3.2.2.
Explicating (2)
\iff
(3) in the simplest nontrivial example

Proof.

Let us consider the case of
n=n^{\prime}=1
and so
N=2.
Take
F
to be an imaginary quadratic field with

{\rm Hom}(F,\mathbb{C})=\{\eta,\bar{\eta}\}.

The weights
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are both a pair of integers indexed by
{\rm Hom}(F,\mathbb{C});
we will write

\mu\ =\ ((a),(a^{*})),\quad\mu^{\prime}\ =\ ((b),(b^{*})),

with
a,a^{*},b,b^{*}\in\mathbb{Z},
with the convention that
\mu^{\eta}=(a),\mu^{\bar{\eta}}=(a^{*})
and similarly for
\mu^{\prime}.
Note that purity of
\mu
and

\mu^{\prime}
is automatic, and the purity weights are

{\bf w}=a+a^{*},\ {\bf w}^{\prime}=b+b^{*}.

The abelian width is

a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=\frac{a+a^{*}-b-b^{*}}{2}.

The cuspidal parameters at the only complex place
v
of
F
are:

\alpha^{v}=(-a),\ \beta^{v}=(-a^{*}),\ \alpha^{\prime v}=(-b),\ \beta^{\prime v}=(-b^{*}).

The cuspidal width is

\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=|-a+a^{*}+b-b^{*}|.

The weight
\mu+\mu^{\prime}
which we would like make dominant using a balanced Kostant representative has the shape

\mu+\mu^{\prime}\ =\ ((a,b),\,(a^{*},b^{*})).

For simplicity, let us denote:

p:=a-b,\ p^{*}:=a^{*}-b^{*}.

Hence,
\mu+\mu^{\prime}
is dominant if and only if
p\geq 0
and
p^{*}\geq 0.

The inequalities in (2) now take the shape:

(3.18)

-\frac{|p^{*}-p|}{2}\ \leq\ \frac{p+p^{*}}{2}\ \leq\ \frac{|p^{*}-p|}{2}-2.

Since,
P_{0}=B_{0}
is the Borel subgroup, the Levi subgroup
M_{P}
is a torus; hence,
W_{M_{P}}
is trivial and

W^{P}=W_{G}.
If
W_{G_{0}}
is written as
\{1,s\}
with
s
the nontrivial element, then
the elements of
W^{P}
may be written as:

W_{G}=(\{1,s\},\{1^{*},s^{*}\}).

The dimension of
U_{P}
is
2
, hence the balanced elements (of length
1
) of
W^{P}
are
(1,s^{*})
and
(s,1)
.
Now, consider three cases depending on the sign of
p-p^{*}
:

•

p=p^{*}.
In this case, (
3.18
) reads
0\leq p\leq-2,
which is absurd; hence (2) is violated. If
p\geq 0
then the only
w\in W^{P}
such that
w^{-1}\cdot(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
is dominant is
w=(1,1^{*})
which has length
0
, hence (3) is violated. Similarly, if
p<0
, then the only
w\in W^{P}
such that
w^{-1}\cdot(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
is dominant is
w=(s,s^{*}),
which has length
2
, hence (3) is violated again. So, both (2) and (3) are false.

•

p>p^{*}.
In this case, (
3.18
) simplifies to
p^{*}-p\leq p+p^{*}\leq p-p^{*}-4,
which implies that

p\geq 0>-2\geq p^{*}.
The only
w\in W^{P}
such that
w^{-1}\cdot(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
is dominant is
w=(1,s^{*})
which has length
1
, hence (3) is satisfied.

•

p<p^{*}.
In this case,
p^{*}\geq 0>-2\geq p
and the only
w\in W^{P}
that works is
(s,1)
which is of length
1
.

In all cases, either both (2) and (3) are satisfied, or both are violated. Hence,
(2)\iff(3).

∎

In the second case (
p>p^{*}
), one might ask what happens in the degenerate case of
p=0
and
p^{*}=-1.
(So we are violating (2) but keeping
p>p^{*}
.) This means that
\mu+\mu^{\prime}
has the shape

((a,a),(b^{*}-1,b^{*}))
. The
\eta
component
(a,a)
is dominant, but one has to make the
\bar{\eta}
-component
(b^{*}-1,b^{*})
dominant. This can only be done using
s^{*}
, however, the reader can easily check that
s^{*}\cdot(b^{*}-1,b^{*})=(b^{*}-1,b^{*})
. In other words, there is no element
w
such that

w^{-1}\cdot(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
is dominant.

3.2.3.
Proof of (2)
\iff
(3) for
{\rm GL}_{n}\times{\rm GL}_{1}

It is most convenient to first understand the case when
{\sf r}=1,
i.e., when
F
is an imaginary quadratic field. Then

\Sigma_{F}=\{\eta,\bar{\eta}\}
(for a non-canonical choice of

\eta:F\to\mathbb{C}
that is fixed once and for all). As above, we will follow a notational artifice that all quantities indexed by
\bar{\eta}
will be designated with a
*
. A weight

\mu\in X^{+}_{0}(T_{n}\times\mathbb{C})
may be written as:

\mu=\{\mu^{\eta},\mu^{\bar{\eta}}\}
with
\mu^{\eta}=(\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{n})
and

\mu^{\bar{\eta}}=(\mu_{1}^{*}\geq\mu_{2}^{*}\geq\cdots\geq\mu_{n}^{*}),

with
\mu_{i},\mu_{j}^{*}\in\mathbb{Z},
and purity implies

{\bf w}\ =\ \mu_{i}+\mu_{n-i+1}^{*}.

A weight
\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{0}(T_{1}\times\mathbb{C})
is simply a pair of integers

\mu^{\prime}=\{b,b^{*}\}
with purity weight
{\bf w}^{\prime}=b+b^{*}
.
The weight
\mu+\mu^{\prime}
is given by:

\mu+\mu^{\prime}\ =\ \{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{n},b),\quad(\mu_{1}^{*},\mu_{2}^{*},\dots,\mu_{n}^{*},b^{*})\}.

We are seeking to understand when we can find a Kostant representative
w\in W^{P}
which is balanced (
l(w^{\eta})+l(w^{\bar{\eta}})=\dim(U_{P_{0}})=n
) and such that
w^{-1}\cdot(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
is a dominant weight. For this, first identify the Kostant representatives for
P_{0}
in
G_{0}
; the simple roots of
M_{P_{0}}
are

{\bf\Pi}_{M_{P_{0}}}=\{e_{1}-e_{2},\,e_{2}-e_{3},\,\dots,e_{n-1}-e_{n}\}.

The Weyl group of
G_{0}
is
W_{G_{0}}=\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
the symmetric group on
n+1
letters. We have

\displaystyle w\in W^{P_{0}}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle w^{-1}(e_{1}-e_{2})>0,\ w^{-1}(e_{2}-e_{3})>0,\ \dots,w^{-1}(e_{n-1}-e_{n})>0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle w^{-1}(1)<w^{-1}(2)<\cdots<w^{-1}(n).

The elements of
W^{P_{0}}
and their lengths are listed below:

\begin{array}[]{|l||c||}\hline\cr w^{-1}\ \ (w\in W^{P_{0}})&l(w)\\
\hline\cr s_{0}:=1&0\\
s_{1}:=(n,n+1)&1\\
s_{2}:=(n-1,n,n+1)&2\\
\quad\quad\vdots&\vdots\\
s_{n-1}:=(2,3,\dots,n+1)&n-1\\
s_{n}:=(1,2,3,\dots,n+1)&n\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Note that the
(n+1)
-cycle
(1,2,\dots,n+1)=(1,2)(2,3)\cdots(n,n+1)
as a product of
n
simple transpositions giving its length which applies to the last row and a similar calculation gives all the other lengths. The Kostant representatives for
P
are:

W^{P}\ =\ \{(w,w^{*})\ :\ w,w^{*}\in W^{P_{0}}\},
where
l(w,w^{*})=l(w)+l(w^{*}).

Hence the inverses of the balanced Kostant representatives are:

\{(s_{0},s_{n}^{*}),\ (s_{1},s_{n-1}^{*}),\dots,(s_{n},s_{0}^{*})\}.

The twisted action of the Kostant representatives on the weight are given in the table below:

(3.19)

\begin{array}[]{||c|l||}\hline\cr w^{-1}\ \ (w\in W^{P_{0}})&\quad w^{-1}\cdot(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{n},b)\\
\hline\cr 1&\quad(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{n},b)\\
(n,n+1)&\quad(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{n-1},b-1,\mu_{n}+1)\\
(n-1,n,n+1)&\quad(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{n-2},b-2,\mu_{n-1}+1,\mu_{n}+1)\\
\quad\quad\vdots&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\vdots\\
(2,3,\dots,n+1)&\quad(\mu_{1},b-n+1,\mu_{2}+1,\dots,\mu_{n-1}+1,\mu_{n}+1)\\
(1,2,3,\dots,n+1)&\quad(b-n,\mu_{1}+1,\dots,\mu_{n-1}+1,\mu_{n}+1)\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

For the combinatorial lemma, the abelian width is given by:

a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ =\ \frac{{\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}}{2}\ =\ \frac{\mu_{i}+\mu_{n-i+1}^{*}-b-b^{*}}{2}\ =\ \frac{(\mu_{i}-b)+(\mu_{n-i+1}^{*}-b^{*})}{2},

and for the cuspidal width, the cuspidal parameters are given by:

\displaystyle\alpha=\ (\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\ =

\displaystyle\ (-\mu_{n}+\tfrac{(n-1)}{2},\ -\mu_{n-1}+\tfrac{(n-3)}{2},\ \dots,\ -\mu_{1}-\tfrac{(n-1)}{2}),

\displaystyle\beta=\ (\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\ =

\displaystyle\ (-\mu_{1}^{*}-\tfrac{(n-1)}{2},\ -\mu_{2}^{*}-\tfrac{(n-3)}{2},\ \dots,\ -\mu_{n}^{*}+\tfrac{(n-1)}{2}),

and similarly,
\alpha^{\prime}=-b,\ \beta^{\prime}=-b^{*},
from which the cuspidal width is:

\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=\min\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}|\mu_{1}^{*}-\mu_{n}+(n-1)+b-b^{*}|,\\
|\mu_{2}^{*}-\mu_{n-1}+(n-3)+b-b^{*}|,\\
\quad\quad\quad\vdots\\
|\mu_{n}^{*}-\mu_{1}-(n-1)+b-b^{*}|\end{array}\right\}.

From the shape of
a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
and
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
it is convenient to introduce the quantities

c_{i}:=\mu_{i}-b
and
c_{i}^{*}:=\mu_{i}^{*}-b^{*}.

(These are the
p
and
p^{*}
when
n=1
.) Then we have:

a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=\frac{c_{i}+c_{n-i+1}^{*}}{2},
and

\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ =\ \min\{\ |c_{1}^{*}-c_{n}+(n-1)|,\ |c_{2}^{*}-c_{n-1}+(n-3)|,\ |c_{n}^{*}-c_{1}-(n-1)|\ \}.

From the dominance of the weights
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
we have the inequalities

c_{1}^{*}-c_{n}+(n-1)\ >\ c_{2}^{*}-c_{n-1}+(n-3)\ >\ \cdots\ >c_{n}^{*}-c_{1}-(n-1).

The proof conveniently breaks into
(n+1)
disjoint cases depending on the relative position of
0
in the above decreasing sequence.

Case 0:

0>c_{1}^{*}-c_{n}+(n-1)\ >\ c_{2}^{*}-c_{n-1}+(n-3)\ >\ \cdots\ >c_{n}^{*}-c_{1}-(n-1),

Case j (
1\ \leq j\leq n-1
):

c_{j}^{*}-c_{n-j+1}+(n-2j+1)\ >0\ >\ c_{j+1}^{*}-c_{n-j}+(n-2j-1),

Case n:

c_{1}^{*}-c_{n}+(n-1)\ >\ c_{2}^{*}-c_{n-1}+(n-3)\ >\ \cdots\ >c_{n}^{*}-c_{1}-(n-1)>0.

In
Case 0
, we have
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=-c_{1}^{*}+c_{n}-(n-1).
Keeping in mind that
N=n+1
, the inequalities in (2) of the lemma read:

-\frac{(n+1)}{2}+1-\frac{(-c_{1}^{*}+c_{n}-(n-1))}{2}\ \leq\ \frac{c_{1}^{*}+c_{n}}{2}\ \leq\ -\frac{(n+1)}{2}-1+\frac{(-c_{1}^{*}+c_{n}-(n-1))}{2}

This simplifies to

c_{1}^{*}-c_{n}\ \leq\ c_{1}^{*}+c_{n}\ \leq\ -c_{1}^{*}+c_{n}-2n-2.

Whence we get:

c_{n}\geq 0,\quad c_{1}^{*}\leq-n-1.

This is exactly the condition that
w^{-1}=(1,s_{n}^{*})
under the twisted action makes
\mu+\mu^{\prime}
dominant. (See the last row of (
3.19
).)

Case n
is similar; we have
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=c_{n}^{*}-c_{1}-(n-1).
The inequalities in (2) of the lemma read:

-\frac{(n+1)}{2}+1-\frac{(c_{n}^{*}-c_{1}-(n-1))}{2}\ \leq\ \frac{c_{n}^{*}+c_{1}}{2}\ \leq\ -\frac{(n+1)}{2}-1+\frac{(c_{n}^{*}-c_{1}-(n-1))}{2}

This simplifies to

-c_{n}^{*}+c_{1}\ \leq\ c_{n}^{*}+c_{1}\ \leq\ c_{n}^{*}-c_{1}-2n-2.

Whence we get:

c_{n}^{*}\geq 0,\quad c_{1}\leq-n-1.

This is exactly the condition that
w^{-1}=(s_{n},1^{*})
makes
\mu+\mu^{\prime}
dominant.

Case j
breaks up into two sub-cases:

Case j1
:

c_{j}^{*}-c_{n-j+1}+(n-2j+1)\geq c_{n-j}-c_{j+1}^{*}-(n-2j-1).

Case j2
:

c_{j}^{*}-c_{n-j+1}+(n-2j+1)<c_{n-j}-c_{j+1}^{*}-(n-2j-1).

For
j1
, we have
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=c_{n-j}-c_{j+1}^{*}-(n-2j-1)
and the inequalities of (2) read:

-\frac{(n+1)}{2}+1-\frac{(c_{n-j}-c_{j+1}^{*}-(n-2j-1))}{2}\ \leq\frac{c_{n-j}+c_{j+1}^{*}}{2},\ {\rm and}\\
\frac{c_{n-j}+c_{j+1}^{*}}{2}\ \leq\ -\frac{(n+1)}{2}-1+\frac{(c_{n-j}-c_{j+1}^{*}-(n-2j-1))}{2}.

These simplify to:

-c_{n-j}+c_{j+1}^{*}-2j\ \leq\ c_{n-j}+c_{j+1}^{*}\ \leq\ c_{n-j}-c_{j+1}^{*}-2n+2j-2.

This in turn implies that:

c_{n-j}\ \geq\ -j,\quad c_{j+1}^{*}\ \leq\ -n+j-1.

Next, we see that the defining inequalities of
j1
gives in particular that

(3.20)

c_{j}^{*}+c_{j+1}^{*}+2n-4j\ \geq\ c_{n-j}+c_{n-j+1}.

Add
c_{n-j+1}
on both sides of (
3.20
) to get

c_{n-j+1}+c_{j}^{*}+c_{j+1}^{*}+2n-4j\ \geq\ c_{n-j}+2c_{n-j+1},

and applying purity, we can rewrite this as

c_{n-j}+2c_{j+1}^{*}+2n-4j\ \geq\ c_{n-j}+2c_{n-j+1},

whence

c_{n-j+1}\ \leq\ c_{j+1}^{*}+n-2j\ \leq\ -j-1.

Next, add
c_{j}^{*}
to both sides of (
3.20
) to get

2c_{j}^{*}+c_{j+1}^{*}+2n-4j\ \geq\ c_{n-j}+c_{n-j+1}+c_{j}^{*},

and applying purity, we can rewrite this as

2c_{j}^{*}+c_{j+1}^{*}+2n-4j\ \geq\ 2c_{n-j}+c_{j+1}^{*}

whence,

c_{j}^{*}\ \geq\ c_{n-j}-n+2j\ \geq\ -n+j.

Putting all this together, we get the following inequalities:

c_{n-j}\geq-j,\quad c_{n-j+1}\leq-j-1,\quad{\rm and}\quad c_{j}^{*}\geq j-n,\quad c_{j+1}^{*}\leq-n+j-1.

For
j2
, we have
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=c_{j}^{*}-c_{n-j+1}+(n-2j+1)
and the inequalities of (2) simplifying to:

c_{j}^{*}\geq-n+j\quad{\rm and}\quad c_{n-j+1}\leq-j-1.

The defining inequalities of
j2
may be written as:

(3.21)

c_{j}^{*}+c_{j+1}^{*}\ \leq\ c_{n-j}+c_{n-j+1}-2n+4j.

Add
c_{j+1}^{*}
to both sides of (
3.21
), apply purity to right hand side, and simplify to get:

c_{j+1}^{*}\ \leq\ -n+j-1.

Next, add
c_{n-j}
to both sides of (
3.21
), apply purity to left hand side, and simplify to get:

c_{n-j}\ \geq\ -j.

Putting all this together, we see exactly as in
Case j1
that

c_{n-j}\geq-j,\quad c_{n-j+1}\leq-j-1,\quad{\rm and}\ c_{j}^{*}\geq-n+j,\quad c_{j+1}^{*}\leq-n+j-1.

Using the table (
3.19
) we see that

c_{n-j}\geq-j,\quad c_{n-j+1}\leq-j-1\ \iff\ s_{j}\cdot(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n},b)\ \mbox{is dominant}.

c_{j}^{*}\geq j-n,\quad c_{j+1}^{*}\leq-n+j-1\ \iff\ s_{n-j}^{*}\cdot(\mu_{1}^{*},\dots,\mu_{n}^{*},b^{*})\ \mbox{is dominant}.

So, in
Case j
, the required balanced Kostant representative is the inverse of
(s_{j},s_{n-j}^{*}).

Conversely, if
w^{-1}=(s_{j},s_{n-j}^{*})
makes
(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
dominant then we just argue backwards in the above paragraphs to see that inequalities of (2) are satisfied. Thus far, we have proved (2)
\iff
(3) when
F
is imaginary quadratic.

3.2.3.1
A general totally imaginary field

Now let
F
be any totally imaginary field. For each
v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}
we have a pair of complex embeddings

\{\eta_{v},\bar{\eta}_{v}\}
of
F
. For any such embedding
\eta
, the weight
\mu
, has a
\eta
-component
\mu^{\eta}=(\mu^{\eta}_{1},\dots,\mu^{\eta}_{n})
which is a non-increasing sequence of integers, and similarly,
\mu^{\prime\eta}=(b^{\eta})
is just an integer. Define
c_{j}^{\eta}=\mu^{\eta}_{j}-b^{\eta}
. The abelian width is given by:

a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ =\ \frac{c_{j}^{\eta}+c_{n-j+1}^{\bar{\eta}}}{2},

for any
j
and any
\eta.

For
v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}
, define
\ell_{v}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
as the minimum of the absolute
values of the following
n
integers:

c^{\bar{\eta}_{v}}_{1}-c^{\eta_{v}}_{n}+(n-1)\ >\ c^{\bar{\eta}_{v}}_{2}-c_{n-1}^{\eta_{v}}+(n-3)\ >\ \cdots\ >c^{\bar{\eta}_{v}}_{n}-c_{1}^{\eta_{v}}-(n-1).

Then

\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=\min\{\ell_{v}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\,:\,v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}\}.

The inequalities of (2) imply that for each
v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}
we have

(3.22)

-\frac{N}{2}+1-\frac{\ell_{v}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}{2}\ \leq\ a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ \leq\ -\frac{N}{2}-1+\frac{\ell_{v}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}{2}.

Using the same argument as in the imaginary quadratic case, we see that there exists

w_{v}\ =\ (w_{\eta_{v}},w_{\bar{\eta}_{v}})\ \in\ W^{(P_{0}\times_{\eta_{v}}\mathbb{C})\times(P_{0}\times_{\bar{\eta}_{v}}\mathbb{C})}

such that

w_{v}^{-1}\cdot((\mu^{\eta_{v}},\mu^{\prime\eta_{v}}),(\mu^{\bar{\eta}_{v}},\mu^{\prime\bar{\eta}_{v}}))
is dominant and
l(w_{\eta_{v}})+l(w_{\bar{\eta}_{v}})=n.
The required balanced Kostant representative then is

w=(w_{v})_{v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}}
; hence (3) is satisfied.
Conversely, if (3) holds, then writing
w=(w^{\eta})
as
w=(w_{v})
with
w_{v}=(w_{\eta_{v}},w_{\bar{\eta}_{v}})
, we see that

w_{v}^{-1}\cdot((\mu^{\eta_{v}},\mu^{\prime\eta_{v}}),(\mu^{\bar{\eta}_{v}},\mu^{\prime\bar{\eta}_{v}}))
is dominant, and working backwards as in the imaginary quadratic case, we deduce
(
3.22
) holds for each
v
, and hence (2) holds.

\Box

3.2.4.
Proof of (2)
\iff
(3) in the general case

First of all, we will prove it in the special case when
F
is imaginary quadratic, i.e.,
\mathfrak{r}=1.

3.2.4.1
Parametrizing Kostant representatives

We will need explicit Kostant representatives. Recall, that
G_{0}={\rm GL}_{N}
and

P_{0}=M_{P_{0}}U_{P_{0}}
the standard
(n,n^{\prime})
-parabolic subgroup of
G_{0}
, where
N=n+n^{\prime};
clearly,
\dim(U_{P_{0}})=nn^{\prime}.

Then
W_{G_{0}}=\mathfrak{S}_{N}
the permutation group on
N
letters, and
W_{M_{P_{0}}}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n^{\prime}}.
The set of Kostant representatives
W^{P_{0}}
may be described as:

(3.23)

W^{P_{0}}\ =\ \{w\in W_{G_{0}}\ :\ w^{-1}(1)<\cdots<w^{-1}(n)\ {\rm and}\ w^{-1}(n+1)<\cdots<w^{-1}(N)\}.

The set
W^{P_{0}}
is in bijection with the set of all
n
-tuples
\kappa=(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
where

1\leq k_{1}<\cdots<k_{n}\leq N
. Any such
\kappa
corresponds to
w_{\kappa}\in W^{P_{0}}
which is uniquely defined by the conditions:

(3.24)

w_{\kappa}^{-1}(1)=k_{1},\,\dots,w_{\kappa}^{-1}(n)=k_{n}.

If
\kappa=(1,2,\dots,n)
then
w_{\kappa}
is the identity element.
There is a self-bijection
W^{P_{0}}\to W^{P_{0}}
defined by
w_{\kappa}\mapsto w_{\kappa^{\sf v}}
, where

(3.25)

\kappa^{\sf v}\ :=\ N+1-k_{n}\ <\ \cdots\ <N+1-k_{1};\quad\kappa^{\sf v}_{j}=N+1-k_{n-j+1}.

Let
w_{N}=w_{G_{0}}\in W_{G_{0}}
denote the element of longest length, which is given by
w_{N}(j)=N+1-j
for any
1\leq j\leq N
; clearly,
w_{G_{0}}^{2}=1.

Similarly,
w_{n}
and
w_{n^{\prime}}
are defined, and we have
w_{M_{P_{0}}}=w_{n}\times w_{n^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 3.26
.

With the notations as above, we have:

(1)

l(w_{\kappa})=(k_{1}-1)+(k_{2}-2)+\dots+(k_{n}-n).

(2)

l(w_{\kappa})+l(w_{\kappa^{\sf v}})=nn^{\prime}=\dim(U_{P_{0}}).

(3)

w_{\kappa^{\sf v}}=w_{M_{P_{0}}}w_{\kappa}w_{G_{0}}.

Proof.

Clearly,
l(w_{\kappa})=l(w_{\kappa}^{-1})
, and
for counting the length of
w_{\kappa}^{-1}
, count the number of its shuffles, i.e., count the number of pairs
(i,j)
with
1\leq i<j\leq N
with
w_{\kappa}^{-1}(i)>w_{\kappa}^{-1}(j).
But for
any such shuffle, by (
3.23
), it is clear that
1\leq i\leq n
and
n+1\leq j\leq N
. We leave it to the reader to see that for a fixed
i\leq n
, the number of shuffles
(i,j)
is
k_{i}-i
.
Also, (2) follows from Statement (1) and Equation (
3.25
). To see the validity of (3), compute the inverses of both sides on any
1\leq j\leq n
:

(w_{G_{0}}w_{\kappa}^{-1}w_{M_{P_{0}}})(j)=(w_{G_{0}}w_{\kappa}^{-1})(n+1-j)=w_{G_{0}}(k_{n+1-j})=N+1-k_{n+1-j}=\kappa^{\sf v}_{j}=w_{\kappa^{\sf v}}^{-1}(j).

∎

3.2.4.2
Twisted action of
W^{P_{0}}
on weights

The usual permutation action of
\sigma\in S_{m}
on an
m
-tuple is given by:

\sigma(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})\ =\ (t_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},\dots,t_{\sigma^{-1}(m)}).
If
\underline{t}:=(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})
, then
the twisted action of
\sigma
on
\underline{t}
is defined by:

\sigma\cdot\underline{t}=\sigma(\underline{t}+\bm{\uprho}_{m})-\bm{\uprho}_{m},
which unravels to

\sigma\cdot(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})\ =\ (t_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}+1-\sigma^{-1}(1),\,t_{\sigma^{-1}(2)}+2-\sigma^{-1}(2),\,\dots,\,t_{\sigma^{-1}(m)}+m-\sigma^{-1}(m)).

Now, keeping the combinatorial lemma in mind, suppose

\mu=((b_{1},\dots,b_{n}),\,(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})),\quad\mu^{\prime}=((b^{\prime}_{1},\dots,b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}),\,(c^{\prime}_{1},\dots,c^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})),

where each
n
-tuple or
n^{\prime}
-tuple is a non-increasing string of integers satisfying the purity condition:

{\bf w}=b_{i}+c_{n-i+1},\ {\bf w}^{\prime}=b^{\prime}_{j}+c^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}-j+1}.

We are seeking a Kostant representative of optimal length that ‘straightens out’

\mu+\mu^{\prime}\ =\ ((b_{1},\dots,b_{n},b^{\prime}_{1},\dots,b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}),\,(c_{1},\dots,c_{n},c^{\prime}_{1},\dots,c^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})).

For this, we need the twisted action of
w_{\kappa}^{-1}
on an
(n+n^{\prime})
-tuple like
(b_{1},\dots,b_{n},b^{\prime}_{1},\dots,b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})
. Given
\kappa
, let us define its complement
\kappa^{c}
as
the ordered string of integers:

\kappa^{c}\ :=\ k^{c}_{1}<\cdots<k^{c}_{n^{\prime}}\ :=\ \{1,2,\dots,N\}\setminus\{k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{n}\}.

It is useful to note that

\kappa^{c}\ =\ \{1,2,\dots,k_{1}-1,k_{1}+1,\dots,k_{2}-1,k_{2}+1,\dots,k_{n}-1,k_{n}+1,\dots,N\}.

The element
w_{\kappa}^{-1}\in W^{P_{0}}
is the permutation that may be written as:

\begin{pmatrix}1&2&\dots&n&n+1&\dots&N\\
k_{1}&k_{2}&\dots&k_{n}&k^{c}_{1}&\dots&k^{c}_{n^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix},

and the permutation
w_{\kappa}
is:

\left(\begin{matrix}1&\dots&k_{1}-1&k_{1}&k_{1}+1&\dots&k_{2}-1&k_{2}&k_{2}+1&\dots\\
n+1&\dots&n+k_{1}-1&1&n+k_{1}&\dots&n+k_{2}-2&2&n+k_{2}-1&\dots\end{matrix}\right.\\
\left.\begin{matrix}\dots&k_{n-1}&k_{n-1}+1&\dots&k_{n}-1&k_{n}&k_{n}+1&\dots&N\\
\dots&n-1&k_{n-1}+2&\dots&k_{n}&n&k_{n}+1&\dots&N\end{matrix}\right).

(The reader should pay some attention to the special cases
k_{1}=1
and
k_{n}=N
.)
Denoting

(b_{1},\dots,b_{n},b^{\prime}_{1},\dots,b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})\ =\ (d_{1},\dots,d_{n},d_{n+1},\dots,d_{N}),

we have:

(3.27)

w_{\kappa}^{-1}\cdot(d_{1},\dots,d_{n},d_{n+1},\dots,d_{N})\ =\\
(d_{w_{\kappa}(1)}+1-w_{\kappa}(1),\ d_{w_{\kappa}(2)}+2-w_{\kappa}(2),\ \dots\ ,d_{w_{\kappa}(N)}+N-w_{\kappa}(N)).

3.2.4.3
Dominance of
w_{\kappa}^{-1}\cdot(d_{1},\dots,d_{N})

Let us enumerate the inequalities that guarantee dominance of the weight in (
3.27
):

Proposition 3.28
.

The weight
w_{\kappa}^{-1}\cdot(d_{1},\dots,d_{n},d_{n+1},\dots,d_{N})
is dominant if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

(0)

If
k_{1}-1\geq 1
then

b^{\prime}_{k_{1}-1}-b_{1}\ \geq\ n+k_{1}-1.

If
k_{1}=1
then there is no such condition.

(1)

If
k_{2}\geq k_{1}+2
then

(i)

b_{1}-b^{\prime}_{k_{1}}\ \geq\ -n-k_{1}+2,

and

(ii)

b^{\prime}_{k_{2}-2}-b_{2}\ \geq\ n+k_{2}-3.

If
k_{2}=k_{1}+1
then there are no such conditions.

\vdots

(l)

(
1\leq l\leq n-1
) If
k_{l+1}\geq k_{l}+2
then

(i)

b_{l}-b^{\prime}_{k_{l}+1-l}\ \geq\ -n-k_{l}+2l,

and

(ii)

b^{\prime}_{k_{l+1}-l-1}-b_{l+1}\ \geq\ n+k_{l+1}-2l-1.

If
k_{l+1}=k_{l}+1
then there are no such conditions.

\vdots

(
n-1
)

If
k_{n}\ \geq\ k_{n-1}+2
then

(i)

b_{n-1}-b^{\prime}_{k_{n-1}+2-n}\ \geq\ n-k_{n-1}-2,

and

(ii)

b^{\prime}_{k_{n}-n}-b_{n}\ \geq\ -n+k_{n}+1.

If
k_{n}=k_{n-1}+1
then there are no such conditions.

(n)

If
k_{n}\leq N-1
then

b_{n}-b^{\prime}_{k_{n}+1-n}\ \geq\ n-k_{n}

If
k_{n}=N
then there is no such condition.

In the above
n+1
conditions, some of them might be empty, however not all can be empty.

Proof.

The tedious argument has the same flavour for each case
(1),(2),\dots(l),\dots(n-1),(n)
; as a representative, let us verify (1).
If
k_{2}\geq k_{1}+2
then looking at the relevant part of
w_{\kappa}
:

\begin{pmatrix}\dots&k_{1}&k_{1}+1&\dots&k_{2}-1&k_{2}&\dots\\
\dots&1&n+k_{1}&\dots&n+k_{2}-2&2&\dots\end{pmatrix}

we will have two dominance conditions: comparing entries at steps
k_{1}
and
k_{1}+1
gives

(3.29)

d_{w_{\kappa}(k_{1})}+k_{1}-w_{\kappa}(k_{1})\ \geq\ d_{w_{\kappa}(k_{1}+1)}+k_{1}+1-w_{\kappa}(k_{1}+1)

and, similarly, comparing entries at steps
k_{2}-1
and
k_{2}
gives

(3.30)

d_{w_{\kappa}(k_{2}-1)}+k_{2}-1-w_{\kappa}(k_{2}-1)\ \geq\ d_{w_{\kappa}(k_{2})}+k_{2}-w_{\kappa}(k_{2}).

Now, (
3.29
) unravels to
b_{1}+k_{1}-1\geq b^{\prime}_{k_{1}}+1-n
which is (1)(i), and similarly, (
3.30
) unravels to

b^{\prime}_{k_{2}-2}+1-n\geq b_{2}+k_{2}-2
which is (1)(ii).
However, if
k_{2}=k_{1}+1
, then the corresponding part of the permutation
w_{\kappa}
just collapses to

\begin{pmatrix}\dots&k_{1}&k_{2}&\dots\\
\dots&1&2&\dots\end{pmatrix}

and dominance is assured since
b_{1}\geq b_{2}
.
∎

Proposition 3.31
.

The weight
w_{\kappa^{\sf v}}^{-1}\cdot(c_{1},\dots,c_{n},c^{\prime}_{1},\dots,c^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})
is dominant if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

(
0^{\sf v}
)

If
k^{\sf v}_{1}-1\geq 1
then

b_{n}-b^{\prime}_{k_{n}+1-n}\ \geq\ n-k_{n}+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})).

If
k^{\sf v}_{1}=1
then there is no such condition.

(
1^{\sf v}
)

If
k^{\sf v}_{2}\geq k^{\sf v}_{1}+2
then

(i)^{\sf v}

b^{\prime}_{k_{n}-n}-b_{n}\ \geq\ -n+k_{n}+1-(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})),

and

(ii)^{\sf v}

b_{n-1}-b^{\prime}_{k_{n-1}+2-n}\ \geq\ n-k_{n-1}-2+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})).

If
k^{\sf v}_{2}=k^{\sf v}_{1}+1
then there are no such conditions.

\vdots

(
l^{\sf v}
)

If
k^{\sf v}_{l+1}\geq k^{\sf v}_{l}+2
then

(i)^{\sf v}

b^{\prime}_{k_{n-l+1}-n+l-1}-b_{n-l+1}\ \geq\ k_{n-l+1}-n+(2l-1)-(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})),

and

(ii)^{\sf v}

b_{n-l}-b^{\prime}_{k_{n-l}+1-n+l}\ \geq\ -k_{n-l}+n-2l+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})).

If
k^{\sf v}_{l+1}=k^{\sf v}_{l}+1
then there are no such conditions.

\vdots

(
(n-1)^{\sf v}
)

If
k^{\sf v}_{n}\ \geq\ k^{\sf v}_{n-1}+2
then

(i)^{\sf v}

b^{\prime}_{k_{2}-2}-b_{2}\ \geq\ n+k_{2}-3-(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})),

and

(ii)^{\sf v}

b_{1}-b^{\prime}_{k_{1}}\ \geq\ -n-k_{1}+2+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})).

If
k^{\sf v}_{n}=k^{\sf v}_{n-1}+1
then there are no such conditions.

(n^{\sf v})

If
k^{\sf v}_{n}\leq N-1
then

b^{\prime}_{k_{1}-1}-b_{1}\ \geq\ n+k_{1}-1-(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})).

If
k^{\sf v}_{n}=N
then there is no such condition.

Proof.

Apply Prop.
3.28
while replacing

•

k_{j}
by
k^{\sf v}_{j}=N+1-k_{n+1-j},

•

b_{j}
by
c_{j}={\bf w}-b_{n+1-j},
and

•

b^{\prime}_{j}
by
c^{\prime}_{j}={\bf w}^{\prime}-b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}-j+1}
,

As an illustrative example, let us make these replacements in case
(1)(i)
of Prop.
3.28
, then we get

c_{1}-c^{\prime}_{k_{1}^{\sf v}}\geq-n-k_{1}^{\sf v}+2\quad\iff\quad c_{1}-c^{\prime}_{N+1-k_{n}}\geq-n+k_{n}+1-N,

which may be written as

({\bf w}-b_{n})-({\bf w}-b^{\prime}_{k_{n}-n})\geq-n+k_{n}+1-N\quad\iff\quad b^{\prime}_{k_{n}-n}-b_{n}\geq-n+k_{n}+1-(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})),

giving us case
(1)^{\sf v}(i)^{\sf v}
. Similarly, all the other cases may be verified.
∎

Remark 3.32
.

Let us note the following ‘duality’ relations between the various cases of Prop.
3.28
and Prop.
3.31
.

•

k^{\sf v}_{1}=1\iff k_{n}=N
.

(Compare
(0)^{\sf v}
of Prop.
3.31
with
(n)
of Prop.
3.28
.)

•

k^{\sf v}_{n}=N\iff k_{1}=1
.

(Compare
(n)^{\sf v}
with
(0)
.)

•

k^{\sf v}_{j}\geq k^{\sf v}_{j-1}+2\iff k_{n+2-j}\geq k_{n+1-j}+2,
for
2\leq j\leq n
.

(Compare
(1)^{\sf v}(i)^{\sf v}
with
(n-1)(ii)
and
(1)^{\sf v}(ii)^{\sf v}
with
(n-1)(i)
.)

In this comparison, an inequality of the form
b_{i}-b^{\prime}_{j}\geq\beta
in Prop.
3.28
corresponds to

b_{i}-b^{\prime}_{j}\geq\beta+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}))
in Prop.
3.31
. Similarly, an inequality of the form

b^{\prime}_{j}-b_{i}\geq\beta
in Prop.
3.28
corresponds to

b^{\prime}_{j}-b_{i}\geq\beta-(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}))
in Prop.
3.31
.

3.2.4.4
The inner structure of the cuspidal width - I

For the weight
\mu
, written as above
\mu=((b_{1},\dots,b_{n}),\,(c_{1},\dots,c_{n}))
, recall its cuspidal parameters from (
2.8
) and (
2.9
):

\alpha_{i}\ =\ -b_{n-i+1}+\tfrac{(n-2i+1)}{2}\quad\beta_{i}\ =\ -c_{i}-\tfrac{(n-2i+1)}{2}.

Similarly, for
\mu^{\prime}=((b^{\prime}_{1},\dots,b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}),\,(c^{\prime}_{1},\dots,c^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}))
, we have

\alpha^{\prime}_{j}\ =\ -b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}-j+1}+\tfrac{(n^{\prime}-2j+1)}{2},\quad\beta^{\prime}_{j}\ =\ -c^{\prime}_{j}-\tfrac{(n^{\prime}-2j+1)}{2}.

For
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}
, define
\ell_{i,j}:=\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i}-\alpha^{\prime}_{j}+\beta^{\prime}_{j}.

Applying purity we have:

(3.33)

\ell_{i,j}\ =\ 2(b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}-j+1}-b_{n-i+1})+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}))+-2n^{\prime}+2(j-i).

These
nn^{\prime}
integers are ordered thus:

(3.34)

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\ell_{1,1}&<&\ell_{1,2}&<&\cdots&<&\ell_{1,n^{\prime}}\\
\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$<$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$<$}&&&&\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$<$}\\
\ell_{2,1}&<&\ell_{2,2}&<&\cdots&<&\ell_{2,n^{\prime}}\\
\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$<$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$<$}&&&&\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$<$}\\
&\vdots&&&\vdots&&\\
\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$<$}&&\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$<$}&&&&\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$<$}\\
\ell_{n,1}&<&\ell_{n,2}&<&\cdots&<&\ell_{n,n^{\prime}}\\
\end{array}

Recall the cuspidal width is defined as

\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ =\ \min\{|\ell_{i,j}|\ :\ 1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}\}.

From (
3.34
), we see that the location of
0
relative to these
nn^{\prime}
integers is important to determine the cuspidal width.

3.2.4.5
On how
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
determine
\kappa

Consider the
j
-th column of (
3.34
). Define
\ell_{0,j}=\infty
(or a large positive integer), and

\ell_{n+1,j}=-\infty
(or a large negative integer). For each
1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}
, define
r_{j}
with
0\leq r_{j}\leq n
such that

\begin{array}[]{c}\ell_{r_{j},j}\\
\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\leq$}\\
0\\
\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$<$}\\
\ell_{r_{j}+1,j}.\end{array}

The integer
r_{j}
defines the location of
0
in the
j
-th column. For example, if all the
\ell_{*,j}\geq 0
then
r_{j}=n,
and similarly, if
all
\ell_{*,j}<0
then
r_{j}=0.
Note that

0\leq r_{1}\leq r_{2}\leq\cdots\leq r_{n^{\prime}}\leq n.

Next, define a string of integers
s_{j}
by:

s_{j}=r_{j}+j-1;
then

0\leq s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{n^{\prime}}\leq N-1.

Now define
\kappa=k_{1}<\cdots<k_{n}
by:

(3.35)

\{k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\}\ :=\ \{1,2,\dots,N\}\setminus\{N-s_{n^{\prime}},N-s_{n^{\prime}-1},\dots,N-s_{1}\}.

3.2.4.6
The inner structure of the cuspidal width - II

Suppose there are
p
strict inequalities in the sequence
r_{1}\leq r_{2}\leq\cdots\leq r_{n^{\prime}}
, i.e, we have:

r_{1}=\cdots=r_{t_{1}}\ <\ r_{t_{1}+1}=\cdots=r_{t_{2}}\ <\ \cdots\ =r_{t_{p}}<r_{t_{p}+1}=\cdots=r_{n^{\prime}}.

Let us denote the common values thus:

(3.36)

r^{(1)}\ :=\ r_{1}=\cdots=r_{t_{1}},\quad r^{(2)}\ :=\ r_{t_{1}+1}=\cdots=r_{t_{2}},\quad\dots,\quad r^{(p+1)}\ :=\ r_{t_{p}+1}=\cdots=r_{n^{\prime}}.

Note that
1\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{p}<n^{\prime}.

Define the quantity:

(3.37)

\delta\ :=\ 2(p+1)-\delta(r_{1},0)-\delta(r_{n^{\prime}},n),

where in the last two terms,
\delta(i,j)=1
if
i=j
and
\delta(i,j)=0
if
i\neq j.
We have the following

Lemma 3.38
.

The cuspidal width
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
is the minimum of the set

\mathbb{L}\ :=\ \{\ell_{r^{(1)},\,1},\ -\ell_{r^{(1)}+1,\,t_{1}},\ \ell_{r^{(2)},\,t_{1}+1},\ -\ell_{r^{(2)}+1,\,t_{2}},\ \dots\ \ell_{r^{(p+1)},\,t_{p}+1},\ -\ell_{r^{(p+1)}+1,\,n^{\prime}}\}

with the understanding that

•

if
\delta(r_{1},0)=1
then
r^{(1)}=0
and we delete the term
\ell_{r^{(1)},\,1}
from
\mathbb{L}
, and similarly,

•

if
\delta(r_{n^{\prime}},n)=1
then
r^{(p+1)}=n
and we delete the term
-\ell_{r^{(p+1)}+1,\,n^{\prime}}
from
\mathbb{L}.

The cardinality of the set
\mathbb{L}
is
\delta.

Proof.

This follows from (
3.34
); the cardinality of
\mathbb{L}
follows from (
3.37
).
∎

3.2.4.7
The proof of the combinatorial lemma - I

The proof of (2)
\iff
(3)
of the combinatorial lemma (for the case of an imaginary quadratic extension) follows from the following

Proposition 3.39
.

The following are equivalent:

(1)

-N+2-\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ \leq\ ({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})\ \leq\ -N-2+\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime}).

(2)

The element
w=(w_{\kappa},w_{\kappa^{\sf v}})
satisfies
w^{-1}\cdot(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
is dominant.

Note that the requirement of the Kostant representative to be balanced is automatically taken care of by (2), since by Lem.
3.26
, (2),
we have
l(w)=l(w_{\kappa})+l(w_{\kappa^{\sf v}})=nn^{\prime}.

Proof.

The information contained in the inequalities

-N+2-\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ \leq\ ({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})\ \leq\ -N-2+\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})

is clearly equivalent to the set of
2\delta
inequalities

(3.40)

\ell\ \geq\ 2+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}))\quad{\rm and}\quad\ell\geq 2-(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})),\quad\forall\ell\in\mathbb{L}.

Let us begin the analysis of various cases and consider each of the above inequalities:

•

Suppose
r_{1}=0.
From (
3.35
) it follows that
r_{1}=0\iff k_{n}\leq N-1\iff k_{1}^{\sf v}\geq 2.

The condition
r_{1}=\cdots=r_{t_{1}}=0
(which means the first
t_{1}
many columns of (
3.34
) are negative)
implies that
N-t_{1}+1,\dots,N-1,N
are deleted in defining
\kappa
in (
3.35
),
hence
k_{n}=N-t_{1}.

Now, consider the term

\ell=-\ell_{r^{(1)}+1,\,t_{1}}=-\ell_{1,N-k_{n}}\in\mathbb{L}.
From (
3.33
) we have

-\ell_{1,N-k_{n}}\ =\ 2(b_{n}-b^{\prime}_{k_{n}+1-n})-(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}))+2n^{\prime}-2(N-k_{n}-1).

Applying (
3.40
) to
-\ell_{1,N-k_{n}}
gives us:

b_{n}-b^{\prime}_{k_{n}+1-n}\ \geq\ n-k_{n}+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})),\quad{\rm and}\quad b_{n}-b^{\prime}_{k_{n}+1-n}\ \geq\ n-k_{n},

which are the same as the bounds in case-
(0^{\sf v})
of Prop.
3.31
and case-
(n)
of Prop.
3.28
.

•

Suppose
r_{n^{\prime}}=n.
From (
3.35
) it follows that
r_{1}=0\iff k_{1}\geq 2\iff k_{n}^{\sf v}\leq N-1.

The condition
r_{t_{p}+1}=\cdots=r_{n^{\prime}}=n

(which means that in (
3.34
) the last
t_{p}
-columns are all non-negative)
implies that
1,2,\dots,N-(n+t_{p})
are deleted in getting
\kappa
in (
3.35
), hence

k_{1}=n^{\prime}-t_{p}+1.
Now, consider the term

\ell=-\ell_{r^{(p+1)},\,t_{p}+1}=-\ell_{n,n^{\prime}-k_{1}+2}\in\mathbb{L}.
From (
3.33
) we have

\ell_{n,n^{\prime}-k_{1}+2}\ =\ 2(b^{\prime}_{k_{1}-1}-b_{1})+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}))-2k_{1}-2n+4.

Applying (
3.40
) to
\ell_{n,n^{\prime}-k_{1}+2}
gives us:

b^{\prime}_{k_{1}-1}-b_{1}\ \geq\ n+k_{1}-1,\quad{\rm and}\quad b^{\prime}_{k_{1}-1}-b_{1}\ \geq\ n+k_{1}-1+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})),

which are exactly the bounds described case-
(0)
of Prop.
3.28
and case-
(n)^{\sf v}
of Prop.
3.31
.

•

Suppose
r_{1}\geq 1.
Then the shape of
\kappa
is of the form:

\kappa\ =\ \{\dots,N-r_{1}-t_{1},\widehat{N-r_{1}+-t_{1}},\dots,\widehat{N-r_{1}},N-r_{1}+1,\dots,N-1,N\},

where the
\widehat{a}
means that
a
is deleted from that list. This implies that

k_{n}=N,\ k_{n-1}=N-1,\ \dots,k_{n-r_{1}+1}=N-r_{1}+1,\ k_{n-r_{1}}=N-r_{1}-t_{1},\dots

Hence we see that

if
\ l\ :=\ n-r_{1}\
then
\ k_{l}=N-r_{1}-t_{1},\ \ k_{l+1}=N-r_{1}+1.

In particular,
k_{l+1}-k_{l}=1+t_{1}\geq 2.

Put
l^{\sf v}=n-l+1
then, by definition of
\kappa^{\sf v}
we also have
k^{\sf v}_{l^{\sf v}}-k^{\sf v}_{l^{\sf v}-1}\geq 2.

Note that
l^{\sf v}=n-(n-r_{1})+1=r_{1}+1.
Hence we have
k^{\sf v}_{r_{1}+1}-k^{\sf v}_{r_{1}}\geq 2.

Consider the elements
\ell_{r_{1},1}
and
-\ell_{r_{1}+1,t_{1}}
in
\mathbb{L}.

Note that

\ell_{r_{1},1}=2(b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}-b_{n-r_{1}+1})+(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}))-2n^{\prime}+2(1-r_{1}).

If we apply (
3.40
) to
\ell_{r_{1},1}
we get

b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}-b_{n-r_{1}+1}\geq n^{\prime}+r_{1}\quad{\rm and}\quad b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}-b_{n-r_{1}+1}\geq n^{\prime}+r_{1}-(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})).

We will leave it to the reader check that these are exactly the inequalities we get from
case-
(l)(ii)
of Prop.
3.28
and case-
(r_{1})^{\sf v}(i)^{\sf v}
of Prop.
3.31
.

Next, note that

-\ell_{r_{1}+1,t_{1}}=-2(b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}-t_{1}+1}-b_{n-r_{1}})-(N+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}))+2n^{\prime}-2(t_{1}-r_{1}-1).

Apply (
3.40
) to
-\ell_{r_{1}+1,t_{1}}
to get

b_{n-r_{1}}-b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}-t_{1}+1}\geq-n^{\prime}+t_{1}-r_{1}

and

b_{n-r_{1}}-b^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}-t_{1}+1}\geq-n^{\prime}+t_{1}-r_{1}+(Nr_{1}+({\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime})).

We will leave it to the reader check that these are exactly the inequalities we get from
case-
(l)(i)
of Prop.
3.28
and case-
(r_{1})^{\sf v}(ii)^{\sf v}
of Prop.
3.31
.

Let us summarize the above three cases as:

(1)

If
r_{1}=0
then
(n)
and
(0^{\sf v})
hold.

(2)

If
r_{n^{\prime}}=n
then
(0)
and
(n)^{\sf v}
hold.

(3)

If
r_{1}\geq 1
then
(n-r_{1})(i),

(n-r_{1})(ii)
,
(r_{1}^{\sf v})(i)^{\sf v}
and
(r_{1}^{\sf v})(ii)^{\sf v}
hold. (Furthermore, cases

(1)^{\sf v}
through
(r_{1}-1)^{\sf v}
are empty and
(n-r_{1}+1)
through
(n)
are empty.)

•

It should be clear now, that for each
q
with
1\leq q\leq p
, using
t_{q}
or
r^{(q)}
as the anchor, we get all the cases of Prop.
3.28
and Prop.
3.31
,
and hence
w^{-1}\cdot(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
is dominant.

The entire argument is reversible, i.e., if the cases of Prop.
3.28
and Prop.
3.31
hold the inequalities in (
3.40
) are satisfied.
This completes the proof of Prop.
3.39
.
∎

3.2.4.8
The general totally imaginary field

Now if
F
is any totally imaginary field, then the proof reduces to working with pairs of complex embeddings
(\eta_{v},\bar{\eta}_{v})
for
a
v\in\mathsf{S}_{\infty}
; it is entirely analogous to Sect.
3.2.3.1
. We will leave the details to the reader.

3.2.5.
The combinatorial lemma at an arithmetic level

All the three statements in the combinatorial lemma work at an arithmetic level. Take
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times E)
and

\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times E)
, and
\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu),

\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime}),
and for

\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
, consider the statement of the lemma for
{}^{\iota}\mu,{}^{\iota}\mu^{\prime},{}^{\iota}\sigma
and
{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}
; let us add some comments
for each of (1), (2) and (3) of the lemma:

(1)

From Sec.
3.1.3
it follows that
-\tfrac{N}{2}
and
1-\tfrac{N}{2}
are critical for

L(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})
for any
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}.

(2)

Since
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are strongly-pure, it is easy to see that the abelian width
a({}^{\iota}\mu,{}^{\iota}\mu^{\prime})
and the cuspidal width

\ell({}^{\iota}\mu,{}^{\iota}\mu^{\prime})
are independent of
\iota.
(See Cor.
3.15
.)
For the assertion for cuspidal width, the reader may check from definitions that the

\ell({}^{\iota}\mu,{}^{\iota}\mu^{\prime})
is given by taking the minimum of

|-2\mu^{\iota\circ\tau}_{n-i+1}+2\mu^{\prime\iota\circ\tau}_{n^{\prime}-j+1}+n-n^{\prime}+2j-2i+{\bf w}-{\bf w}^{\prime}|
over all
\tau:F\to E,
and all indices

1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}.
As
\tau
varies over
{\rm Hom}(F,E)
,
\iota\circ\tau
varies over
{\rm Hom}(F,\mathbb{C}),
making the above minimum
independent of
\iota.

(3)

Write
w\in W^{G}
as
w=(w^{\tau})_{\tau:F\to E}.
We will say
w\in W^{P}
is balanced if
l(w^{\tau})+l(w^{\overline{\tau}^{\iota}})=\dim(U_{P_{0}})
for all
\tau\in{\rm Hom}(F,E)
and for all
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
; recall that
\iota
induces a complex conjugation
\tau\mapsto{\overline{\tau}^{\iota}}
on
{\rm Hom}(F,E)
. (See Rem.
3.41
below.)

It should now be clear that
(1)\iff(2)\iff(3)
of the lemma is independent of
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}.

Remark 3.41
.

Strongly-pure weights
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times E)
and

\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times E)
being the base-change from
F_{1}
(Prop.
2.6
), it follows when the conditions of the combinatorial lemma hold,
that the Kostant representative
w=(w^{\tau})_{\tau:F\to E}
is also the base-change from
F_{1}
in the sense that if
\tau|_{F_{1}}=\tau^{\prime}|_{F_{1}}
then

w^{\tau}=w^{\tau^{\prime}}.

4.
Archimedean intertwining operator

4.1.
The case of
{\rm GL}_{2}

The calculations in this subsection are in principle the same as in Harder
[
22
, Sect. 3.5]
, but we need to go through this exercise to reorganise our thoughts, while using inputs from
[
26
, Chap. 9]
, so as to generalize them to
{\rm GL}_{N}
in
the next subsection. The main result of this subsection is Prop.
4.20
.

4.1.1.
Explicit cohomology class for
{\rm GL}_{2}

Let
\mu=((b_{1},b_{2}),(c_{1},c_{2}))
be a pure dominant integral weight for
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
as a real group. Integrality means
b_{1},b_{2},c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
; dominance is
b_{1}\geq b_{2}
and
c_{1}\geq c_{2}
; purity means

b_{1}+c_{2}=b_{2}+c_{1}
, which allows us to define
m:=b_{1}-b_{2}+1=c_{1}-c_{2}+1
. The cuspidal parameters are

(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=(-b_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2},-b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2})
and
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(-c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2},-c_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}).

We have the induced representation

\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\ =\ {\rm Ind}_{B_{2}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\left(z^{-b_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\bar{z}^{-c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\ \otimes\ z^{-b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\bar{z}^{-c_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}}\right).

Recall,
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})=B_{2}(\mathbb{C}){\rm SU}(2)
with
T_{c}^{(1)}:=B_{2}(\mathbb{C})\cap{\rm SU}(2)\approx{\rm SU}(1)\approx{\bf S}^{1}.
Let us write
e^{i\theta}
for an element of
{\bf S}^{1}
which is the element

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e^{i\theta}&0\\
0&e^{-i\theta}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in
T_{c}^{(1)}
.
If
(\tau_{k},V_{k})
denotes the irreducible representation of
{\rm SU}(2)
of dimension
k
, and
\chi_{2m}(e^{i\theta})=e^{i(2m)\theta},

then

(4.1)

\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\ =\ {\rm Ind}_{T_{c}^{(1)}}^{{\rm SU}(2)}\left(\chi_{2m}\right)\ \approx\ \tau_{2m+1}\oplus\tau_{2m+3}\oplus\cdots\oplus\tau_{2m+2k+1}\oplus\cdots,

since by Frobenius reciprocity, any irreducible representation of
{\rm SU}(2)
that appears in
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}
has to contain the character
\chi_{2m}
with multiplicity one. Note that
\tau_{2m+1}
is the minimal
K
-type in
the induced representation
\mathbb{J}_{\mu};
we denote
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}(\tau_{2m+1})
for this minimal
K
-type as it sits inside the ambient
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}
.
Let’ us next describe
(\rho_{\mu},\mathcal{M}_{\mu})
restricted to
{\rm SU}(2).
We have

\mathcal{M}_{(b_{1},b_{2})}=\text{Sym}^{b_{1}-b_{2}}(\mathbb{C}^{2})\otimes\det^{b_{2}}
as a representation of
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),

where
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the standard representation. Hence,

\rho_{(b_{1},b_{2})}|_{{\rm SU}(2)}=\tau_{m}.
Similarly,
\rho_{(c_{1},c_{2})}|_{{\rm SU}(2)}=\tau_{m}.
If
g\in{\rm SU}(2)
then
\bar{g}={}^{t}g^{-1}
, hence

\bar{\tau}_{m}=\tau_{m}^{\vee}=\tau_{m}.
This implies that
\rho_{\mu}|_{{\rm SU}(2)}=\tau_{m}\otimes\tau_{m}.
Recall Clebsch-Gordon for
{\rm SU}(2)
: for
p\geq q\geq 1
we have

(4.2)

\tau_{p}\otimes\tau_{q}\ =\ \tau_{p-q+1}\oplus\tau_{p-q+3}\oplus\cdots\oplus\tau_{p+q-1}.

Applying this to
p=q=m
we get:

(4.3)

\rho_{\mu}|_{{\rm SU}(2)}\ =\ \tau_{1}\oplus\tau_{3}\oplus\cdots\oplus\tau_{2m-1}.

Denote
\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\tau_{2m-1})
for the copy of
\tau_{2m-1}
as it sits inside
\mathcal{M}_{\mu}.

Let
\mathfrak{g}_{2}:=\mathfrak{gl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2}:=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathfrak{u}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
be the Lie algebras of the connected real Lie groups
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
Z_{2}(\mathbb{C}){\rm U}(2),
respectively.
Then the Adjoint-action of
{\rm SU}(2)
on
\mathfrak{g}_{2}/\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2}
is irreducible whose complexification is isomorphic to
\tau_{3}.
Furthermore, we have:

\wedge^{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{2}/\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2})\approx\wedge^{3}(\mathfrak{g}_{2}/\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2})\approx\tau_{1}
and

\wedge^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2}/\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2})\approx\wedge^{2}(\mathfrak{g}_{2}/\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2})\approx\tau_{3}.

We can now describe the complex
{\rm Hom}_{{\rm SU}(2)}(\wedge^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}_{2}/\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2}),\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu}).
Apply
(
4.1
) and (
4.3
) to
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu}
and then apply (
4.2
) to see that
the smallest
p
for which
\tau_{p}
can occur in
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu}
is
p=3
and this is realized exactly once as:

\tau_{3}\ \hookrightarrow\ \tau_{2m+1}\otimes\tau_{2m-1}\ =\ \mathbb{J}_{\mu}(\tau_{2m+1})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\tau_{2m-1}).

Hence,
{\rm Hom}_{{\rm SU}(2)}(\wedge^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}_{2}/\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2}),\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\neq 0\iff\bullet=1,2,
and is one-dimensional in these degrees.
Knowing that the differentials for this complex are zero, we deduce that
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu}
has nonvanishing
(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2})
-cohomology
only in degrees
1
and
2
and the cohomology group is one-dimensional in these degrees.
Fix a basis
[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]
for:

(4.4)

H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ =\ H^{1}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),Z_{2}(\mathbb{C}){\rm U}(2);\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ =\ \mathbb{C}[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}].

Now, we express
[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]\in{\rm Hom}_{Z_{2}(\mathbb{C}){\rm U}(2)}(1\!\!1,\wedge^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2}/\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{J}_{\mu}(\tau_{2m+1})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\tau_{2m-1})),
as

[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]\ =\ \sum_{i,\alpha}X_{i}^{*}\otimes\phi_{i,\alpha}\otimes m_{\alpha},

where
\{X_{i}^{*}\}
is a basis for
(\mathfrak{g}_{2}/\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2})^{*}
, and
\{m_{\alpha}\}
is a basis for
\mathcal{M}_{\mu}.
(Of course, if
m_{\alpha}\notin\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\tau_{2m-1})
then
\phi_{i,\alpha}=0.
) We call the finite set
\{\phi_{i,\alpha}\}
of vectors in
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}
as

cohomological vectors
. Since
H^{1}
has dimension one, a scaling of the basis element
[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]
means jointly scaling this
finite set of cohomological vectors. Furthermore,
we contend, via an explicit version of Clebsch–Gordon, that one of
the
\phi_{i,\alpha}
is a highest weight vector of the lowest
K
-type
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}(\tau_{2m+1}).
Call this particular vector
as the
distinguished cohomological vector
for a given choice of
[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]
.

4.1.2.
The highest weight vector of the lowest
K
-type in
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}

We can explicitly describe such a vector
f_{\mu}
; first of all, since
f_{\mu}
is in the induced representation
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}
we have

(4.5)

f_{\mu}(\begin{pmatrix}z&*\\
&w\end{pmatrix}g)\ =\ z^{-b_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\bar{z}^{-c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\cdot w^{-b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\bar{w}^{-c_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}}\cdot\left|\frac{z}{w}\right|_{\mathbb{C}}^{1/2}f_{\mu}(g),

for all
g\in{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
z,w\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}.
Next, we note:

(4.6)

f_{\mu}(\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\alpha}&\\
&e^{-i\alpha}\end{pmatrix}g\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\beta}&\\
&e^{-i\beta}\end{pmatrix})=\ e^{i(2m)\alpha}e^{i(2m)\beta}f_{\mu}(g),

for all
g\in{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. The left-equivariance under
T_{c}^{(1)}
is by (
4.5
), and the right-equivariance
under
T_{c}^{(1)}
is because of being the highest weight vector in
\tau_{2m+1}
. Finally,
f_{\mu}
is completely determined
by its values on
{\rm SU}(2)
, for which, observe that

{\rm SU}(2)=T_{c}^{(1)}\cdot{\rm SO}(2)\cdot T_{c}^{(1)}.

For the values of
f_{\mu}
on
{\rm SO}(2)
, recall that the weight-vectors of
\tau_{2m+1}
maybe enumerated as
\{f_{-2m},f_{-2m+2},\dots,f_{2m-2},f_{2m}\}
where

T_{c}^{(1)}
acts on
f_{k}
via the character
e^{i\beta}\mapsto e^{ik\beta}.
So our
f_{\mu}
is
f_{2m}
up to a scalar multiple.
Let
\mathfrak{r}(\theta)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\cos(\theta)&-\sin(\theta)\\
\sin(\theta)&\cos(\theta)\end{smallmatrix}\right)
; then the weight
vectors
\{f_{-2m},f_{-2m+2},\dots,f_{2m-2}\}
may be normalized so that:

(4.7)

\mathfrak{r}(\theta)\cdot f_{2m}\ =\ \cos^{2m}(\theta)f_{2m}\ +\ \cos^{2m-2}(\theta)\sin^{2}(\theta)f_{2m-2}\ +\ \cdots\ +\ \sin^{2m}(\theta)f_{-2m}.

(Think of the model for
\tau_{2m+1}
consisting of homogeneous polynomials of degree
2m
in two variables.)
Using the analogue of (
4.6
) for the other weight vectors we see that
f_{k}(I)=0
if
k\neq 2m
. (Here
I
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix.)
Evaluating (
4.7
) on
I
we get:

(4.8)

f_{\mu}(\mathfrak{r}(\theta))\ =\ \cos^{2m}(\theta),

where, we have normalized
f_{\mu}
by
f_{\mu}(I)=1.
Putting (
4.5
), (
4.6
) and (
4.8
) together we can write:

(4.9)

f_{\mu}(\begin{pmatrix}z&*\\
&w\end{pmatrix}\mathfrak{r}(\theta)\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\beta}&\\
&e^{-i\beta}\end{pmatrix})\ =\ z^{-b_{2}+1}\,\bar{z}^{-c_{1}}\cdot w^{-b_{1}-1}\bar{w}^{-c_{2}}\cdot\cos^{2m}(\theta)\cdot e^{i(2m)\beta}.

4.1.3.
The cohomology class
[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]_{0}

The compact Lie group
{\rm SO}(2)
is the real points of an algebraic group defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, whose
\mathbb{Q}
-points we denote
{\rm SO}(2)(\mathbb{Q})
; this consists
of all those
\mathfrak{r}(\theta)
such that
\cos(\theta),\,\sin(\theta)\in\mathbb{Q}
.
We will scale the cohomology class
[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]
, such that the distinguished cohomological vector is rational, i.e., takes rational values on
{\rm SO}(2)(\mathbb{Q});
we denote
this class by
[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]_{0}
. Observe that
[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]_{0}
is well-defined only up to homothety by
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}.
By (
4.9
), we see
that some
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
-multiple of
f_{\mu}
is a distinguished cohomological vector for
[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]_{0}.

4.1.4.
The intertwining operator
T_{\rm st}

Consider the induced representation

\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\ =\ {\rm Ind}_{B_{2}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\left(z^{-b_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\bar{z}^{-c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\ \otimes\ z^{-b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\bar{z}^{-c_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}}\right)\ \ {\rm as}\ \ {\rm Ind}_{B_{2}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi_{1}(\mu)\otimes\chi_{2}(\mu)),

where
\chi_{1}(\mu)(z)=z^{-b_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\bar{z}^{-c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}
and
\chi_{2}(\mu)(z)=z^{-b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\bar{z}^{-c_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}}.

The standard intertwining operator from
\mathbb{J}_{\mu}
to its ‘companion’ induced representation

T_{\rm st}:{\rm Ind}_{B_{2}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi_{1}(\mu)\otimes\chi_{2}(\mu))\ \longrightarrow\ {\rm Ind}_{B_{2}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi_{2}(\mu)\otimes\chi_{1}(\mu))

is given by the integral:

(4.10)

T_{\rm st}(f)(g)\ =\ \int_{\mathbb{C}}f\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}g\right)\,du

where
du
is the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{C}
; if
u=x+\mathfrak{i}y
then
du=dx\,dy.

Proposition 4.11
.

Suppose
s=-1
and
s=0
are regular points for both
L(s,\chi_{1}(\mu)\chi_{2}(\mu)^{-1})
and
L(1-s,\chi_{1}(\mu)^{-1}\chi_{2}(\mu)).
Then,
the representation
{\rm Ind}_{B_{2}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi_{1}(\mu)\otimes\chi_{2}(\mu))
is irreducible, and the standard intertwining operator
T_{\rm st}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Irreducibility follows from
[
20
, Chap. 2, Thm. 3]
. The proof of
T_{\rm st}
being an isomorphism follows the same argument as in the proof of
[
26
, Prop. 7.54]
. We will elaborate further when we deal with
{\rm GL}_{N}
; see Prop.
4.27
below.
∎

4.1.5.
The highest weight vector of the lowest
K
-type on the ‘other side’

Since
T_{\rm st}
is an isomorphism of
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-modules, it maps the minimal
{\rm SU}(2)
-type in
{\rm Ind}(\chi_{1}(\mu)\otimes\chi_{2}(\mu))

isomorphically onto the minimal
{\rm SU}(2)
-type in
{\rm Ind}(\chi_{2}(\mu)\otimes\chi_{1}(\mu))
, and within these
{\rm SU}(2)
-types, it maps
f_{\mu}
, which is the highest weight vector for
T_{c}^{(1)}
described above
to a multiple of the highest weight vector on the other side, which we denote
\tilde{f_{\mu}}
. We have the analogues of
(
4.5
) and (
4.6
) for
\tilde{f_{\mu}}
:

(4.12)

\tilde{f}_{\mu}(\begin{pmatrix}z&*\\
&w\end{pmatrix}g)\ =\ z^{-b_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\bar{z}^{-c_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}}\cdot w^{-b_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\bar{w}^{-c_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}}\cdot\left|\frac{z}{w}\right|_{\mathbb{C}}^{1/2}\tilde{f}_{\mu}(g),

(4.13)

\tilde{f}_{\mu}(\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\alpha}&\\
&e^{-i\alpha}\end{pmatrix}g\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\beta}&\\
&e^{-i\beta}\end{pmatrix})=\ e^{i(-2m)\alpha}e^{i(2m)\beta}\tilde{f}_{\mu}(g).

for all
g\in{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}).

But, (
4.13
) also says that
\tilde{f}_{\mu}(I)=0
(since
m\geq 1
). Let

w_{0}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\ =\ \mathfrak{r}(-\pi/2).

Then, using (
4.7
), and evaluating at
w_{0}
we see:
(\mathfrak{r}(\theta)\cdot\tilde{f}_{\mu})(w_{0})=\cos^{2m}(\theta)\cdot\tilde{f}_{\mu}(w_{0}).
(The other summands vanish on
w_{0}
using the analogue
of (
4.13
).) Hence

\tilde{f}_{\mu}(\mathfrak{r}(\theta-\pi/2))\ =\ \tilde{f}_{\mu}(w_{0}\mathfrak{r}(\theta))\ =\ \cos^{2m}(\theta)\cdot\tilde{f}_{\mu}(w_{0}).

Change
\theta\mapsto\theta+\pi/2
and noting
\cos(\theta+\pi/2)=-\sin(\theta)
we get the analogue of (
4.8
):

(4.14)

\tilde{f}_{\mu}(\mathfrak{r}(\theta))\ =\ \sin^{2m}(\theta),

where, we have normalized
\tilde{f}_{\mu}
by
\tilde{f}_{\mu}(w_{0})=1.
From (
4.12
), (
4.13
) and (
4.14
), we have:

(4.15)

\tilde{f}_{\mu}(\begin{pmatrix}z&*\\
&w\end{pmatrix}\mathfrak{r}(\theta)\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\beta}&\\
&e^{-i\beta}\end{pmatrix})\ =\ z^{-b_{1}}\,\bar{z}^{-c_{2}+1}\cdot w^{-b_{2}}\bar{w}^{-c_{1}-1}\cdot\sin^{2m}(\theta)\cdot e^{i(2m)\beta}.

4.1.6.
The basic intertwining calculation for
{\rm GL}_{2}

Proposition 4.16
.

T_{\rm st}(f_{\mu})\ \approx_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}\ \frac{L(0,\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1})}{L(1,\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1})}\,\tilde{f}_{\mu},

where,
\approx_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}
means equality up to a nonzero rational number.

Proof.

It is clear that
T_{\rm st}(f_{\mu})
is a scalar multiple of
\tilde{f}_{\mu}.
To compute that scalar we evaluate
T_{\rm st}(f_{\mu})
at
w_{0}
:

T_{\rm st}(f_{\mu})(w_{0})\ =\ \int_{\mathbb{C}}f_{\mu}\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&u\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\right)\,du\ =\ \int_{\mathbb{C}}f_{\mu}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-u&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\,du.

Change to polar coordinates:
u=re^{i\theta}.
Note that

\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-re^{i\theta}&1\end{pmatrix}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}e^{-i\theta/2}&0\\
0&e^{i\theta/2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-r&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\theta/2}&0\\
0&e^{-i\theta/2}\end{pmatrix}.

Hence, applying (
4.5
) and (
4.6
) we get

f_{\mu}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-re^{i\theta}&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\ =\ e^{-i(2m)\theta/2}f_{\mu}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-r&1\end{pmatrix}\right)e^{i(2m)\theta/2}\ =\ f_{\mu}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-r&1\end{pmatrix}\right).

Next, we note

\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-r&1\end{pmatrix}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{r}^{-1}&-r\Delta_{r}^{-1}\\
0&\Delta_{r}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{r}^{-1}&r\Delta_{r}^{-1}\\
-r\Delta_{r}^{-1}&\Delta_{r}^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

where
\Delta_{r}=\sqrt{1+r^{2}}.
Note that
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Delta_{r}^{-1}&r\Delta_{r}^{-1}\\
-r\Delta_{r}^{-1}&\Delta_{r}^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\mathfrak{r}(\alpha)
with
\alpha=\tan^{-1}(-r).

From (
4.5
) and (
4.8
) we get

f_{\mu}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
