-r&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\ =\ \frac{1}{\Delta_{r}^{2m+1}}.

The integral evaluates to:

(4.17)

T_{\rm st}(f_{\mu})(w_{0})\ =\ \int_{\theta=0}^{2\pi}\int_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{r\,dr\,d\theta}{(\sqrt{1+r^{2}})^{2m+1}}\ =\ \frac{2\pi}{2m-1}.

Now,
\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1}(z)=z^{m}\bar{z}^{-m}
, and by (
3.3
) we have

L(s,\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1})=2(2\pi)^{-(s+m)}\Gamma(s+m).
Hence,

(4.18)

\frac{L(0,\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1})}{L(1,\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1})}\ =\ \frac{(2\pi)^{-m}}{(2\pi)^{-1-m}}\frac{\Gamma(m)}{\Gamma(m+1)}\ =\ \frac{2\pi}{m}.

The proof follows from (
4.17
) and (
4.18
).
∎

4.1.7.
Arithmetic interpretation of the intertwining calculation

Denote the induced representation in the range of
T_{\rm st}
as
\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}={\rm Ind}(\chi_{2}(\mu)\otimes\chi_{1}(\mu))

Now, fix a cohomology class
[\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}]_{0}
:

(4.19)

H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ =\ H^{1}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),Z_{2}(\mathbb{C}){\rm U}(2);\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ =\ \mathbb{C}[\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}]_{0},

characterised by the property that its distingusihed cohomological vector is rational; hence, up to
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
-multiples, the vector

\tilde{f}_{\mu}
is a cohomological vector for
\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}.

Consider the map induced in cohomology by the operator
T_{\rm st}:\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\to\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}
; at the level of generators it will map

[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]=\sum_{i,\alpha}X_{i}^{*}\otimes\phi_{i,\alpha}\otimes m_{\alpha}
to

\sum_{i,\alpha}X_{i}^{*}\otimes T_{\rm st}(\phi_{i,\alpha})\otimes m_{\alpha}.
Then, in terms of the cohomology classes with rational
distinguished cohomological vectors, Prop.
4.16
may be stated as:

Proposition 4.20
.

T_{\rm st}([\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]_{0})\ \approx_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}\ \frac{L(0,\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1})}{L(1,\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1})}\,[\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}]_{0},

4.1.8.
Rational classes via Delorme’s Lemma

Recall Delorme’s Lemma (see Borel–Wallach
[
5
, Thm. III.3.3]
) which in the current context can be explicated as:

(4.21)

H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ \simeq\\
H^{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{k}_{1};z^{-b_{2}+1}\bar{z}^{-c_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{(b_{2}-1)(c_{1})})\otimes H^{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{k}_{1};z^{-b_{1}-1}\bar{z}^{-c_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{(b_{1}+1)(c_{2})}),

where
\mathfrak{g}_{1}=\mathfrak{gl}_{1}(\mathbb{C})
,
\mathfrak{k}_{1}=\mathfrak{su}(1),
for
b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
we abbreviate the character
z\mapsto z^{b}\bar{z}^{c}
simply as

z^{b}\bar{z}^{c}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{(b)(c)}
is the algebraic representation
z^{b}\bar{z}^{c}
of the real group
\mathbb{C}^{\times}.
Note
that on the right hand side, in each factor, we are looking at the relative Lie algebra cohomology for
{\rm GL}_{1}(\mathbb{C})
of

z^{-b}\bar{z}^{-c}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{(b)(c)},
which is nothing but the trivial character! For brevity, denote

H^{0}_{b,c}=H^{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{k}_{1};z^{-b}\bar{z}^{-c}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{(b)(c)}).

Parse the isomorphism in Delorme’s Lemma: the map
f\mapsto f(1_{2})
for
f\in\mathbb{J}_{\mu}
induces an isomorphism coming from Frobenius reciprocity:

H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}(z^{-b_{2}+1}\bar{z}^{-c_{1}}\otimes z^{-b_{1}-1}\bar{z}^{-c_{2}})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ \simeq\\
H^{1}(\mathfrak{b}_{2},\mathfrak{k}_{B_{2}};(z^{-b_{2}+1}\bar{z}^{-c_{1}}\otimes z^{-b_{1}-1}\bar{z}^{-c_{2}})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu}),

where
\mathfrak{b}_{2}
(resp.,
\mathfrak{k}_{B_{2}}
)
is the real Lie algebra of
B_{2}(\mathbb{C})
(resp.,
{\rm U}(2)\cap B_{2}(\mathbb{C})
).
The proof of
[
5
, Thm. III.3.3]
gives that

H^{1}(\mathfrak{b}_{2},\mathfrak{k}_{B};(z^{-b_{2}+1}\bar{z}^{-c_{1}}\otimes z^{-b_{1}-1}\bar{z}^{-c_{2}})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ \simeq\\
H^{0}(\mathfrak{t}_{2},\mathfrak{k}_{T_{2}};(z^{-b_{2}+1}\bar{z}^{-c_{1}}\otimes z^{-b_{1}-1}\bar{z}^{-c_{2}})\otimes H^{1}(\mathfrak{u}_{B_{2}},\mathcal{M}_{\mu})),

where
\mathfrak{t}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{k}_{T_{2}}
, and
\mathfrak{u}_{B_{2}}
are the real Lie algebras of the diagonal torus
T_{2}(\mathbb{C})
in
B_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, its maximal compact
{\rm U}(2)\cap T_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
and the unipotent radical of
B_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, respectively.
To apply Kostant’s theorem (
2.24
) we need the Kostant representatives of length
1
for the Borel subgroup in the
real reductive group
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C});
if
w_{0}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
then the required Kostant representatives are

w_{l}=(w_{0},1)
and
w_{r}=(1,w_{0}).
By direct calculation we have:

w_{l}\cdot\mu\ =\ (w_{0},1)\cdot((b_{1},b_{2}),(c_{1},c_{2}))\ =\ ((b_{2}-1,b_{1}+1)(c_{1},c_{2})).

Hence,
\mathcal{M}_{w_{l}\cdot\mu},
as an algebraic irreducible representation for the diagonal torus in
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),
is

\mathcal{M}_{(b_{2}-1)(c_{1})}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{(b_{1}+1)(c_{2})},
giving us (
4.21
) that we rewrite as:

(4.22)

\gamma_{1}:H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\approx}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ H^{0}_{(b_{2}-1,c_{1})}\otimes H^{0}_{(b_{1}+1,c_{2})}.

Fix a basis
\omega_{(b,c)}
for
H^{0}_{(b,c)}
which is the rational class corresponding to the cohomology of the trivial representation.
We take for
[\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]_{0}
, the basis element
H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu}),
such that

\gamma_{1}([\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]_{0})=\omega_{(b_{2}-1,c_{1})}\otimes\omega_{(b_{1}+1,c_{2})}.

Now, we work with the cohomology class for the induced module
\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}
in the codomain of
T_{\rm st}.
Here, the integral in
(
4.10
) tells us to consider Frobenius reciprocity via the map
\tilde{f}\mapsto\tilde{f}(w_{0}),
which
induces an isomorphism:

H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}(z^{-b_{1}}\bar{z}^{-c_{2}+1}\otimes z^{-b_{2}}\bar{z}^{-c_{1}-1})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ \simeq\\
H^{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{b}}_{2},\mathfrak{k}_{\bar{B}};(z^{-b_{1}}\bar{z}^{-c_{2}+1}\otimes z^{-b_{2}}\bar{z}^{-c_{1}-1})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu}),

where
\bar{B}
is the Borel subgroup of
{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of lower triangular matrices that is opposite to
B_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. In this situation we
use the Kostant representative
w_{r}=(1,w_{0})
to give ourselves the isomosphism:

(4.23)

\gamma_{w_{0}}:H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\approx}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ H^{0}_{(b_{1},c_{2}-1)}\otimes H^{0}_{(b_{2},c_{1}+1)}.

We take for
[\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}]_{0}
, the basis element
H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu}),
such that

\gamma_{w_{0}}([\mathbb{J}_{\mu}]_{0})=\omega_{(b_{1},c_{2}-1)}\otimes\omega_{(b_{2},c_{1}+1)}.
It helps to keep the following diagram in mind:

\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{\rm st}^{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{1}}
\textstyle{H^{0}_{(b_{2}-1,c_{1})}\otimes H^{0}_{(b_{1}+1,c_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{2},\tilde{\mathfrak{k}}_{2};\tilde{\mathbb{J}}_{\mu}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\mu})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{w_{0}}}
\textstyle{H^{0}_{(b_{1},c_{2}-1)}\otimes H^{0}_{(b_{2},c_{1}+1)}}

The diagram is not commutative! Prop.
4.20
says that it is commutative up to nonzero rational numbers and a particular
ratio of archimedean
L
-values. The reader is referred to
[
26
, Sect. 9.6]
.

4.2.
The case of
{\rm GL}_{N}

Now, we generalize Prop.
4.20
to the case of
{\rm GL}_{N},
giving us the main result of this subsection in
Prop.
4.31
.

4.2.1.
The induced representations and the standard intertwining operator

Take strongly-pure weights
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
as in Sect.
3
.
Fix an archimedean place
v
(which we often drop simply to avoid tedious notation). Consider the induced representation

\sigma_{v}\ =\ \mathbb{J}_{\mu}\ =\ {\rm Ind}_{B_{n}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})}\left(z^{\alpha_{1}}\bar{z}^{\beta_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes z^{\alpha_{n}}\bar{z}^{\beta_{n}}\right),\quad\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in\tfrac{(n-1)}{2}+\mathbb{Z};

see (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) for the cuspidal parameters
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}.
Abbreviate this as:

\sigma_{v}\ =\ \mathbb{J}_{\mu}\ =\ \psi_{1}\times\cdots\times\psi_{n};\quad\psi_{i}(z)=z^{\alpha_{i}}\bar{z}^{\beta_{i}}.

Similarly, we have

\sigma^{\prime}_{v}\ =\ \mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}}\ =\ {\rm Ind}_{B_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C})}\left(z^{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}}\bar{z}^{\beta^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes z^{\alpha^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}}\bar{z}^{\beta^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}}\right),\quad\alpha^{\prime}_{j},\beta^{\prime}_{j}\in\tfrac{(n^{\prime}-1)}{2}+\mathbb{Z},

which we abbreviate as:

\sigma^{\prime}_{v}\ =\ \mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}}\ =\ \psi^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\psi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}};\quad\psi^{\prime}_{j}(z)=z^{\alpha^{\prime}_{j}}\bar{z}^{\beta^{\prime}_{j}}.

We are interested in the standard intertwining operator

T_{\rm st}\ :\ {}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P_{(n,n^{\prime})}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}(\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\times\mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}})\ \longrightarrow\ {}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P_{(n^{\prime},n)}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}(\mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}}(-n)\times\mathbb{J}_{\mu}(n^{\prime})).

which, in terms of normalized induced representations looks like:

(4.24)

T_{\rm st}\ :\ {\rm Ind}_{P_{(n,n^{\prime})}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}(\mathbb{J}_{\mu}(-n^{\prime}/2)\times\mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}}(n/2))\ \longrightarrow\ {\rm Ind}_{P_{(n^{\prime},n)}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}(\mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}}(n/2)\times\mathbb{J}_{\mu}(-n^{\prime}/2)).

Write

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{\mu}(-n^{\prime}/2)\

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ {\rm Ind}_{B_{n}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi_{n}),\quad\chi_{i}=\psi_{i}(-n^{\prime}/2),\quad{\rm and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}}(n/2)\

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ {\rm Ind}_{B_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}),\quad\chi^{\prime}_{j}=\psi^{\prime}_{j}(n/2).

Apply transitivity of normalized induction to the representation in the domain of (
4.24
) to get:

{\rm Ind}_{P_{(n,n^{\prime})}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}\left({\rm Ind}_{B_{n}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi_{n})\times{\rm Ind}_{B_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})\right)\ =\\
{\rm Ind}_{B_{N}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi_{n}\otimes\chi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})\ =:\ \chi_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi_{n}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}},

and, similarly, the induced representation in the target is:

\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\times\chi_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi_{n}.

Hence, (
4.24
) takes the shape:

(4.25)

T_{\rm st}\ :\ \chi_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi_{n}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\ \longrightarrow\ \chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\times\chi_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi_{n}.

For a function
f\in\chi_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi_{n}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
we have the intertwining integral

(4.26)

T_{\rm st}(f)(g)\ =\ \int_{M_{n\times n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C})}f\left(w_{0}\begin{pmatrix}1_{n}&u\\
&1_{n^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}g\right)\,du,

where
w_{0}
is the element of the Weyl group of
{\rm GL}_{N}
given by the following permutation:

w_{0}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}1&2&\dots&n-1&n&n+1&n+2&\dots&N-1&N\\
n^{\prime}+1&n^{\prime}+2&\dots&N-1&N&1&2&\dots&n^{\prime}-1&n^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},

and the measure
du
on
M_{n\times n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C})
in in the integral is taken as the product of the Lebesgue measures on each coordinate of
u
.

Proposition 4.27
.

Assume that the archimedean local factors
L(s,\mathbb{J}_{\mu}\times\mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}}^{\sf v})
and
L(1-s,\mathbb{J}_{\mu}^{\sf v}\times\mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}})
are finite at

s=-N/2
and
s=1-N/2.
Then

(1)

the representations
\chi_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi_{n}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
and

\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\times\chi_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi_{n}
are irreducible; and furtheremore,

(2)

the standard intertwining integral
T_{\rm st}
in (
4.26
) converges and gives an isomorphism between these two irreducible representations.

Proof.

The proof follows from the Langlands–Shahidi machinery. For brevity, only for this proof, let
\sigma=\mathbb{J}_{\mu}
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\mathbb{J}_{\mu^{\prime}}.
Let

I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})={\rm Ind}_{P_{(n,n^{\prime})}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}((\sigma\otimes|\ |^{\frac{n^{\prime}}{N}s})\otimes(\sigma^{\prime}\otimes|\ |^{\frac{-n}{N}s})).

The
s
-variable is introduced using the fundamental weight corresponding to the simple root that is deleted for the maximal standard parabolic subgroup
P_{(n,n^{\prime})}
whose Levi quotient is the
block diagonal subgroup
{\rm GL}_{n}\times{\rm GL}_{n^{\prime}}.
Similarly, we let

I_{Q}^{G}(-s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})={\rm Ind}_{P_{(n^{\prime},n)}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}((\sigma^{\prime}\otimes|\ |^{\frac{-n}{N}s})\otimes(\sigma\otimes|\ |^{\frac{n^{\prime}}{N}s})).

The standard intertwining operator
T_{\rm st}(s,w_{0}):I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\to I_{Q}^{G}(-s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})
is given by the integral (
4.26
).
Under the hypothesis of the proposition, it follows from Casselman–Shahidi
[
6
, Prop. 5.3]
that the induced representations

I_{P}^{G}(-N/2,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})=\chi_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi_{n}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
and

I_{Q}^{G}(N/2,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})=\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\times\chi_{1}\times\cdots\times\chi_{n}

are irreducible. The operator
T_{\rm st}=T_{\rm st}(-N/2,w_{0})
being an isomorphism follows exactly as in the proof of

[
26
, Prop. 7.54]
; this part of the proof uses Shahidi’s
results on local constants
[
43
]
.
∎

4.2.2.
Factorizing the intertwining operator

For
1\leq i\leq N-1
, let
{\sf s}_{i}=(i,i+1)
be the
i
-th simple reflection corresponding to the
i
-th simple root
\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}.
Its easy to see that
a positive root
e_{i}-e_{j}
(positivity is
i<j
) is mapped to a negative root by
w_{0}
if and only if
1\leq i\leq n
and
n+1\leq j\leq N,
hence

l(w_{0})=nn^{\prime}.

Furthermore, its easy to see that:

w_{0}\ =\ ({\sf s}_{n^{\prime}}\dots{\sf s}_{2}{\sf s}_{1})\cdots({\sf s}_{N-2}\dots{\sf s}_{n}{\sf s}_{n-1})({\sf s}_{N-1}\dots{\sf s}_{n+1}{\sf s}_{n}),

where the right hand side is grouped into
n
parenthetical expressions each of which is a product of
n^{\prime}
simple reflections; hence
giving a minimal expression of
w_{0}
in terms of
l(w_{0})
many simple reflections. This gives a factorization:

(4.28)

T_{\rm st}=T_{\rm st}(w_{0})\ =\\
\left(T({\sf s}_{n^{\prime}})\circ\cdots\circ T({\sf s}_{2})\circ T({\sf s}_{1})\right)\circ\cdots\circ\left(T({\sf s}_{N-1})\circ\cdots\circ T({\sf s}_{n+1})\circ T({\sf s}_{n})\right),

which is well-known in the Langlands–Shahidi method; see, for example,
[
44
, Thm. 4.2.2]
as applied to our situation.

Example 4.29
.

To visualise such a factorisation, consider the simple but nontrivial example: take
n=3
and
n^{\prime}=2
, then
the right hand side of (
4.28
) is the sequence of operators:

(1)

T({\sf s}_{3})\ :\ \chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi_{3}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\ \longrightarrow\ \chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi_{3}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}

(2)

T({\sf s}_{4})\ :\ \chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi_{3}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\ \longrightarrow\ \chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\times\chi_{3}

(3)

T({\sf s}_{2})\ :\ \chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\times\chi_{3}\ \longrightarrow\ \chi_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\times\chi_{3}

(4)

T({\sf s}_{3})\ :\ \chi_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\times\chi_{3}\ \longrightarrow\ \chi_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi_{3}

(5)

T({\sf s}_{1})\ :\ \chi_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi_{3}\ \longrightarrow\ \chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi_{3}

(6)

T({\sf s}_{2})\ :\ \chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi_{3}\ \longrightarrow\ \chi^{\prime}_{1}\times\chi^{\prime}_{2}\times\chi_{1}\times\chi_{2}\times\chi_{3}.

The point is that at every intermediate stage, there are only two characters
\chi_{i}
and
\chi^{\prime}_{j}
that are getting switched. The corresponding integral is happening over
the coordinate
u_{ij}
in the variable
u\in M_{n\times n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C})
that appears in (
4.26
). The measure
du
, as mentioned above, is the product of the Lebesgue measures

du_{ij}.
Such an intermediate integral is the induction to
{\rm GL}_{N}
of a
{\rm GL}_{2}
-intertwining integral, and we have seen that it corresponds to scaling by a factor

L_{\infty}(0,\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime-1})/L_{\infty}(1,\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime-1})
(up to a nonzero rational). This implies that
T_{\rm st}
will have a scaling factor of the product of all
intermediate scaling factors, towards which note the easy lemma:

Lemma 4.30
.

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\frac{L_{\infty}(0,\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime-1})}{L_{\infty}(1,\chi_{i}\chi_{j}^{\prime-1})}\ =\ \prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\frac{L_{\infty}(-\tfrac{N}{2},\psi_{i}\psi_{j}^{\prime-1})}{L_{\infty}(1-\tfrac{N}{2},\psi_{i}\psi_{j}^{\prime-1})}\ =\ \frac{L_{\infty}(-\tfrac{N}{2},\sigma_{\infty}\times\sigma_{\infty}^{\prime\sf v})}{L_{\infty}(1-\tfrac{N}{2},\sigma_{\infty}\times\sigma_{\infty}^{\prime\sf v})}.

4.2.3.
The intertwining operator in cohomology

Let
\mathscr{J}=\mathscr{J}^{0}
stand for the underlying
(\mathfrak{g}_{N},\mathfrak{k}_{N})
-module of

{\rm Ind}_{B_{N}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi_{n}\otimes\chi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})

and
similarly,
\tilde{\mathscr{J}}=\mathscr{J}^{nn^{\prime}}
that of

{\rm Ind}_{B_{N}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\otimes\chi_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi_{n}).

Rewrite the factorization in (
4.28
) as:

T_{\rm st}=T^{nn^{\prime}}\circ\cdots\circ T^{2}\circ T^{1},\quad T^{k}:\mathscr{J}^{k-1}\to\mathscr{J}^{k}\ \mbox{for $1\leq k\leq nn^{\prime}.$}

with each
\mathscr{J}^{k}
being an irreducible principal series representation, and each
T^{k}
is the induction of a
{\rm GL}_{2}
-intertwining operator as explained.
Note that

{\rm Ind}_{B_{N}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}(\chi_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi_{n}\otimes\chi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}})={}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{B_{N}(\mathbb{C})}^{{\rm GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})}(\xi_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\xi_{n}\otimes\xi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\xi^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}),

where

\xi_{i}=\chi_{i}\left(\tfrac{N-2i+1}{2}\right)=\psi_{i}\left(\tfrac{n-2i+1}{2}\right)
and

\xi^{\prime}_{j}=\chi^{\prime}_{j}\left(\tfrac{N-2j-2n+1}{2}\right)=\psi^{\prime}_{j}\left(\tfrac{n^{\prime}-2j+1}{2}\right),

are all
algebraic
characters of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Similarly, each
\mathscr{J}^{k}
is the algebraic parabolic induction of an algebraic character of the diagonal torus.
Delorme’s lemma identifies the one-dimensional cohomology group

H^{b_{N}^{\mathbb{C}}}(\mathfrak{g}_{N},\mathfrak{k}_{N};\mathscr{J}^{k}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\lambda})
as a tensor product of the
{\rm GL}_{1}
cohomology groups for the
\xi_{i}
’s and
\xi_{j}^{\prime}
’s;
as in (
4.22
), but simplifying notations we have:

\gamma_{k}:H^{b_{N}^{\mathbb{C}}}(\mathfrak{g}_{N},\mathfrak{k}_{N};\mathscr{J}^{k}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\lambda})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\approx}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \mbox{(product of ${\rm GL}_{1}$ cohomology groups)}.

This product of
{\rm GL}_{1}
-cohomology groups may be identified with each other for
1\leq k\leq nn^{\prime}.

Fixing a rational basis
\omega_{(b,c)}
for
each of the
{\rm GL}_{1}
-classes and so for their tensor product, we define a basis element
[\mathscr{J}^{k}]_{0}
for

H^{b_{N}^{\mathbb{C}}}(\mathfrak{g}_{N},\mathfrak{k}_{N};\mathscr{J}^{k}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\lambda})
via
\gamma_{k}^{-1}.

We start with
T^{1}:\mathscr{J}^{0}\to\mathscr{J}^{1}
and note that this is the induction from
(n-1,2,n^{\prime}-1)
-parabolic subgroup of
{\rm GL}_{N}
of the
{\rm GL}_{2}
-intertwining operator that switches
\chi_{n}
and
\chi^{\prime}_{1}
. Prop.
4.20
applied to
T^{1}
gives:

(T^{1})^{\bullet}([\mathscr{J}^{0}]_{0})\ \approx_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}\ \frac{L(0,\chi_{n}\chi^{\prime}_{1}{}^{-1})}{L(1,\chi_{n}\chi^{\prime}_{1}{}^{-1})}[\mathscr{J}^{1}]_{0}.

At the next step, from the factorisation in (
4.28
), we will get:

(T^{2})^{\bullet}([\mathscr{J}^{1}]_{0})\ \approx_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}\ \frac{L(0,\chi_{n}\chi^{\prime}_{2}{}^{-1})}{L(1,\chi_{n}\chi^{\prime}_{2}{}^{-1})}[\mathscr{J}^{2}]_{0},

and so on. Using Lem.
4.30
, Prop.
4.20
generalizes to the following

Proposition 4.31
.

T_{\rm st}^{\bullet}([\mathscr{J}]_{0})\ \approx_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}\ \frac{L_{\infty}(-\tfrac{N}{2},\sigma_{\infty}\times\sigma_{\infty}^{\prime\sf v})}{L_{\infty}(1-\tfrac{N}{2},\sigma_{\infty}\times\sigma_{\infty}^{\prime\sf v})}\,[\tilde{\mathscr{J}}]_{0}.

The reader is referred to Harder
[
24
]
where a hope is expressed in general, and verified in the context therein, that the rational number implicit in
\approx_{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}
has a simple
shape. See (
4.17
) and (
4.18
) above in the simplest possible case of
n=n^{\prime}=1.

5.
The main theorem on special values of
L
-functions for
{\rm GL}_{n}\times{\rm GL}_{n^{\prime}}

Before the main theorem on
L
-values (Thm.
5.16
) can be stated and proved,
two technical results on the boundary cohomology are necessary; the
first is what is known as a ‘Manin–Drinfeld’ principle and the second is on rank-one Eisenstein cohomology.

5.1.
A Manin–Drinfeld Principle

The main purpose of this subsection is to state and prove Thm.
5.5
.

5.1.1.
Kostant representatives

To begin, two important lemmas about Kostant representatives from
[
26
, Sect. 5.3.2]
are recorded below.
Recall that
P=\mathrm{Res}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(P_{0})
and
P_{0}=P_{(n,n^{\prime})}
is the maximal parabolic subgroup of type
(n,n^{\prime})
of
G_{0}={\rm GL}_{N}/F.
Let
Q_{0}=P_{(n^{\prime},n)}
be the associate parabolic, and
Q=\mathrm{Res}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(Q_{0})
.
Let
\mathbf{\Pi}_{M_{P_{0}}}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{G_{0}}-\{\alpha_{P_{0}}\}
.
Let
w_{P_{0}}
be the unique element of
W_{0}=W_{G_{0}}
such that
w_{P_{0}}(\mathbf{\Pi}_{M_{P_{0}}})\subset\mathbf{\Pi}_{G_{0}}
and

w_{P_{0}}(\alpha_{P_{0}})<0,
it is the longest Kostant representative for
W^{P_{0}}.

Lemma 5.1
.

With notations as above, one has:

(1)

The map
w\mapsto w^{\prime}:=w_{P}\,w
gives a bijection
W^{P}\to W^{Q}
. If
w=(w^{\tau})_{\tau:F\to E}
, then by definition,

w_{P}w=(w_{P_{0}}w^{\tau})_{\tau:F\to E}.

(2)

This bijection has the property that
l(w^{\tau})+l(w^{\prime\tau})=\dim{(U_{P_{0}^{\tau}})}
.

(3)

w
is balanced if and only if
w^{\prime}
is balanced.

Similarly, there is the following self-bijection of
W^{P}
:

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
w_{G}
be the element of longest length in the Weyl group
W_{G}
of
G
, and similarly,
let
w_{M_{P}}
be the element of longest length in the Weyl group
W_{M_{P}}
.
Then:

(1)

The map
w\mapsto w^{\mathsf{v}}:=w_{M_{P}}\cdot w\cdot w_{G}
gives a bijection
W^{P}\to W^{P}
.

(2)

This bijection has the property that
l(w^{\tau})+l(w^{{\sf v}\tau})=\dim{(U_{P_{0}^{\tau}})}.

(3)

w
is balanced if and only if
w^{\sf v}
is balanced.

5.1.2.
Induced representations in boundary cohomology

The conditions imposed by the combinatorial lemma has a consequence on the occurrences of induced representations as Hecke summands in the boundary cohomology.
Recall that
E
is a large enough Galois extension of
\mathbb{Q}
that takes a copy of
F
. Consider strongly-pure weights

\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times E)
and

\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times E).
Let
\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu)
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime})
be strongly-inner Hecke-summands. The effect of the related
balanced representatives:
w^{\prime},w^{\sf v}
and
w^{\sf v}{}^{\prime}
on certain weights are recorded in the following

Proposition 5.3
.

Assume that the weights
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
satisfy the conditions of the combinatorial lemma. Hence there is a balanced element
w\in W^{P}
such that
\lambda:=w^{-1}\cdot(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
is dominant. Then (after recalling the notations in Sect.
2.4.1
):

(1)

w^{\prime}\cdot\lambda=(\mu^{\prime}-n\bm{\updelta}_{n^{\prime}})+(\mu+n^{\prime}\bm{\updelta}_{n}),

(2)

w^{\sf v}\cdot\lambda^{\sf v}=(\mu^{\sf v}-n^{\prime}\bm{\updelta}_{n})+(\mu^{\prime{\sf v}}+n\bm{\updelta}_{n^{\prime}}),
and

(3)

w^{\sf v}{}^{\prime}\cdot\lambda^{\sf v}=\mu^{\prime{\sf v}}+\mu^{\sf v}.

For a proof of the above proposition, the reader is referred to
[
26
, Sect. 5.3.4]
. The appearance of various induced modules in boundary cohomology in bottom and top degrees are recorded in the following

Proposition 5.4
.

Let the notations be as above.

(1)

The module
{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}(\sigma_{f}\times\sigma^{\prime}_{f})
appears in
H^{{\sf q}_{b}}(\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E}),

where

{\sf q}_{b}=b_{N}^{F}=b_{n}^{F}+b_{n^{\prime}}^{F}+\frac{1}{2}\dim(U_{P}).

(2)

The module
{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{Q(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n)\times\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))
appears in
H^{{\sf q}_{b}}(\partial_{Q}\mathcal{S}^{G},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E}).

The contragredient of the algebraically-induced modules is

{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}(\sigma_{f}\times\sigma^{\prime}_{f})^{\sf v}\ =\ {}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}(\sigma_{f}^{\sf v}(n^{\prime})\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f}(-n)).

Furthermore, for the contragredients and cohomology in top-degree we have:

(3)

{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}(\sigma_{f}^{\sf v}(n^{\prime})\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f}(-n))
appears in
H^{{\sf q}_{t}}(\partial_{P}\mathcal{S}^{G},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda^{\sf v},E}),

where
{\sf q}_{t}=t_{N}^{F}-1=t_{n}^{F}+t_{n^{\prime}}^{F}+\frac{1}{2}\dim(U_{P}).

(4)

{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{Q(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}(\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f}\times\sigma_{f}^{\sf v})
appears in
H^{{\sf q}_{t}}(\partial_{Q}\mathcal{S}^{G},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda^{\sf v},E}).

Proof.

For (1), use the summand in Prop.
2.25
indexed by the balanced Kostant representative
w\in W^{P}
provided by the combinatorial lemma.
For (2), use
w^{\prime}\in W^{Q}
from Lem.
5.1
, and then use (1) of Prop.
5.3
. For (3), use
w^{\sf v}\in W^{P}
from Lem.
5.2
, and then use (2) of Prop.
5.3
. For (4) use
w^{\sf v}{}^{\prime}\in W^{Q}
from Lem.
5.1
and
5.2
, and (3) of Prop.
5.3
. The assertions of the cohomology degrees
is clear from Prop.
2.15
and
2.16
.
∎

5.1.3.
The Manin–Drinfeld principle

Continue with the notations
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times E)
,

\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times E),

\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu)
, and
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime}).

Assume that
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
satisfy the conditions of the combinatorial lemma (Lem.
3.16
), and let

\lambda=w^{-1}\cdot(\mu+\mu^{\prime}).

Let
K_{f}
be an open-compact subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
such that

{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}(\sigma_{f}\times\sigma^{\prime}_{f})
has nonzero
K_{f}
-fixed vectors; suppose
\sf{k}
is the dimension of these
K_{f}
-fixed vectors. Let

I_{b}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma_{f},\sigma^{\prime}_{f})_{P,w}\ :=\ {}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}\left(H^{b_{n}^{F}+b_{n^{\prime}}^{F}}(\mathcal{S}^{M_{P}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{w\cdot\lambda})(\sigma_{f}\times\sigma^{\prime}_{f})\right)^{K_{f}},

and, similarly, define

I_{b}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}\ :=\ {}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{Q(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}\left(H^{b_{n}^{F}+b_{n^{\prime}}^{F}}(\mathcal{S}^{M_{Q}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{w^{\prime}\cdot\lambda})(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n)\times\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))\right)^{K_{f}}.

Now, go to ‘top-degree’ for the contragredient modules and define:

I_{t}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma_{f}^{\sf v}(n^{\prime}),\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f}(-n))_{P,w^{\sf v}}\ :=\ {}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}\left(H^{t_{n}^{F}+t_{n^{\prime}}^{F}}(\mathcal{S}^{M_{P}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{w^{\sf v}\cdot\lambda})(\sigma_{f}^{\sf v}(n^{\prime})\times\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f}(-n))\right)^{K_{f}},

and, similarly, define

I_{t}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\sf v})_{Q,w^{\sf v}{}^{\prime}}\ :=\ {}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{Q(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}\left(H^{t_{n}^{F}+t_{n^{\prime}}^{F}}(\mathcal{S}^{M_{Q}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{w^{\sf v}{}^{\prime}\cdot\lambda})(\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f}\times\sigma_{f}^{\sf v})\right)^{K_{f}}.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let the notations be as above.

(1)

The sum

I_{b}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma_{f},\sigma^{\prime}_{f})_{P,w}\ \oplus\ I_{b}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}

is a
2\sf{k}
-dimensional
E
-vector space that is isotypic in
H^{q_{b}}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E}).
Note that if
Q=P
then
w^{\prime}\neq w.
Furthermore,
there is a
\mathcal{H}^{G,\mathsf{S}}
-equivariant projection:

\mathfrak{R}^{b}_{\sigma_{f},\sigma^{\prime}_{f}}\ :\ H^{q_{b}}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\ \longrightarrow\ I_{b}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma_{f},\sigma^{\prime}_{f})_{P,w}\oplus I_{b}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}.

(2)

Similarly, in ‘top-degree’, the sum

I_{t}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma_{f}^{\sf v}(n^{\prime}),\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f}(-n))_{P,w^{\sf v}}\ \oplus\ I_{t}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\sf v})_{Q,w^{\sf v}{}^{\prime}}

is a
2\sf{k}
-dimensional
E
-vector space that is isotypic in
H^{q_{t}}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda^{\sf v},E}).
Note that if
Q=P
then
w^{\sf v}{}^{\prime}\neq w^{\sf v}.
Furthermore,
there is a
\mathcal{H}^{G,\mathsf{S}}
-equivariant projection:

\mathfrak{R}^{t}_{\sigma_{f},\sigma^{\prime}_{f}}\ :\ H^{q_{t}}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda^{\sf v},E})\ \longrightarrow\ I_{t}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma_{f}^{\sf v}(n^{\prime}),\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f}(-n))_{P,w^{\sf v}}\oplus I_{t}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\sf v})_{Q,w^{\sf v}{}^{\prime}}.

The above theorem is the exact analogue of
[
26
, Thm. 5.12]
, and the proof is identical. To help the reader, the two key-ideas are adumbrated as follows:

•

There is a spectral sequence–built from the cohomology of various boundary strata
\partial_{R}\mathcal{S}^{G}
, as
R
runs over
G(\mathbb{Q})
-conjugacy classes of parabolic subgroups of
G
–that converges to the boundary cohomology
H^{\bullet}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G},-)
. This spectral sequence was alluded to in Sect.
2.6
and is discussed in greater detail in
[
26
, Sect. 4.1]
. The basic idea here is that up to semi-simplification the cohomology of the boundary is built from parabolically induced representations.

•

Recall the strong multiplicity one theorem of Jacquet and Shalika for isobaric automorphic representations

[
31
, Thm. 4.4]
. The two induced modules in
I_{b}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma_{f},\sigma^{\prime}_{f})_{P,w}
and
I_{b}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}
are themselves, of course,
\mathcal{H}^{G,\mathsf{S}}
-equivalent, and more importantly, after applying Jacquet–Shalika, they are not almost-everywhere equivalent to any other induced module anywhere else in boundary cohomology; see
[
26
, Sect. 5.3.3]
for more details.

5.2.
Eisenstein cohomology

All the statements in
[
26
, Chap. 6]
go through
mutatis mutandis
in the current situation. Therefore, the discussion below is very brief and just enough details are provided for this article to be reasonably self-contained, and to be able to state the main theorem on rank-one
Eisenstein cohomology in Thm.
5.6
below.

5.2.1.
Poincaré duality and consequences

The Poincaré duality pairings on
\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}}
and
\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}}
are compatible with the maps in the long exact sequence in Sect.
2.1
:

\textstyle{H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{r}^{*}}
\textstyle{\times}
\textstyle{H^{{\sf d}-\bullet}_{c}(\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda^{\sf v},E})}
\textstyle{\longrightarrow}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{H^{\bullet}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})}
\textstyle{\times}
\textstyle{H^{{\sf d}-1-\bullet}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda^{\sf v},E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{d}^{*}}
\textstyle{\longrightarrow}
\textstyle{E}

Here
{\rm dim}(\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}})=b_{N}^{F}+t_{N}^{F}=:{\sf d}
; and so
{\rm dim}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}})={\sf d}-1=q_{b}+q_{t}.

A consequence of the above diagram is that Eisenstein cohomology, defined as

H^{q}_{\rm Eis}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\ :=\ {\rm Image}\left(H^{q}(\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{r}^{\bullet}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{q}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\right),

is a maximal isotropic subspace of boundary cohomology, i.e.,

H^{q}_{{\rm Eis}}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\ =\ H^{{\sf d}-1-q}_{{\rm Eis}}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda^{\sf v},E})^{\perp}.

5.2.2.
Main result on rank-one Eisenstein cohomology

With notations as in Sect.
5.1.3
,
consider the following maps
starting from global cohomology
H^{q_{b}}(\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})

and ending with an isotypic component in boundary cohomology:

\textstyle{H^{q_{b}}(\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{r}^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{q_{b}}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{R}_{\sigma_{f},\sigma^{\prime}_{f}}^{b}}
\textstyle{I_{b}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma_{f},\sigma^{\prime}_{f})_{P,w}\ \oplus\ I_{b}^{\mathsf{S}}(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}}

Recall, from Thm.
5.5
, that

I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}({{\sigma}_{f}},{\sigma}^{\prime}_{f})_{P,w}\oplus I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma_{f}^{\prime}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}

is a
E
-vector space of dimension
2{\mathsf{k}}
. In the self-associate case replace
Q
by
P
.
The proof of the main result on Eisenstein cohomology stated below also needs the analogue of the above maps for cohomology in degree
q_{t}
for
the coefficient system
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda^{\sf v},E}.

Theorem 5.6
.

For brevity, let

\mathfrak{I}^{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})\ :=\ \mathfrak{R}_{\sigma_{f},\sigma^{\prime}_{f}}^{b}(H^{q_{b}}_{{\rm Eis}}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda,E})),\quad\mathfrak{I}^{t}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})^{\sf v}\ :=\ \mathfrak{R}_{\sigma_{f},\sigma^{\prime}_{f}}^{t}(H^{q_{t}}_{{\rm Eis}}(\partial\mathcal{S}^{G}_{K_{f}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\lambda^{\sf v},E})).

(1)

In the non-self-associate cases (
n\neq n^{\prime}
) we have:

(a)

\mathfrak{I}^{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})
is an
E
-subspace of dimension
{\mathsf{k}}
.

(b)

\mathfrak{I}^{t}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})^{\sf v}
is an
E
-subspace of dimension
{\mathsf{k}}
.

(2)

In the self-associate case (
n=n^{\prime}
) the same assertions hold by putting
Q=P.

It helps to have a mental picture of when
{\mathsf{k}}=1
, i.e., then

\mathfrak{I}^{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})
is a line in the ambient two-dimensional space

I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}({{\sigma}_{f}},{\sigma}^{\prime}_{f})_{P,w}\oplus I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma_{f}^{\prime}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}
;
as will be seen later, the “slope” of this line contains arithmetic information about
L
-values.

A very brief sketch of proof.

The proof works exactly as explained in
[
26
, Sect. 6.2.2]
, and involves two basic steps:

(i)

The first step is to show that both
\mathfrak{I}^{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})
and

\mathfrak{I}^{t}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})^{\sf v}
have dimension at least
{\mathsf{k}}
; this is achieved by going to a transcendental level and appealing to Langlands’s constant term theorem and producing enough cohomology classes in the image. The essential features are reviewed in Sect.
5.2.3
below, and for more details the reader is referred to
[
26
, Sect. 6.3.7]
.

(ii)

The second step, after invoking properties of the Poincaré duality pairing reviewed above, is to show that both

\mathfrak{I}^{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})
and
\mathfrak{I}^{t}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})^{\sf v}
have dimension exactly

{\mathsf{k}}.
This step works exactly as in
[
26
, Sect. 6.2.2.2]
.

∎

5.2.3.
L
-values and rank-one Eisenstein cohomology

The key ingredient in the main theorem on the rationality of these
L
-values
is the role played by the
L
-values in the above result on rank-one Eisenstein cohomology.

5.2.3.1
Induced representations

Let
\sigma
(resp.,
\sigma^{\prime}
) be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{n}(\mathbb{A})
(resp.,
G_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A})
). The relation with the previous ‘arithmetic’ notation is that given
\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu)
and given
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
, think of

{}^{\iota}\sigma_{f}
to be the finite part of a cuspidal automorphic representation
{}^{\iota}\sigma
, etc. The
\iota
is fixed and it is suppressed until otherwise mentioned.
Consider the induced representation
I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})
consisting of all smooth functions

f:G(\mathbb{A})\to V_{\sigma}\otimes V_{\sigma^{\prime}}
such that

(5.7)

f(mug)\ =\ |\delta_{P}|(m)^{\frac{1}{2}}|\delta_{P}|(m)^{\frac{s}{N}}\,(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})(m)\,f(g),

for all
m\in M_{P}(\mathbb{A})
,
u\in U_{P}(\mathbb{A})
, and
g\in G(\mathbb{A})
; where
V_{\sigma}
(resp.,
V_{\sigma^{\prime}}
) is the subspace inside the space of cusp forms on
G_{n}(\mathbb{A})
(resp.,
G_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A})
) realizing the representation
\sigma
(resp.,

\sigma^{\prime}
). In other words,

I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})={\mathrm{Ind}}_{P(\mathbb{A})}^{G(\mathbb{A})}((\sigma\otimes|\ |^{\frac{n^{\prime}}{N}s})\otimes(\sigma^{\prime}\otimes|\ |^{\frac{-n}{N}s})),

where
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{P}^{G}
denotes the normalized parabolic induction. In terms of
algebraic or un-normalized induction, we have

(5.8)

I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})={}^{\mathrm{a}}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{P(\mathbb{A})}^{G(\mathbb{A})}((\sigma\otimes|\ |^{\frac{n^{\prime}}{N}s+\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}})\otimes(\sigma^{\prime}\otimes|\ |^{\frac{-n}{N}s-\frac{n}{2}})).

Specifically, note the
point of evaluation

s_{0}=-N/2
:

{}^{\mathrm{a}}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{P(\mathbb{A})}^{G(\mathbb{A})}\left(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime}\right)\ =\ I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})|_{s=-N/2},\ \ {}^{\mathrm{a}}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{Q(\mathbb{A})}^{G(\mathbb{A})}\left(\sigma^{\prime}(n)\otimes\sigma(-n^{\prime})\right)\ =\ I_{Q}^{G}(s,\sigma^{\prime}\otimes\sigma)|_{s=N/2}.

The finite parts of the induced representations appear in boundary cohomology.

5.2.3.2
Standard intertwining operators

There is an element
w_{P}\in W_{G}
, the Weyl group of
G
, which is uniquely determined by the property

w_{P}(\mathbf{\Pi}_{G}-\{\bm{\upalpha}_{P}\})\subset\mathbf{\Pi}_{G}
and
w_{P}(\bm{\upalpha}_{P})<0.
If we write

w_{P}=(w_{P_{0}}^{\tau})_{\tau:F\to E}
, then for each
\tau
, as a permutation matrix in
{\rm GL}_{N}
, we have

w_{P_{0}}^{\tau}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}&1_{n}\\
1_{n^{\prime}}&\end{smallmatrix}\right].

The parabolic subgroup
Q
, which is associate to
P
, corresponds to

w_{P}(\mathbf{\Pi}_{G}-\{\bm{\upalpha}_{P}\}).
Since

w_{P_{0}}^{\tau}\!{}^{-1}\,\mathrm{diag}(h,h^{\prime})\,w_{P_{0}}^{\tau}=\mathrm{diag}(h^{\prime},h)
for all
\mathrm{diag}(h,h^{\prime})\in M_{P_{0}^{\tau}}
, we get

w_{P}(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})=\sigma^{\prime}\otimes\sigma
as a representation of
M_{Q}(\mathbb{A})
.
The global standard intertwining operator:

T_{\mathrm{st}}^{PQ}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime}):I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\ \longrightarrow\ I_{Q}^{G}(-s,\sigma^{\prime}\otimes\sigma)

is given by the integral

(5.9)

(T_{\mathrm{st}}^{PQ}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})f)(\underline{g})=\int_{U_{Q}(\mathbb{A})}f(w_{P_{0}}^{-1}\,\underline{u}\,\underline{g})\,d\underline{u}.

See
5.2.3.5
for the choice of measure
d\underline{u}.

Abbreviate
T_{\mathrm{st}}^{PQ}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})
as
T_{\mathrm{st}}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime}).

The global standard intertwining operator factorizes as a product of local standard intertwining operators:

T_{\mathrm{st}}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})=\otimes_{v}T_{\mathrm{st}}(s,\sigma_{v}\otimes\sigma^{\prime}_{v})

where the local operator
is given by a similar local integral. (At an archimedean place, the effect of this operator in relative Lie algebra cohomology has already been described in Sect.
4
.)

5.2.3.3
Eisenstein series

Let
f\in I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})
, for
\underline{g}\in G(\mathbb{A})
the value

f(\underline{g})
is a cusp form on
M_{P}(\mathbb{A}).
By the defining equivariance property of
f
, the complex number

f(\underline{g})(\underline{m})
determines and is determined by
f(\underline{m}\underline{g})(\underline{1})
for any
\underline{m}\in M_{P}(\mathbb{A}).

Henceforth,

f\in I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})
will be identified with the complex valued function
\underline{g}\mapsto f(\underline{g})(\underline{1}),
i.e., one has embedded:

I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})\ \hookrightarrow\ \cal C^{\infty}\left(U_{P}(\mathbb{A})M_{P}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\omega_{\infty}^{-1}\right)\ \subset\ \cal C^{\infty}\left(P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\omega_{\infty}^{-1}\right),

where
\omega^{-1}_{\infty}
is a simplified notation for the central character of
\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime}
restricted to

S(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}.
If
\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}(G_{n},\mu)
,
\sigma_{f}^{\prime}\in{\rm Coh}(G_{n^{\prime},}\mu^{\prime})
and
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
then

\omega_{\infty}
is the product of the central characters
\omega_{\mathcal{M}_{{}^{\iota}\!\mu}}\omega_{\mathcal{M}_{{}^{\iota}\!\mu^{\prime}}}
restricted to
S(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}.

Given
f\in I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})
, thought of as a function on
P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})
, define the corresponding Eisenstein series

{\rm Eis}_{P}(s,f)\in\cal C^{\infty}\left(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\omega_{\infty}^{-1}\right)
by the usual averaging over
P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q})
:

(5.10)

{\rm Eis}_{P}(s,f)(\underline{g})\ :=\ \sum_{\gamma\in P(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{Q})}f(\gamma\underline{g}),

which is convergent if
\Re(s)\gg 0
and has meromorphic continuation to the entire complex plane.
This provides an intertwining operator

{\rm Eis}_{P}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})\ :\ I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})\ \longrightarrow\ \cal C^{\infty}\left(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\omega_{\infty}^{-1}\right);

denote
{\rm Eis}_{P}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})(f)
simply as
{\rm Eis}_{P}(s,f).

To construct a map in cohomology, one needs to evaluate at
s=-N/2,
begging the question whether the
Eisenstein series is holomorphic at
s=-N/2.
For this, it is well-known that one has to show that the constant term of the Eisenstein series is holomorphic at
s=-N/2.

5.2.3.4
The constant term map

For the parabolic subgroup
Q
, the constant term map, denoted

\mathcal{F}^{Q}:\cal C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\omega_{\infty}^{-1})\to\cal C^{\infty}(M_{Q}(\mathbb{Q})U_{Q}(\mathbb{A})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\omega_{\infty}^{-1}),
is given by:

(5.11)

\mathcal{F}^{Q}(\phi)(\underline{g})\ =\ \int\limits_{U_{Q}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash U_{Q}(\mathbb{A})}\phi(\underline{u}\,\underline{g})\,d\underline{u}.

5.2.3.5
The choice of the global measure
d\underline{u}

In the integrals defining the intertwining operator (
5.9
) and the constant term map (
5.11
) the
choice of measure
d\underline{u}
on
U_{Q}(\mathbb{A})
needs to be fixed, where
U_{Q}=\mathrm{Res}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(U_{Q_{0}})
is the unipotent radical of the maximal parabolic subgroup
Q
;
recall that
Q_{0}
is the standard maximal parabolic subgroup of
{\rm GL}(N)
corresponding to
N=n^{\prime}+n
.
To begin, take the global measure
{}^{L}\!d\underline{u}
on
U_{Q}(\mathbb{A})=U_{Q_{0}}(\mathbb{A}_{F})

as a product over the coordinates of
U_{Q_{0}}
of the additive measure
d\underline{x}
on
\mathbb{A}_{F}
, which in turn is a product
\prod_{v}dx_{v}
of local additive measures
dx_{v}
on
F_{v}^{+}
; for a finite place
v
normalise
dx_{v}
by
{\rm vol}(\cal O_{v})=1
, where
\cal O_{v}
is the ring of integers of
F_{v}
,
and for an archimedean
v
take
dx_{v}
as the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{C}.
The notation
{}^{L}\!d\underline{u}
is to suggest that this measure is well-suited
for the purposes of the analytic theory of
L
-functions.
For the constant term map (
5.11
) to correspond to the restriction map in cohomology, the global measure should be normalised by asking

{\rm vol}(U_{Q_{0}}(F)\backslash U_{Q_{0}}(\mathbb{A}_{F}))=1;
see Borel
[
3
, Sect. 6]
. Hence, consider the global measure on

U_{Q}(\mathbb{A})=U_{Q_{0}}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
:

d\,\underline{u}\ :=\ \frac{1}{{\rm vol}_{{}^{L}\!d\underline{u}}(U_{Q_{0}}(F)\backslash U_{Q_{0}}(\mathbb{A}_{F}))}\,{}^{L}\!d\underline{u}.

Of course,

{\rm vol}_{{}^{L}\!d\underline{u}}(U_{Q_{0}}(F)\backslash U_{Q_{0}}(\mathbb{A}_{F}))={\rm vol}_{d\underline{x}}(F\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F})^{\dim(U_{Q_{0}})}={\rm vol}_{d\underline{x}}(F\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F})^{nn^{\prime}}.

Recall some classical algebraic number theory (see Tate
[
47
, Sect. 4.1]
) for the volume of
F\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F}.

For the measure
d\underline{x}
on
\mathbb{A}_{F}
, the volume of a fundamental domain for the action of
F
on
\mathbb{A}_{F}
is
|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{1/2}.

If the set
{\rm Hom}(F,\mathbb{C})
of complex embeddings of
F
is enumerated as, say,
\{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{d_{F}}\}
, and
suppose
\{\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{d_{F}}\}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
F
, then the absolute discriminant of
F
is defined as

\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=\det[\sigma_{i}(\omega_{j})]^{2}.
The square root of the absolute value of the discriminant,
|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{1/2},

as an element of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times},
is independent of the enumeration and the choice of basis. For the main theorem on
L
-values, the choice of measure can be changed by a nonzero rational number, which will still give the same rationality results and the reciprocity laws as in Thm.
5.16
. Define the global measure on

U_{Q}(\mathbb{A})=U_{Q_{0}}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
as:

(5.12)

d\,\underline{u}\ :=\ |\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\,{}^{L}\!d\underline{u}.

5.2.3.6
Theorem of Langlands on the constant term of an Eisenstein series

Theorem 5.13

(Langlands)
.

Let
f\in I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime}).

(1)

In the non-self-associate cases (
n\neq n^{\prime}
), one has:

\mathcal{F}^{Q}\circ{\rm Eis}_{P}(s,f)\ =\ T_{\mathrm{st}}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})(f).

(2)

In the self-associate cases (
n=n^{\prime}
and
P=Q
), one has:

\mathcal{F}^{P}\circ{\rm Eis}_{P}(s,f)\ =\ f+T_{\mathrm{st}}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime})(f).

Suppose
f=\otimes_{v}f_{v}
is a pure tensor in
I_{P}^{G}(s,\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})
, and
for
v\notin\mathsf{S}
suppose
f_{v}=f_{v}^{0}
is the normalized spherical vector (normalized to take the value
1
on the identity),
and similarly,
\tilde{f}_{v}^{0}
is such a vector in the
v
-th component of
I_{Q}^{G}(-s,\sigma^{\prime}\otimes\sigma)
, then
from
[
44
, Thm. 6.3.1]
we have the fundamental analytic identity

(5.14)

\mathcal{F}^{Q}({\rm Eis}_{P}(s,f))\ =\ |\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\,\frac{L^{\mathsf{S}}(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime\mathsf{v}})}{L^{\mathsf{S}}(s+1,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime\mathsf{v}})}\,\otimes_{v\notin\mathsf{S}}\tilde{f}_{v}^{0}\otimes_{v\in\mathsf{S}}T_{\mathrm{st}}(s,\sigma_{v}\otimes\sigma^{\prime}_{v})f_{v}.

The proof of the main theorem on the arithmetic of special values of
L(s,\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime\mathsf{v}})
comes from seeing the contribution of this identity in cohomology.

5.2.3.7
Holomorphy of the Eisenstein series at the point of evaluation

Given weights
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times E)
and

\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times E)
and strongly-inner Hecke-summands

\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu)
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime}),
recall then that
{}^{\iota}\sigma
and
{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}
are cuspidal automorphic representations of

G_{n}(\mathbb{A})={\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
and
G_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A})={\rm GL}_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
for any
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
.
The pair
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
of weights
is said to be
on the right of the unitary axis
if the abelian width is bounded above by the point of evaluation:

a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\leq-N/2,

To explain the terminology, it is clear from the definition of the cuspidal parameters (
2.8
), (
2.9
),
and the archimedean representation (
2.10
),
that
{}^{\iota}\sigma={}^{\iota}\sigma_{u}\otimes|\!|\ |\!|^{-{\bf w}/2}
for a unitary cuspidal representation
{}^{\iota}\sigma_{u};
similarly,

{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}={}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}_{u}\otimes|\!|\ |\!|^{-{\bf w}^{\prime}/2}.
Hence, we have

L(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})\ =\ L(s-a(\mu,\mu^{\prime}),{}^{\iota}\sigma_{u}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma_{u}^{\prime{\sf v}}).

Now, suppose
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
is on the right of the unitary axis, then for the
L
-value in the denominator of the right hand side of (
5.14
) at the point of evaluation

s=-\tfrac{N}{2}
we have:

L^{\mathsf{S}}(-\tfrac{N}{2}+1,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})\ =\ L^{\mathsf{S}}(1-\tfrac{N}{2}-a(\mu,\mu^{\prime}),{}^{\iota}\sigma_{u}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma_{u}^{\prime{\sf v}})\ \neq\ 0,

since
1-\tfrac{N}{2}-a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\geq 1,
by a nonvanishing result for Rankin–Selberg
L
-functions recalled in

[
26
, Thm. 7.1, (4)]
.
Of course, the nonvanishing of this
L
-value, the holomorphy of the Eisenstein series
{\rm Eis}_{P}(s,f)
or of its constant term are all intimately linked. We have the following well-known result (
[
37
]
and
[
27
, Chap. IV
S
5]
).

Theorem 5.15
.

Suppose we are given weights
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times E)
and

\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times E),

strongly-inner Hecke-summands
\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu)
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime}),
and
an
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}.
Assume that
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
is on the right
of the unitary axis. Then
{\rm Eis}_{P}(s,f)
is holomorphic at
s=-N/2
, unless we are in the exceptional case

n=n^{\prime}
and
{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}={}^{\iota}\sigma\otimes|\ |^{-n-1}.

The poles on the right of the unitary axis are simple and contribute to the residual spectrum

[
27
]
and then the assertion follows from
[
37
]
. The exceptional case exactly corresponds to when the numerator of
of the right hand side of (
5.14
) at the point of evaluation is a pole at
1
, i.e., when

L(-\tfrac{N}{2}-a(\mu,\mu^{\prime}),{}^{\iota}\sigma_{u}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma_{u}^{\prime{\sf v}})
is a pole at
1
; which is possible only when
n=n^{\prime}
and
{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}={}^{\iota}\sigma\otimes|\!|\ |\!|^{-n-1}.

To parse this further: If
{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}={}^{\iota}\sigma\otimes|\!|\ |\!|^{r}
for any integer
r
, then
\mu^{\prime}=\mu-r
. Then the cuspidal parameters for
\mu^{\prime}
and
\mu
are related thus:

\alpha^{\prime v}_{i}=\alpha^{v}_{i}+r,\ \beta^{\prime v}_{i}=\beta^{v}_{i}+r;
hence the cuspidal width
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=0.
For the main theorem on
L
-values, we will assume the conditions imposed by the combinatorial lemma, and in particular we will have
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\geq 2
to guarantee at least two critical values. Hence, the exceptional case will not be relevant to us.

5.3.
The main theorem on
L
-values

5.3.1.
Statement of the main theorem

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
n
and
n^{\prime}
be two positive integers.
Let
F
be a totally imaginary field, and
E
a finite Galois extension of
\mathbb{Q}
that contains a copy of
F
.
Consider strongly-pure weights
\mu\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n}\times E)
and

\mu^{\prime}\in X^{+}_{00}(T_{n^{\prime}}\times E).
Let
\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n},\mu)
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(G_{n^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime})
be strongly-inner Hecke-summands,
and assume that
E
is large enough to contain all the Hecke-eigenvalues for
\sigma_{f}
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}.

Let
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
be an embedding. Recall then that
{}^{\iota}\sigma
and
{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}
are cuspidal automorphic representations of

G_{n}(\mathbb{A})={\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
and
G_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A})={\rm GL}_{n^{\prime}}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
, respectively. Put
N=n+n^{\prime}.

Suppose that
m\in\tfrac{N}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
is such that both
m
and
1+m
are critical for the Rankin–Selberg
L
-function
L(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}).

(1)

If for some
\iota
,
L(1+m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})=0
, then
1+m-a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=\tfrac{1}{2}
and

L(1+m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})\ =\ L(\tfrac{1}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma_{u}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{u})=0

is the central critical value. Furthermore,
L(1+m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})=0
for every
\iota.

(2)

Assume
F
is in the
CM
-case.
Suppose that
L(1+m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})\neq 0
, then we have

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\,\frac{L(m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(1+m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}\ \in\ \iota(E).

Since
L(m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})=L(-N/2,{}^{\iota}\sigma(N/2+m)\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})
, the pair

(\mu-N/2-m,\mu^{\prime})
of weights satisfies Lem.
3.16
giving a balanced Kostant representative
w\in W^{P}
. Let

w^{\prime}\in W^{Q}
be determined by Lem.
5.1
.
Furthermore, for any
\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
we have

\gamma\left(|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\,\frac{L(m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(1+m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}\right)\ =\ \varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\cdot\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)\cdot|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\,\frac{L(m,{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(1+m,{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}.

(3)

Assume
F
is in the
TR
-case. Then
nn^{\prime}
is even.
Suppose that
L(1+m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})\neq 0
, then we have

\frac{L(m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(1+m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}\ \in\ \iota(E).

Furthermore, for any
\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
we have

\gamma\left(\frac{L(m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(1+m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}\right)\ =\ \frac{L(m,{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(1+m,{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}.

As mentioned in the introduction, statement (1), is originally due to Mœglin
[
36
, Sect. 5]
; our proof below is independent of
[
36
]
. More generally,
the assertion on the vanishing of the central critical value is a special case of
Deligne’s conjecture
[
8
, Conj. 2.7, (ii)]
based on a suggestion of Benedict Gross, that the order of vanishing at
a critical point is independent of the embedding
\iota
.

5.3.2.
Proof of the main theorem

5.3.2.1
Finiteness of the relevant
L
-values

The paragraph after Thm.
5.15
says that if
L(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma\otimes\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})

has a pole at
m\in\tfrac{N}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
then the cuspidal width
\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=0
; a situation which is ruled out by the hypothesis
that requires two critical points. Hence all the
L
-values under consideration are finite; a fact that will be used without further comment.

5.3.2.2
It suffices to prove Thm.
5.16
for the point of evaluation
m=-N/2

If the theorem is known for the critical points
s=-\tfrac{N}{2}
and
1-\tfrac{N}{2}
and for all possible

\mu
,
\mu^{\prime}
,
\sigma_{f}
,
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}
, then one can deduce the theorem
for any pair of successive critical points
m,\,m+1
for a given
\sigma_{f}
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}
.
This follows from using Tate-twists (Sect.
2.4.1
) and the combinatorial lemma.
Take any integer
r
and replace
\mu
by
\mu-r\bm{\updelta}_{n}
and
\sigma_{f}
by
\sigma_{f}(r).
Note that

{}^{\iota}(\sigma_{f}(r))={}^{\iota}\sigma_{f}\otimes|\!|\ |\!|^{r}.

The condition that

-\tfrac{N}{2}
and
1-\tfrac{N}{2}
are critical for

L(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma\otimes|\!|\ |\!|^{r}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})=L(s+r,{}^{\iota}\sigma\otimes\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})
, after the combinatorial lemma, bounds the abelian width
a(\mu-r\bm{\updelta}_{n},\mu^{\prime})
by the cuspidal width
\ell(\mu-r\bm{\updelta}_{n},\mu^{\prime})

as in (2) of Lem.
3.16
.
Now, the crucial point is that, whereas for the abelian width one has
a(\mu-r\bm{\updelta}_{n},\mu^{\prime})=a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})-r
, but for the cuspidal width one has independence of
r
in as much as

\ell(\mu-r\bm{\updelta}_{n},\mu^{\prime})=\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
.
This bounds the possible twisting integers
r
above and below as:

-\frac{N}{2}+1-\frac{\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}{2}-a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\ \leq\ -r\ \leq\ -\frac{N}{2}-1+\frac{\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})}{2}-a(\mu,\mu^{\prime}).

As
r
ranges over this set,
using the critical set described in Prop.
3.12
, one sees that

\frac{L(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma(r)\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(1-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma(r)\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}\ =\ \frac{L(r-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(r+1-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}

runs over the set of all successive pairs of critical points;
no more and no less!
The number of possible
r
is

\ell(\mu,\mu^{\prime})-1
which is exactly the number of pairs of successive critical points.

5.3.2.3
Being on the right versus on the left of the unitary axis

Suppose that
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
is on the right of the unitary axis:
a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\leq-\tfrac{N}{2}.
Then (1) is vacuously true since

L(1-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})=L(1-\tfrac{N}{2}-a(\mu,\mu^{\prime}),{}^{\iota}\sigma_{u}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}}_{u})\neq 0
by a well-known nonvanishing result for Rankin–Selberg
L
-functions as already mentioned before.
Next, recall that the Eisenstein series
{\rm Eis}_{P}(s,f)
is holomorphic at
s=-\tfrac{N}{2}
, and at this point of evaluation (2) and (3) will be proved below.
Granting this, suppose, on the other hand, that
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
is on the left of the unitary axis, i.e.,
a(\mu,\mu^{\prime})>-\tfrac{N}{2}.
Then,

a(\mu^{\prime},\mu)<\tfrac{N}{2},
i.e.,
(\mu^{\prime},\mu)
is on the right of the unitary axis for the point of evaluation is
\tfrac{N}{2}
, so we get
the holomorphy of
{\rm Eis}_{Q}(s,f)
at
s=\tfrac{N}{2}
and whence statement (2) for

L(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v})
and for
s=\tfrac{N}{2}.
And as above, (1) is in fact vacuously true, because

L(1+\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v})=L(1+\tfrac{N}{2}-a(\mu^{\prime},\mu),{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}_{u}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v}_{u})\neq 0.
Statement (2) for this situation says:

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\,\frac{L(\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v})}{L(1+\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v})}\ \in\ \iota(E),

where the
L
-value in the denominator is not zero. Suppose the
L
-value in the numerator is
0
(which can happen in the special case

\tfrac{N}{2}-a(\mu^{\prime},\mu)=\tfrac{1}{2}
) then the Galois equivariance in (2) implies that
L(\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v})=0
for

every

\iota.

Applying the functional equation (
[
26
, (3), Thm. 7.1]
) to the above ratio of
L
-values we get:

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon(\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v})}{\varepsilon(1+\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v})}\cdot\frac{L(1-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}\ \in\ \iota(E).

For brevity, let
\tau:={}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v}
. The global epsilon-factor is a product of local factors as

\varepsilon(s,\tau)=\prod_{v}\varepsilon(s,\tau_{v},\psi_{v})
, where we have fixed an additive character
\psi:F\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}.
(See, for example,
[
44
, Sect. 10.1]
.)
At a non-archimedean place
v
, the local factor has the form
\varepsilon(s,\tau_{v},\psi_{v})=W(\tau_{v})q_{v}^{(1/2-s)(c(\tau_{v})+c(\psi_{v}))}
, where
W(\tau_{v})
is the local root number,
q_{v}
the cardinality of the residue field of
F_{v}
,
and
c(\tau_{v})
and
c(\psi_{v})
are integers defined to be the conductoral exponents of the respective data; it follows that

\varepsilon(N/2,\tau_{v},\psi_{v})/\varepsilon(1+N/2,\tau_{v},\psi_{v})
is an integral power of
q_{v}
.
At an archimedean place, it follows from

[
33
, (4.7)]
that the local factor is a constant, and hence the ratio is
1
. Whence,

\varepsilon(\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v})/\varepsilon(1+\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v})

is a nonzero integer,
from which it follows that

(5.17)

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\,\frac{L(1-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}\ \in\ \iota(E).

From the functional equation it is clear that

L(1-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})=0\iff L(\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\sf v})=0,

proving (1) when
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
is on the left of the unitary axis.
If
L(1-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})\neq 0
, then taking reciprocal of the ratio on the left hand side of
(
5.17
), (2) will follows when
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
is on the left of the unitary axis. (See also
[
26
, Sect. 7.3.2.5]
for
a slightly different way to argue this point if
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
is on the left of the unitary axis.) The discussion is the same for (3).

5.3.2.4
Proof of the rationality result

After the above reductions, it suffices to prove (2) and (3) of Thm.
5.16
when
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
is on the right of the unitary axis and for
m=-N/2.

This involves Eisenstein cohomology. Assume henceforth that
(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
is on the right of the unitary axis. For now
F
is any totally imaginary field
(either
CM
or
TR
).
By Thm.
5.6
,
the subspace
\mathfrak{I}^{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})
, which is the image of global cohomology in the
2{\mathsf{k}}
-dimensional
E
-vector space

I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})_{P,w}\oplus I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}
,
is a
{\mathsf{k}}
-dimensional
E
-subspace; and furthermore, from the proof of that theorem,
we get an intertwining operator
T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
defined over
E

such that in the non-self-associate case (
n\neq n^{\prime}
) we have:

\mathfrak{I}^{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})\ =\ \left\{\left(\xi,\,T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})(\xi)\right)\ |\ \xi\in I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})_{P,w}\right\},

and in the self-associate case we will have:

\mathfrak{I}^{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})\ =\ \left\{\left(\xi,\,\xi+T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})(\xi)\right)\ |\ \xi\in I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})_{P,w}\right\}.

The idea of the proof is to take
T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
to a transcendental level, use the constant term theorem which gives
L
-values, and then descend back to an arithmetic level, giving a rationality result for the said
L
-values.
Take an
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
, and consider
T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\otimes_{E,\iota}\mathbb{C}
. We have:

\mathfrak{I}^{b}({}^{\iota}\!\sigma_{f},{}^{\iota}\!\sigma_{f}^{\prime})\ =\ \left\{\left(\xi\,,\,T_{\mathrm{st}}(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\!\sigma\otimes{}^{\iota}\!\sigma^{\prime})^{\bullet}\xi\right)\ :\ \xi\in I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}({}^{\iota}\!\sigma_{f},{}^{\iota}\!\sigma_{f}^{\prime})_{P,{}^{\iota}\!w}\right\}

in the non-self-associate case, and with analogous modification for the self-associate case. Here,

T_{\mathrm{st}}(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\!\sigma\otimes{}^{\iota}\!\sigma^{\prime})^{\bullet}
is the map induced by the standard intertwining operator in relative Lie algebra cohomology. For brevity, let
\underline{\sigma}=\sigma\times\sigma^{\prime}
.
The global operator
T_{\mathrm{st}}(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\!\underline{\sigma})^{\bullet}
factors into local standard intertwining operators.
The discussion in
[
26
, Sect. 7.3.2.1]
involving rationality properties of local standard intertwining operators at
finite places goes through verbatim in our situation, and we have for

T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\otimes_{E,\iota}\mathbb{C}
the following expression (which is the exact analogue of

[
26
, (7.38)]
):

(5.18)

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\,\frac{L(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma_{f}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma_{f}^{\prime\mathsf{v}})}{L(1-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma_{f}\times{}^{\iota}\sigma_{f}^{\prime\mathsf{v}})}\,\cdot\bigotimes_{v\in{\mathsf{S}}_{\infty}}T_{\mathrm{st}}(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\underline{\sigma}_{v})^{\bullet}\otimes\\
\otimes\Bigl(\bigl(\bigotimes_{v\in\mathsf{S}_{f}}T^{\mathrm{ar}}_{\mathrm{norm}}(\underline{\sigma}_{v})(1)\otimes\bigotimes_{v\not\in\mathsf{S}}T^{\mathrm{ar}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\underline{\sigma}_{v})(1)\Bigr)\otimes_{E,\iota}\mathbb{C}.

The local operators
T^{\mathrm{ar}}_{\mathrm{norm}}(\underline{\sigma}_{v})(1)
and
T^{\mathrm{ar}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\underline{\sigma}_{v})(1)
are exactly as in
loc. cit.
; the point of immediate interest for us being that they are defined over
E
.
For the archimedean component
T_{\mathrm{st}}(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\underline{\sigma}_{v})^{\bullet}
,
we use Prop.
4.31
, to get for

T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\otimes_{E,\iota}\mathbb{C}
the following expression involving values of the completed
L
-function:

(5.19)

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\,\frac{L(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime\mathsf{v}})}{L(1-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime\mathsf{v}})}\,\cdot\,\Bigl(\bigl(\bigotimes_{v\in\mathsf{S}_{f}}T^{\mathrm{ar}}_{\mathrm{norm}}(\underline{\sigma}_{v})(1)\otimes\bigotimes_{v\not\in\mathsf{S}}T^{\mathrm{ar}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\underline{\sigma}_{v})(1)\Bigr)\otimes_{E,\iota}\mathbb{C}.

We conclude that the complex number

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\,L(-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime\mathsf{v}})/L(1-\tfrac{N}{2},{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime\mathsf{v}})
is in

\iota(E).
When
F
is in case-
TR
, existence of a critical point implies
nn^{\prime}
is even (Cor.
3.14
), which forces

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}.

5.3.2.5
Proof of reciprocity

For Galois equivariance, apply
\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
,
to the objects and maps in the first paragraph of
Sect.
5.2.2
, while keeping in mind the behaviour of cohomology groups as Hecke modules
under changing the base field
E
. Assume now that
F
is a totally imaginary field in the
CM
-case. The claim is
that Galois-action and Eisenstein operator
T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
intertwine as:

(5.20)

(1\otimes\gamma)\circ\left(T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\otimes_{E,\iota}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}\right)\ =\ \varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\cdot\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)\cdot T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\otimes_{E,\gamma\circ\iota}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}.

From this claim and (
5.19
) the reciprocity law will follow. To prove this claim, take
n\neq n^{\prime}
(the reader can easily modify the argument for the self-associate case) and
consider the following diagram

\textstyle{I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})_{P,w}\otimes_{E,\iota}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\otimes_{E,\iota}1_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\gamma}
\textstyle{I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\otimes_{E,\iota}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\gamma}
\textstyle{I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})_{P,w}\otimes_{E,\gamma\circ\iota}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{\mathrm{Eis}}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\otimes_{E,\gamma\circ\iota}1_{\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}}
\textstyle{I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}\otimes_{E,\gamma\circ\iota}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}

The left (resp., right) vertical arrow introduces the signature
\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)
(resp.,
\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)
), and the diagram commutes up to the product
of these two signatures. For the left vertical arrow, recall from
5.1.3
that
the induced module
I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})_{P,w}
appears in boundary cohomology as

{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}\left(H^{b_{n}^{F}+b_{n^{\prime}}^{F}}(\mathcal{S}^{M_{P}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{w\cdot\lambda,E})(\sigma_{f}\times\sigma^{\prime}_{f})\right)^{K_{f}}.
Hence,

I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{f}^{\prime})_{P,w}\otimes_{E,\iota}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
appears as a Hecke-summand in

{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}\left(H^{b_{n}^{F}+b_{n^{\prime}}^{F}}(\mathcal{S}^{M_{P}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{{}^{\iota}\!w\cdot{}^{\iota}\!\lambda,\bar{\mathbb{Q}}})\right)
.
Recall from (
2.30
) that
\gamma
maps the the highest weight vector of
the coefficient system
\mathcal{M}_{{}^{\iota}\!w\cdot{}^{\iota}\!\lambda,\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
to
\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)
times
the highest weight vector of the coefficient system
\mathcal{M}_{{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\!w\cdot{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\!\lambda,\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
explaining the homothety by

\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)
in the left vertical arrow. Similarly, the induced module

I^{\mathsf{S}}_{b}(\sigma^{\prime}_{f}(n),\sigma_{f}(-n^{\prime}))_{Q,w^{\prime}}\otimes_{E,\iota}\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
appears in

{}^{\rm a}{\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbb{A}_{f})}^{G(\mathbb{A}_{f})}\left(H^{b_{n}^{F}+b_{n^{\prime}}^{F}}(\mathcal{S}^{M_{P}},\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{{}^{\iota}\!w^{\prime}\cdot{}^{\iota}\!\lambda,\bar{\mathbb{Q}}})\right)
for
w^{\prime}
related to
w
by Lem.
5.1
.
Hence, the right vertical arrow, one gets a homothety by
\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)
. Hence the claim, and whence
the reciprocity law.

If the totally imaginary field
F
is in case
CM
then the proof of (2) is complete. In case of
TR
, the rationality and the
Galois-equivariance, as will be now shown, will simplify to give the corresponding statements in (3). Assume now that
F
is
TR
, and that
F_{1}
is the maximal totally real subfield of
F
.
Existence of a critical point implies
nn^{\prime}
is even by Cor.
3.14
, hence
|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{-\tfrac{nn^{\prime}}{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
and so we may absorb it into
\iota(E)
and ignore it
from the Galois-equivariance. The simplified Galois equivariance in (3) follows from the following

Lemma 5.21
.

Suppose that
F
is
TR
, then

\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)=1=\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma).

Proof.

Recall the notations from
2.3.5.2
:

\Sigma_{F_{1}}=\{\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{d_{1}}\}
,
\varrho:\Sigma_{F}\to\Sigma_{F_{1}}
,
k=2k_{1},
and

\varrho^{-1}(\nu_{j})=\{\eta_{j1},\bar{\eta}_{j1},\dots,\eta_{jk_{1}}\bar{\eta}_{jk_{1}}\}.
Since
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are strongly-pure weights that are the base-change from weight over

F_{1}
, the Kostant representative
w
(and then so also
w^{\prime}
) has the property that all the constituents
w^{\eta},
as
\eta
varies over
\varrho^{-1}(\nu_{j}),

are copies of the same element of
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
–the Weyl group of
{\rm GL}_{N}
; in particular, since
w
is balanced,
l(w^{\eta})+l(w^{\bar{\eta}})=nn^{\prime}
and
l(w^{\eta})=l(w^{\bar{\eta}})
since

\eta
and
\bar{\eta}
have the same restriction to
F_{1}
; hence
l(w^{\eta})=nn^{\prime}/2
. Recalling the notations from Sec.
2.7
,
consider the wedge product

e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w,[\nu_{j}]}}^{*}\ :=\ e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w^{\eta_{j1}}}}^{*}\wedge e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w^{\bar{\eta}_{j1}}}}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w^{\eta_{jk_{1}}}}}^{*}\wedge e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w^{\bar{\eta}_{jk_{1}}}}}^{*}.

All the individual factors such as
e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w^{\eta_{ji}}}}^{*}
or
e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w^{\bar{\eta}_{ji}}}}^{*}
are identical and have degree
nn^{\prime}/2
. Hence the total degree of

e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w,[\nu_{j}]}}^{*}
is
nn^{\prime}/2\cdot k=nn^{\prime}k_{1}.
From (
2.26
) one gets:

e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w}}^{*}\ =\ e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w,[\nu_{1}]}}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w,[\nu_{d_{1}}]}}^{*}.

Denote the action of
\gamma
on
\Sigma_{F_{1}},
for the ordering fixed above, as
\pi_{F_{1}}(\gamma),
and let
\varepsilon_{F_{1}}(\gamma)
denote its signature.
Then one has

(1\otimes\gamma)e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w}}^{*}\ =\ \varepsilon_{F_{1}}(\gamma)^{(nn^{\prime}k_{1})^{2}}e_{\Phi_{{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\!w}}^{*};

from Def.
2.29
, one has
\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)=\varepsilon_{F_{1}}(\gamma)^{(nn^{\prime}k_{1})^{2}}=1
since
nn^{\prime}k_{1}\equiv 0\pmod{2}.

Similarly,
\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)=1.

∎

This concludes the proof of Thm.
5.16
.

5.4.
Compatibility with Deligne’s Conjecture

5.4.1.
Statement of Deligne’s Conjecture

In this subsection Deligne’s celebrated conjecture on the special values of motivic
L
-functions is formulated for the ratios of successive
successive critical
L
-values for Rankin–Selberg
L
-functions.
The notations of
[
8
]
will be freely used; a motive
M
over
\mathbb{Q}
with coefficients in a field
E
will be thought in terms of its Betti, de Rham, and
\ell
-adic realizations. Attached to a critical motive
M
are its periods
c^{\pm}(M)\in(E\otimes\mathbb{C})^{\times}
as in
loc. cit.,
that are well-defined in
(E\otimes\mathbb{C})^{\times}/E^{\times}.
We begin with a relation between the two periods over a totally imaginary base field
F
. Recall from the introduction that if
F
is in the
CM
-case, then
F_{1}
is its maximal CM subfield which is totally imaginary quadratic
over the totally real subfield
F_{1}
; suppose
F_{1}=F_{0}(\sqrt{D})
for a totally negative
D\in F_{0}
, then define

\Delta_{F_{1}}\ :=\ \sqrt{N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(D)},\quad\Delta_{F}\ :=\ \Delta_{F_{1}}^{[F:F_{1}]}.

If
F
is in the
TR
-case, then
F_{1}=F_{0}
is the maximal totally real subfield, then define

\Delta_{F_{1}}\ :=\ 1,\quad\Delta_{F}\ :=\ \Delta_{F_{1}}^{[F:F_{1}]}=1.

A proof of the following proposition due to Deligne is given in
[
9
]
:

Proposition 5.22
.

Let
M_{0}
be a pure motive of rank
n
over a totally imaginary number field
F
with coefficients in a number field
E
. Put
M=\mathrm{Res}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(M_{0}),
and suppose that
M
has no middle Hodge type. Let
c^{\pm}(M)
be the periods defined in
[
8
]
.
Then

\frac{c^{+}(M)}{c^{-}(M)}\ =\ (1\otimes\Delta_{F})^{n},\quad\mbox{in $(E\otimes\mathbb{C})^{\times}/E^{\times}$.}

Under the identification
E\otimes\mathbb{C}=\prod_{\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}
, the element
1\otimes\Delta_{F}
is
\pm 1
in each component of
(E\otimes\mathbb{C})^{\times}/E^{\times}
, since its square is trivial.
Based on Prop.
5.22
, Deligne’s conjecture
[
8
]
for the ratios of successive critical values of the completed
L
-function of
M
may be stated as:

Conjecture 5.23

(Deligne)
.

Let
M_{0}
be a pure motive of rank
n
over a totally imaginary
F
with coefficients in
E
. Put
M=\mathrm{Res}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(M_{0}),
and suppose that

M
has no middle Hodge type. For
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
let
L(s,\iota,M)
denote the completed
L
-function attached to
(M,\iota).
Put

L(s,M)=\{L(s,\iota,M)\}_{\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}}
for the array of
L
-functions taking values in
E\otimes\mathbb{C}.
Suppose
m
and
m+1
are critical
integers for
L(s,M)
, and assuming that
L(m+1,M)\neq 0
, we have

\frac{L(m,M)}{L(m+1,M)}\ =\ (1\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{d_{F}/2}\Delta_{F})^{n},\quad\mbox{in $(E\otimes\mathbb{C})/E^{\times}$.}

A word of explanation is in order, since, in
[
8
]
, Deligne formulated his conjecture for critical values of
L_{f}(s,M)
–the finite-part of the
L
-function attached to
M.

From Conj. 2.8 and (5.1.8) of
[
8
]
for
M
as above, one can deduce :

\frac{L_{f}(m,M)}{L_{f}(m+1,M)}\ =\ (1\otimes(2\bm{\uppi}\mathfrak{i})^{-n\cdot d_{F}/2})\,\frac{c^{\pm}(M)}{c^{\mp}(M)},\quad\mbox{in $E\otimes\mathbb{C}$}.

Knowing the
L
-factor at infinity one has:
L_{\infty}(m,M)/L_{\infty}(m+1,M)=1\otimes(2\bm{\uppi})^{d_{F}/2};
hence for the completed
L
-function, one can deduce:

(5.24)
