\frac{L(m,M)}{L(m+1,M)}\ =\ (1\otimes\mathfrak{i}^{n\cdot d_{F}/2})\,\frac{c^{\pm}(M)}{c^{\mp}(M)}.

It is clear now that (
5.24
) and Prop.
5.22
give Conj.
5.23
.

There is conjectural correspondence between
\sigma_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(\mathrm{Res}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}({\rm GL}_{n}/F),\mu/E)
and a pure regular motive
M(\sigma_{f})
of rank
n
over
F
with coefficients
in
E
(see
[
7
]
or
[
26
, Chap. 7]
). Given such a
\sigma_{f}
and also
\sigma^{\prime}_{f}\in{\rm Coh}_{!!}(\mathrm{Res}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}({\rm GL}_{n^{\prime}}/F),\mu^{\prime}/E)
,
Conj.
5.23
applied to
M=M(\sigma_{f})\otimes M(\sigma_{f}^{\prime{\sf v}})
gives the following conjecture
or the Rankin–Selberg
L
-functions
L(s,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})
:

Conjecture 5.25

(Deligne’s conjecture for Rankin–Selberg
L
-functions)
.

Let the notations and hypotheses be as in Thm.
5.16
.
Then:

(\mathfrak{i}^{d_{F}/2}\Delta_{F})^{nn^{\prime}}\,\frac{L(m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(m+1,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}\ \in\iota(E),

and, furthermore, for every
\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
we have the reciprocity law:

\gamma\left((\mathfrak{i}^{d_{F}/2}\Delta_{F})^{nn^{\prime}}\,\frac{L(m,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(m+1,{}^{\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}\right)\ =\ (\mathfrak{i}^{d_{F}/2}\Delta_{F})^{nn^{\prime}}\,\frac{L(m,{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}{L(m+1,{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma\times{}^{\gamma\circ\iota}\sigma^{\prime{\sf v}})}.

5.4.2.
Thm.
5.16
implies Conj.
5.25

If
F
is in the
TR
-case, then existence of a critical point implies
nn^{\prime}
is even (Cor.
3.14
), hence

(\mathfrak{i}^{d_{F}/2}\Delta_{F})^{nn^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{i}^{d_{F}nn^{\prime}/2}=\pm 1
that may be absorbed into
\iota(E)
and ignored from the Galois-equivarance, which is exactly
the content of (3) of Thm.
5.16
. Assume henceforth that
F
is in the
CM
-case. The required
compatibility follows from the equality of signatures in the following

Proposition 5.26
.

\frac{\gamma(|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{nn^{\prime}/2})}{|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{nn^{\prime}/2}}\cdot\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\cdot\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)\ =\ \frac{\gamma((\mathfrak{i}^{d_{F}/2}\Delta_{F})^{nn^{\prime}})}{(\mathfrak{i}^{d_{F}/2}\Delta_{F})^{nn^{\prime}}}.

The proof uses the following lemma.

Lemma 5.27
.

Let
F
be a totally imaginary field in the
CM
-case and suppose
F_{1}
the maximal CM subfield of
F
.
Then, as elements of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
, we have:

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{1/2}\ =\ \mathfrak{i}^{d_{F}/2}\cdot\Delta_{F}\cdot\left(N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})\right)^{1/2}.

Proof of Lem.
5.27
.

Transitivity of discriminant for the tower of fields
F/F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}

gives:

\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=\delta_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}^{[F:F_{0}]}\cdot N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{0}}).
Since the degree
[F:F_{0}]=2[F:F_{1}]
is even, one has

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{1/2}\ =\ |N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{0}})|^{1/2}\pmod{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}.

Next, one has

\delta_{F/F_{0}}=\delta_{F_{1}/F_{0}}^{[F:F_{1}]}\cdot N_{F_{1}/F_{0}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}}),
by
using transitivity of discriminant for the tower of fields
F/F_{1}/F_{0}
; using the
F_{0}
-basis
\{1,\sqrt{D}\}
for

F_{1}
, one has
\delta_{F_{1}/F_{0}}=4D;
therefore,

N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{0}})\ =\ N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(4D)^{[F:F_{1}]}\cdot N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(N_{F_{1}/F_{0}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}}))\\
=\ N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(D)^{[F:F_{1}]}\cdot N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})\pmod{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}{}^{2}}.

Since
F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}
is a CM-extension,
N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})>0
; hence

|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{1/2}\ =\ |N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{0}})|^{1/2}\ =\ |N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(D)|^{[F:F_{1}]/2}\cdot\left(N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})\right)^{1/2}\pmod{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}.

Since
D\ll 0
in
F_{0}
, we see that
(-1)^{[F_{0}:\mathbb{Q}]}N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(D)>0
. Hence,

|N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(D)|^{[F:F_{1}]/2}\ =\ ((-1)^{[F_{0}:\mathbb{Q}]}N_{F_{0}/\mathbb{Q}}(D))^{[F:F_{1}]/2}\ =\ (\mathfrak{i}^{[F_{0}:\mathbb{Q}]}\Delta_{F_{1}})^{[F:F_{1}]}\\
=\ \mathfrak{i}^{[F_{0}:\mathbb{Q}][F:F_{1}]}\Delta_{F}\ =\ \mathfrak{i}^{d_{F}/2}\Delta_{F}.

∎

After the above lemma, the proof of Prop.
5.26
follows from the following

Lemma 5.28
.

\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\cdot\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)\ =\ \frac{\gamma\left(N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})^{nn^{\prime}/2}\right)}{N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})^{nn^{\prime}/2}}.

Proof.

Suppose
F
is a CM field, then
F=F_{1}
, hence the right hand side is
1
. We contend that in this case the left hand side is also
1
, or that

\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)=\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma).
One may suppose that the ordering on

{\rm Hom}(F,E)
is fixed such that conjugate embeddings are paired:

{\rm Hom}(F,E)\ =\ \{\tau_{1},\bar{\tau}_{1},\tau_{2},\bar{\tau}_{2},\dots,\tau_{r},\bar{\tau}_{r}\}.

After composing with
\iota:E\to\mathbb{C}
, one gets an enumeration:

{\rm Hom}(F,\mathbb{C})\ =\ \{\eta_{1},\bar{\eta}_{1},\eta_{2},\bar{\eta}_{2},\dots,\eta_{r},\bar{\eta}_{r}\}.
For brevity, and hopefully, additional clarity, denote

e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta,\bar{\eta}}}}^{*}:=e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta}}}^{*}\wedge\ e_{\Phi_{w^{\bar{\eta}}}}^{*}
, and rewrite (
2.26
) as

(5.29)

e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w}}^{*}\ :=\ e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta_{1},\bar{\eta}_{1}}}}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta_{r},\bar{\eta}_{r}}}}^{*}.

The action of
\gamma
gives:

(5.30)

(1\otimes\gamma)(e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w}}^{*})\ :=\ e_{\Phi_{w^{\gamma\circ\eta_{1},\gamma\circ\bar{\eta}_{1}}}}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{\Phi_{w^{\gamma\circ\eta_{1},\gamma\circ\bar{\eta}_{1}}}}^{*}.

The right hand sides of (
5.29
) and (
5.30
) differ by the signature
\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)
that one seeks to identify.
Each pair of conjugates embeddings
\{\eta_{j},\bar{\eta}_{j}\}
corresponds to a place
v_{j}
of
F
; as before,
\eta_{j}
is called the distinguished embedding–a base point in that pair
of conjugate embeddings. The ordering on
{\rm Hom}(F,\mathbb{C})
fixes an ordering
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{r}\}
on
\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F)
.
Let
\pi_{F}(\gamma)
denote the permutation of
\gamma
on
\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F)
, and
\varepsilon_{F}(\gamma)
its signature. For each
1\leq j\leq r
, let

l_{j}=l(w^{\eta_{j}})
and
l_{j}^{*}=l(w^{\bar{\eta}_{j}})
; then
l_{j}+l_{j}^{*}=nn^{\prime}
since
w
is balanced. The total degree of
e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta,\bar{\eta}}}}^{*}
is
nn^{\prime}
;
interchanging two successive factors of (
5.29
) introduces the signature
(-1)^{(nn^{\prime})^{2}}=(-1)^{nn^{\prime}}.

Finally, let

J_{\gamma}:=\{j\,|\,\mbox{$\gamma\circ\eta_{j}$ is not distinguished.}\}.
Then one has

(5.31)

\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\ =\ \varepsilon_{F}(\gamma)^{nn^{\prime}}\prod_{j\in J_{\gamma}}(-1)^{l_{j}l_{j}^{*}};

since the term
\varepsilon_{F}(\gamma)^{nn^{\prime}}
arises by the permutation of the factors of (
5.29
) to get the factors of (
5.30
); and then within
each such factor
indexed by
j\in J_{\gamma}
the constituent factors in
e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta_{j}}}}^{*}\wedge\ e_{\Phi_{w^{\bar{\eta}_{j}}}}^{*}
get interchanged. Similarly,

(5.32)

\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)\ =\ \varepsilon_{F}(\gamma)^{nn^{\prime}}\prod_{j\in J_{\gamma}}(-1)^{l(w^{\prime\eta_{j}})l(w^{\prime\bar{\eta}_{j}})}.

From Lem.
5.1
it follows that
l(w^{\prime\eta_{j}})=nn^{\prime}-l(w^{\eta_{j}})=l_{j}^{*}
and
l(w^{\prime\bar{\eta}_{j}})=nn^{\prime}-l(w^{\bar{\eta}_{j}})=l_{j}
; hence

(-1)^{l(w^{\prime\eta_{j}})l(w^{\prime\bar{\eta}_{j}})}=(-1)^{l_{j}l_{j}^{*}}
; whence,
\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)=\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma).

Now suppose
F
is a totally imaginary field in the
CM
-case and
F_{1}
its maximal CM subfield. In preparation,
fix orderings on
\Sigma_{F}
,
\Sigma_{F_{1}}
and
\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F_{1})
in a compatible way as follows:

(1)

fix an ordering
\{w_{1},\dots,w_{r_{1}}\}
on
\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F_{1})
;

(2)

then fix the ordering
\{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\dots,\nu_{r_{1}},\bar{\nu}_{1},\bar{\nu}_{2},\dots,\bar{\nu}_{r_{1}}\}
; where the pair of conjugate embeddings
\{\nu_{j},\bar{\nu}_{j}\}
map to
w_{j}
, and recall that we call
\nu_{j}
as the distinguished embedding;

(3)

finally, to fix an ordering on
\Sigma_{F}
, let
\Sigma_{F}(\nu)
denote the fiber over
\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}
under the canonical restriction map
\Sigma_{F}\to\Sigma_{F_{1}}
;
if
\nu<\nu^{\prime}
in
\Sigma_{F_{1}}
then each element in
\Sigma_{F}(\nu)
is less than every element of
\Sigma_{F}(\nu^{\prime})
; and within each fiber
\Sigma_{F}(\nu)
fix any ordering.

The Galois element
\gamma
induces permutations on
\Sigma_{F}
,
\Sigma_{F_{1}}
and
\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F_{1})
giving the commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\Sigma_{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{F}(\gamma)}
\textstyle{\Sigma_{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma_{F_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{F_{1}}(\gamma)}
\textstyle{\Sigma_{F_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1\infty}(\gamma)}
\textstyle{\mathsf{S}_{\infty}(F_{1})}

Define
\hat{\pi}_{F}(\gamma)
to be the permutation of
\Sigma_{F}
that induces
\pi_{F_{1}}(\gamma)
on
\Sigma_{F_{1}}
, and

if
\pi_{F_{1}}(\gamma)(\eta)=\eta^{\prime}
then
\hat{\pi}_{F}(\gamma)
is an order preserving bijection
\Sigma_{F}(\nu)\to\Sigma_{F}(\nu^{\prime})
.

Define the permutation
\pi_{F}^{\prime}(\gamma)
of
\Sigma_{F}
by

(5.33)

\pi_{F}(\gamma)\ =\ \pi_{F}^{\prime}(\gamma)\circ\hat{\pi}_{F}(\gamma).

Observe that
\pi_{F}^{\prime}(\gamma)
induces the identity permutation on
\Sigma_{F_{1}}
, and denote
\pi_{\Sigma_{F}(\nu)}^{\prime}(\gamma)

for the permutation that
\pi_{F}^{\prime}(\gamma)
induces on the fiber
\Sigma_{F}(\nu)
above
\nu.
Let
\varepsilon(*)
denote the signature of a permutation
*
.
The proof of Lem.
5.28
follows from the following two sub-lemmas.

Sublemma 5.34
.

\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\cdot\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)\ =\ \varepsilon(\pi_{F}^{\prime}(\gamma))^{nn^{\prime}}.

Sublemma 5.35
.

\frac{\gamma\left(N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})^{1/2}\right)}{N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})^{1/2}}\ =\ \varepsilon(\pi_{F}^{\prime}(\gamma))

Proof of Sublemma
5.34
.

Define
J_{\gamma}=\{j\ :\ \mbox{$\pi_{F}(\gamma)(\eta_{j})$ is not distinguished}\}
. Keeping in mind that strongly-pure weights such as
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are the base-change
of (strongly-)pure weights from
F_{1}
, we deduce that the constituents
({}^{\iota}\!w^{\eta})_{\eta:F\to\mathbb{C}}

of the Kostant representative
{}^{\iota}\!w
are such that if
\eta|_{F_{1}}=\eta^{\prime}|_{F_{1}},
then
{}^{\iota}\!w^{\eta}
and
{}^{\iota}\!w^{\eta^{\prime}}
are the same element in
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
–the Weyl group of

{\rm GL}_{N}.
For
1\leq j\leq r_{1}
denote

l_{j}=l({}^{\iota}\!w^{\eta_{ji}})
and
l_{j}^{*}=l({}^{\iota}\!w^{\bar{\eta}_{ji}}).

One has
l_{j}+l_{j}^{*}=nn^{\prime}
since
w
is balanced. Also, denote
l_{\nu}=l({}^{\iota}\!w^{\eta})
for any
\eta\in\Sigma_{F}(\nu)
. We claim that

(5.36)

\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\ =\ \varepsilon(\pi_{1\infty}(\gamma))^{(nn^{\prime}k)^{2}}\cdot\prod_{j\in J_{\gamma}}(-1)^{l_{j}l_{j}^{*}k^{2}}\cdot\prod_{\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}}\varepsilon(\pi^{\prime}_{\Sigma_{F}(\nu)}(\gamma))^{l_{\nu}^{2}}.

Recall that the signature
\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)
is determined by the action of
\gamma
on the wedge-product in (
2.26
):

e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w}}^{*}=e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta_{1}}}}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta_{d_{F}}}}}^{*}.
The proof of (
5.36
) boils down to
becoming aware how the factors in this wedge-product are permuted, and what signature is introduced in un-permuting them. The following scheme
depicts from bottom to top, the places of
F_{1}
, embeddings of
F_{1}
, embeddings of
F
, and the lengths of the Kostant representatives they parametrize:

\textstyle{l(w^{\eta_{j1}})=l_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{l(w^{\eta_{jk}})=l_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{l(w^{\bar{\eta}_{j1}})=l_{j}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{l(w^{\bar{\eta}_{jk}})=l_{j}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\eta_{j1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{\eta_{jk}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bar{\eta}_{j1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{\bar{\eta}_{jk}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\nu_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bar{\nu}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{w_{j}}

Group together the wedge-factors as follows:

e_{\Phi_{{}^{\iota}\!w}}^{*}\ =\ e_{\Phi_{[w_{1}]}}^{*}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{\Phi_{[w_{r_{1}}]}}^{*},

where, for each
1\leq j\leq r_{1}
,

e_{\Phi_{[w_{j}]}}^{*}\ =\ e_{\Phi_{[\nu_{j}]}}^{*}\wedge e_{\Phi_{[\bar{\nu}_{j}]}}^{*}

and for each
\nu_{j}\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}
,

e_{\Phi_{[\nu_{j}]}}^{*}=e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta_{j1}}}}^{*}\wedge\dots\wedge e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta_{jk}}}}^{*},\quad e_{\Phi_{[\bar{\nu}_{j}]}}^{*}=e_{\Phi_{w^{\bar{\eta}_{j1}}}}^{*}\wedge\dots\wedge e_{\Phi_{w^{\bar{\eta}_{jk}}}}^{*}.

Recall that
e_{\Phi_{w^{\eta_{ji}}}}^{*}
has degree
l_{j}
and
e_{\Phi_{w^{\bar{\eta}_{ji}}}}^{*}
has degree
l_{j}^{*}.
Hence,

e_{\Phi_{[\nu_{j}]}}^{*}
has degree
kl_{j}
, and
e_{\Phi_{[\bar{\nu}_{j}]}}^{*}
has degree
kl_{j}^{*}.
Therefore,
e_{\Phi_{[w_{j}]}}^{*}
has
degree
kl_{j}+kl_{j}^{*}=knn^{\prime}
. Now, the permutation
\pi_{1\infty}(\gamma)
on
S_{\infty}(F_{1})=\{w_{1},\dots,w_{r_{1}}\}
can be
undone by the signature
\varepsilon(\pi_{1\infty}(\gamma))^{(nn^{\prime}k)^{2}}.
Next, only for those
j\in J_{\gamma}
,
the two factors in
e_{\Phi_{[\nu_{j}]}}^{*}\wedge e_{\Phi_{[\bar{\nu}_{j}]}}^{*}
get interchanged, giving the signature
(-1)^{l_{j}l_{j}^{*}k^{2}}
.
Finally, adjusting for the action of
\gamma
on
\Sigma_{F_{1}}
, i.e., now working with
\pi^{\prime}_{F}(\gamma)
, which only permutes internally
within each fiber
\Sigma_{F}(\nu)
over
\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}
, one sees the signature
\varepsilon(\pi^{\prime}_{\Sigma_{F}(\nu)}(\gamma))^{l_{\nu}^{2}}

for each such
\nu.
This proves the claim (
5.36
).

For any integer
a
, since
a^{2}\equiv a\pmod{2}
, (
5.36
) simplifies to

(5.37)

\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\ =\ \varepsilon(\pi_{1\infty}(\gamma))^{nn^{\prime}k}\cdot\prod_{j\in J_{\gamma}}(-1)^{l_{j}l_{j}^{*}k}\cdot\prod_{\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}}\varepsilon(\pi^{\prime}_{\Sigma_{F}(\nu)}(\gamma))^{l_{\nu}}.

Similarly, using the relation of
w^{\prime}
with
w
, one has:

(5.38)

\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)\ =\ \varepsilon(\pi_{1\infty}(\gamma))^{nn^{\prime}k}\cdot\prod_{j\in J_{\gamma}}(-1)^{l_{j}^{*}l_{j}k^{2}}\cdot\prod_{\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}}\varepsilon(\pi^{\prime}_{\Sigma_{F}(\nu)}(\gamma))^{l_{\nu}^{*}}.

Multiply (
5.37
) and (
5.38
) to get:

\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\cdot\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma)\ =\ \prod_{\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}}\varepsilon(\pi^{\prime}_{\Sigma_{F}(\nu)}(\gamma))^{l_{\nu}+l_{\nu}^{*}}\ =\ \left(\prod_{\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}}\varepsilon(\pi^{\prime}_{\Sigma_{F}(\nu)}(\gamma))\right)^{nn^{\prime}}\ =\ \varepsilon(\pi_{F}^{\prime}(\gamma))^{nn^{\prime}}.

∎

Proof of Sublemma
5.35
.

For
x\in F_{1}^{\times}
one has
N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(x)=\prod_{\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}}\nu(x)>0.
Let
\{\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{k}\}
denote the set of
all embeddings of
F
into
\bar{F_{1}}
over
F_{1}
, for some algebraic closure
\bar{F_{1}}
of
F_{1}
;
let
\{\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{k}\}
is an
F_{1}
-basis for
F
; then
\delta_{F/F_{1}}=\det[\rho_{i}(\omega_{j})]^{2}.
Hence,

N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})\ =\ \prod_{\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}}\nu(\det[\rho_{i}(\omega_{j})]^{2})\ =\ \prod_{\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}}\det[\rho_{i}^{\nu}(\omega_{j})]^{2},

where
\{\rho_{1}^{\nu},\dots,\rho_{k}^{\nu}\}
is the set of all embeddings of
F
into
\mathbb{C}
that restrict to
\nu:F_{1}\to\mathbb{C}.
(We may take
\rho_{i}^{\nu}
to be
\tilde{\nu}\circ\rho_{i}
for any
extension
\tilde{\nu}:\bar{F_{1}}\to\mathbb{C}
of
\nu.
Whence,

(5.39)

N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})^{1/2}\ =\ \pm\det\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}[\rho_{i}^{\nu_{1}}(\omega_{j})]&&&\\
&[\rho_{i}^{\nu_{2}}(\omega_{j})]&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&[\rho_{i}^{\nu_{d_{1}}}(\omega_{j})]\end{array}\right],

where the appropriate sign
\pm
is chosen to make the right hand side positive. Each block
[\rho_{i}^{\nu}(\omega_{j})]
is a
k\times k
-block.
Apply
\gamma
to (
5.39
) and the change in the sign of the determinant on the right is
the requisite sign
\gamma\left(N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})^{1/2}\right)/N_{F_{1}/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta_{F/F_{1}})^{1/2}.
The blocks are permuted according to
\pi_{F_{1}}(\gamma)
which
does not change the sign. Hence, the signature is accounted for by assuming that the blocks remain where they are and looking at how each block’s rows are permuted internally;
in other words, keeping (
5.33
) in mind, the requisite signature is

\prod_{\nu\in\Sigma_{F_{1}}}\varepsilon(\pi^{\prime}_{\Sigma_{F}(\nu)}(\gamma))\ =\ \varepsilon(\pi_{F}^{\prime}(\gamma)).

∎

This concludes the proof of Lem.
5.28
.
∎

This concludes the proof of Prop.
5.26
, proving compatbility of our main theorem with Deligne’s conjecture.

5.5.
An example

If we take
n=n^{\prime}=1
then the main result and techniques are all due to Harder
[
22
]
. However, the signature

\varepsilon_{\iota,w}(\gamma)\cdot\varepsilon_{\iota,w^{\prime}}(\gamma),
that can be nontrivial in general, is missing in
[
22
]
. Furthermore, the subtle distinction between when
F

is in the
CM
-case or in the
TR
-case is not seen
[
22
]
and it becomes apparent only in the larger context of this article.
This case
n=n^{\prime}=1
is also extensively
discussed in
[
40
]
wherein examples are constructed to show the non-triviality of these signatures. As an alternative, it is worth the effort to illustrate the content of the main theorem in the
simplest non-trivial example: when
n=n^{\prime}=1
and
F
is an imaginary quadratic field, not so much by appealing to Harder
[
22
]
, or this article,
but rather via recourse to modular forms of CM type. Here,
\sigma
and

\sigma^{\prime}
are both algebraic Hecke characters and main theorem concerns the ratios of successive critical values of the
L
-function attached to
the algebraic Hecke character:
\chi=\sigma\sigma^{\prime-1}.
After relabelling, take
\sigma=\chi
an algebraic Hecke character,
and for
\sigma^{\prime}
take the trivial character. This
{\rm GL}(1)
-example is instructive, and was helpful to the author to see some finer details.

For an imaginary quadratic field
F,
let
{\rm Hom}(F,\mathbb{C})=\{\eta,\bar{\eta}\}
; the choice of
\eta
is not canonical; it induces an isomorphism

\eta:F_{\infty}\simeq\mathbb{C}.
Let
\chi:F^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be an algebraic Hecke character; this means that
\chi
is a continuous homomorphism whose infinite component
\chi_{\infty}:F_{\infty}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is of the form
\chi_{\infty}(z)=z^{p}\bar{z}^{q},
for integers
p
and
q
. Then

\chi\in{\rm Coh}({\rm GL}_{1}/F,\mu)
with
\mu=(\mu^{\eta},\mu^{\bar{\eta}})
and
\mu^{\eta}=-p
and
\mu^{\bar{\eta}}=-q.
The weight

\mu
is strongly-pure with purity weight
{\bf w}=-p-q.
One also has

\chi_{\infty}(z)=\left(\frac{z}{\bar{z}}\right)^{\ell/2}(z\bar{z})^{-{\bf w}/2},\quad\ell=p-q\in\mathbb{Z}.

As recalled in (
3.3
), the
\Gamma
-factors at infinity (up to nonzero constants and exponentials) on either side of the functional equation are:

L_{\infty}(s,\chi)\sim\Gamma(s-\tfrac{{\bf w}}{2}+\tfrac{|\ell|}{2}),\quad L_{\infty}(1-s,\chi^{-1})\sim\Gamma(1-s+\tfrac{{\bf w}}{2}+\tfrac{|\ell|}{2}).

Assume, without any loss of generality (if necessary, replacing
\chi
by
\chi^{-1}
), that
\ell\geq 0,
i.e.,
p\geq q.
Then

L_{\infty}(s,\chi)\sim\Gamma(s+p)
and
L_{\infty}(1-s,\chi^{-1})\sim\Gamma(1-s-q).

The critical set for
L(s,\chi)
is the set of
\ell
consecutive integers:

\{1-p,\,2-p,\dots,\,-q\}.

The critical set is nonempty if
\ell\geq 1
, and we have
\ell
many critical points and
\ell-1
pairs of successive critical points. The cuspidal width
\ell(\mu,0)
between
\mu
and the weight
\mu^{\prime}=0
is

\ell(\mu,0)=\ell.
If we were to apply the main theorem to the pair
\chi
and the trivial Hecke character (which is cohomological with respect to
\mu^{\prime}=0
) then the combinatorial lemma imposes the condition
\ell\geq 2,
and Thm.
5.16
gives a rationality result for the ratios
L(m,\chi)/L(m+1,\chi)
of all successive critical values. This theorem can also be seen independently by appealing to the rationality results of Shimura for
L
-functions of modular forms.

Take
\pi=\pi(\chi)=\text{AI}_{F}^{\mathbb{Q}}(\chi)
to be the automorphic induction of
\chi
from
F
to
\mathbb{Q}
. Then
\pi
is a cuspidal automorphic representation of

{\rm GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}).
The representation
\pi_{\infty}
at the infinite place is, by definition,
\text{AI}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathbb{R}}(\chi_{\infty}),
which in turn is defined by asking for its Langlands parameter to be the induced representation

{\rm Ind}_{W_{\mathbb{C}}}^{W_{\mathbb{R}}}(\chi_{\infty})={\rm Ind}_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}^{W_{\mathbb{R}}}(z\mapsto\left(\frac{z}{\bar{z}}\right)^{\ell/2})\otimes|\ |_{\mathbb{R}}^{-{\bf w}/2}.
This is exactly the representation that has cohomology with respect to the irreducible representation of
{\rm GL}(2)
with highest weight
\lambda=(p,q).
By the standard dictionary between modular forms and automorphic representations (see, for example,
Gelbart
[
13
]
) there is a primitive modular form
f_{\chi}
of weight
k=p-q+1
such that
\pi(\chi)=\pi(f_{\chi})\otimes|\ |^{-{\bf w}/2}.
One of the properties of this dictionary gives us the following equality of
L
-functions:

L(s,f_{\chi})\ =\ L(s-\tfrac{(k-1)}{2},\pi(f_{\chi}))\ =\ L(s-\tfrac{(k-1)}{2}+\tfrac{{\bf w}}{2},\pi(\chi))\ =\ L(s-p,\chi).

The critical set for
L(s,f_{\chi})
is the string of integers
\{1,2,\dots,k-1\}.
A word about the normalizations of these
L
-functions: first of all
L(s,f_{\chi})
is
the Hecke
L
-function of the modular form
f_{\chi}
which has a functional equation with respect to
s\leftrightarrow k-s
. For a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
, as applied to
\pi(\chi)
or to
\pi(f_{\chi})
, the functional equation is with respect to
s\leftrightarrow 1-s
. The
L
-function
L(s,\chi)

also has a functional equation with respect to
s\leftrightarrow 1-s
. Furthermore, for any Dirichlet character
\omega
, by which we mean a character

\omega:\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of finite-order, there is the equality:

L(s,f_{\chi},\omega)\ =\ L(s-p,\chi\otimes\omega^{F}),

where
\omega^{F}:=\omega\circ N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}
is the base-change of
\omega
from
\mathbb{Q}
to
F
. In particular, if
\omega=\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}}
the
quadratic Dirichlet character of
\mathbb{Q}
attached to
F
by class field theory then:

L(s,f_{\chi},\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}})\ =\ L(s,f_{\chi}),

since the base-change of
\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}}
back to
F
is the trivial character. This is also seen at the level of representations since

\pi(\chi)\simeq\pi(\chi)\otimes\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}}.

From Shimura
[
46
]
applied to
f_{\chi}
, there exists two periods
u^{\pm}(f_{\chi})\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, such that for any critical integer

r\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, and any primitive Dirichlet character
\psi
, one has

L_{f}(r,f_{\chi},\psi)\ \approx\ (2\bm{\uppi}\mathfrak{i})^{r}u^{\pm}(f_{\chi})\mathfrak{g}(\psi),

where

\mathfrak{g}(\psi)
is the Gauß sum of
\psi
, and the choice of periods is dictated by the parities of
r
and
\psi
via:
\psi(-1)=\pm(-1)^{r}
; and

\approx
is a simplified notation to mean that the ratio of the left hand side divided by everything on the right hand side is algebraic, and is
{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
-equivariant:

\gamma\left(\frac{L_{f}(r,f_{\chi},\psi)}{(2\bm{\uppi}\mathfrak{i})^{r}u^{\pm}(f_{\chi})\mathfrak{g}(\psi)}\right)\ =\ \frac{L_{f}(r,{}^{\gamma}f_{\chi},{}^{\gamma}\psi)}{(2\bm{\uppi}\mathfrak{i})^{r}u^{\pm}({}^{\gamma}f_{\chi})\mathfrak{g}({}^{\gamma}\psi)},\quad\forall\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}).

The finite part of the
L
-function
L_{f}(r,f_{\chi},\psi)

is completed using the archimedean
\Gamma
-factor

L_{\infty}(s,f_{\chi},\psi)=2(2\bm{\uppi})^{-s}\Gamma(s)
. In terms of the completed
L
-function the above relation takes the form:

L(r,f_{\chi},\psi)\ \approx\ \mathfrak{i}^{r}u^{\pm}(f_{\chi})\mathfrak{g}(\psi).

Take
r=1
and use the above relation once for
\psi
the trivial character and then for
\psi=\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}}
to deduce:

u^{+}(f_{\chi})\ \approx\ u^{-}(f_{\chi})\,\mathfrak{g}(\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}}).

Next, apply Shimura’s result to
L(s,f_{\chi})
for
s=r
and
s=r+1
, where
r\in\{1,\dots,k-2\}
(possible when
k\geq 3
, i.e.,
\ell\geq 2
), and
divide one by the other to deduce:

\frac{L(r,f_{\chi})}{L(r+1,f_{\chi})}\ \approx\ \mathfrak{i}\,\mathfrak{g}(\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}}),

while using
\mathfrak{i}^{2}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
and
\mathfrak{g}(\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}})^{2}\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}.
Since
L(s,f_{\chi})=L(s-p,\chi),
and putting
r-p=m,
one gets for the
ratio of two successive critical values of the completed
L
-function of
\chi
the rationality result:

\mathfrak{i}\,\mathfrak{g}(\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}}L(m,\chi)/L(m+1,\chi)\approx)\in\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
and furthermore,

\gamma\left(\mathfrak{i}\,\mathfrak{g}(\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}})\frac{L(m,\chi)}{L(m+1,\chi)}\right)\ =\ \mathfrak{i}\,\mathfrak{g}(\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}})\frac{L(m,{}^{\gamma}\chi)}{L(m+1,{}^{\gamma}\chi)},\quad\forall\gamma\in{\rm Gal}(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}).

One has used that
{}^{\gamma}f_{\chi}=f_{{}^{\gamma}\chi}
which follows from the definition of
f_{\chi}
(see
[
45
, Sect. 5]
).
To see that the above result is indeed an instance of Thm.
5.16
, one needs the basic fact about quadratic Gauss sums:

\mathfrak{i}\,\mathfrak{g}(\omega_{F/\mathbb{Q}})=|\delta_{F/\mathbb{Q}}|^{1/2}\pmod{\mathbb{Q}^{\times}}
. It is shown in
[
39
]
that this example generalizes from
{\rm GL}(1)

over an imaginary quadratic extension to
{\rm GL}(n)
over a CM field.
